Unraveling the Bott spiral
Arun Debray, Cameron Krulewski, Luuk Stehouwer

Abstract
. We construct and compute a homotopy-theoretic model for the Bott spiral of symmetry-protected topological phases (SPTs) studied by Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern. We model free and interacting fermionic SPTs using K K -theory and reflection-positive invertible field theories (IFTs), resp., and define a twisted generalization of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation to produce a free-to-interacting map . We also define and compute spiral maps of IFTs to model dimensional reduction in this context, answering a question of Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren. Our analysis highlights two general aspects of homotopical free-to-interacting maps. First, IFTs are more sensitive than K K -theory is to the input symmetry data; in particular, the specification of an Altland–Zirnbauer class is insufficient information to define symmetry type for an IFT. Second, the remnant of Bott periodicity on the interacting side relies on an isomorphism of two extraspecial groups of order 32 32 . Our computations use a novel 4 4 -periodic description of a sector of the twisted 𝑘𝑜 \mathit{ko} -homology of elementary abelian 2 2 -groups.



Introduction

Background and motivation

For the last half-century, what have come to be called
symmetry protected topological phases
(SPTs) have inspired a fruitful interplay between algebraic topology and quantum physics, in which topological invariants make physical predictions, while physical desiderata and computations also inspire new mathematics.
SPTs are gapped quantum phases of matter whose stability relies on the presence of a symmetry—on the physics side, often encoded as a group action on a lattice Hamiltonian—and which are invertible under a stacking operation, meaning that the set of SPTs with fixed dimension and
symmetry type
1
1

1

We will define symmetry types precisely later in the introduction.
—the collection of symmetries acting on the system—forms a group.

Free fermion
SPTs in particular have received a great deal of interest due to the property that their energy spectrum is exactly solvable, making them convenient models, and due to their applicability to the theory of topological insulators and superconductors, which include experimentally-realized phases like the quantum Hall insulator
[
324
]
and quantum spin Hall insulator
[
205
]
.
Free fermion models are so-called because their Hamiltonians feature no interactions between particles, and this property ultimately allows the ground states of such models to be described by Clifford modules and for the phases to be classified by
K
-theory
[
197
,
272
]
.
Our preferred mathematical phrasing of this classification will be briefly recalled in
Section

2.1
; specifically, see
2.3
.

However, as first discovered by Fidkowski–Kitaev in 2009, free fermion models are in general
not
sufficient for describing interacting systems
[
111
]
.
They found that eight stacked copies of the time-reversal symmetric Majorana chain, which defines a nontrivial phase within free fermion models, becomes trivializable when considered in a
space of interacting gapped Hamiltonian models with unique ground states. This indicates that when comparing a moduli space of free fermion
Hamiltonians with a corresponding space of interacting Hamiltonians, the number of connected components can change. There are also SPTs, called
intrinsically interacting
, that are not adiabatically connected to free fermion models.
Since it is often a reasonable physical assumption that fermionic systems do have interactions, it has become a major open question in condensed matter to determine when and how the free and interacting classifications differ.
A special case of this question is the main topic of this paper, and rather than physical, our methods will be homotopy-theoretic.

On the free side, the application of homotopy theory (specifically,
K
-theory) toward classifying
free fermion SPTs is a well-known and highly general story
[
197
,
272
,
314
,
121
,
122
,
139
,
295
]
.
On the interacting side, the application of homotopy theory is more recent.
Mathematical models for interacting fermionic systems are less developed, as these systems are more subtle primarily because
there is at present no agreed-upon definition of a moduli space of interacting lattice Hamiltonians.
However, it is believed that any such moduli space should deformation retract onto the space of ground states of the theories, meaning that deformation classes of interacting Hamiltonians should be determined by properties of their ground states, which are governed by the low-energy field theory of the SPT
[
198
,
199
]
.
According to an ansatz (see
2.11
) of Freed–Hopkins (see
[
118
]
,
[
128
, Section 9.1]
), which builds on work of Kapustin–Thorngren–Turzillo–Wang
[
185
]
, the low-energy theory of an SPT should be modeled as an
invertible field theory
(IFT); specifically, a reflection-positive fully extended invertible field theory on manifolds with (in the fermionic case) twisted spin structures.
This version of invertible field theory is a mathematically well-defined object, and by results of Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Remark 8.39]
and Grady
[
142
]
, such theories (up to deformation) admit a classification in terms of
Anderson-dual bordism groups
.
Standard homotopical methods such as the Adams spectral sequence have been used to compute these groups
[
118
,
64
,
49
,
89
]
, finding agreement with physical computations. We recall more details on the invertible field theory model for interacting SPTs in
Section

2.2
, and state their classification in
2.19
.

Formalizing the process of flowing to the low-energy limit is an area of active and exciting research
[
132
,
184
,
251
,
207
]
, but in this paper, due to the success of
2.11
in prior work, we will simply assume it and observe what it predicts computationally in new settings. These computations provide further support to the ansatz.

Symmetry types and the tenfold way

In any problem motivated by physics, one of the first things to understand is the collection of symmetries of the system. This turned out to be an important but subtle point in our analysis of the Bott spiral, and in free-to-interacting maps in general, so here we review the usual classification of symmetries of free fermion systems in the
tenfold way
.

A free fermion Hamiltonian may have time-reversal and an internal symmetry group
K
equal to
\mathrm{O}_{1}
,
\mathrm{U}_{1}
, or
\mathrm{Sp}_{1}
.
Kitaev
[
197
]
showed that these symmetries can combine in ten ways, called
Altland–Zirnbauer classes

[
15
]
, labeled by a subset of Cartan’s labels
[
65
]
for symmetric spaces: A, AI, AII, AIII, BDI, C, CI, CII, D, and DIII.
2
2

2

See
[
97
,
15
,
160
,
272
,
121
,
328
,
193
,
9
,
216
,
76
,
118
,
134
,
280
,
295
,
248
]
for related but distinct approaches to this “tenfold way” classification, as well as
[
348
,
244
,
69
,
33
]
for reviews.
These classes correspond to the eight real and two complex Morita classes of Clifford algebras
[
197
,
3
]
. Moreover, Kitaev showed that the classifications of free fermion phases in these classes are periodic, matching the real
K
-theory groups
\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
,
0
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
0
,
0
,
0
,
\mathbb{Z}
for the eight real cases.
Phases in the two remaining classes, A and AIII, are classified by the two shifts of the complex
K
-theory groups. Thus Bott periodicity in
K
-theory appears twice in the classification of free fermion phases:
first, in the periodicity in spatial dimension for a fixed symmetry class,
and second, more fundamentally, in the classification of the possible symmetries itself.
3
3

3

In the presence of additional symmetries, such as spatial reflection symmetry, the classification can get more complicated. Following Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
, we will sometimes consider one of the ten Altland–Zirnbauer symmetry classes together with a spatial reflection symmetry
\mathcal{R}
squaring to fermion parity, acting trivially on the
\mathrm{U}_{1}
charge, and commuting with time-reversal symmetry (if present). We will denote this modification with a prime, e.g. classes
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
,
\mathrm{A^{\prime}}
, etc. See
Section

4.1.1
for more information and Song–Schnyder
[
286
, §II.A.1]
for a discussion of more general Altland–Zirnbauer classes incorporating a spatial reflection.

Passing to interacting SPT phases, then to IFTs, we would like to describe the symmetries of the system as a group
G
that generates the Clifford algebra for a given Altland–Zirnbauer class in an appropriate sense. Complicating matters, however, classifications of IFTs lack Bott periodicity in
both
of the above senses: the groups of IFTs in dimensions
d
and
d+8
are generally nonisomorphic, and independently, there are ambiguities in choosing the symmetry group corresponding to a given Altland–Zirnbauer class, preventing a clean eight- or two-periodic description.

More specifically, consider the collection
E_{\ell,k}
of
\ell
time-reversing symmetries with square
1
and
k
time-reversing symmetries with square
-1
, all required to anticommute.
In the context of free phases, this infinite collection of symmetry groups reduces to eight symmetry classes only dependent on
\ell-k
modulo
8
.
The underlying mathematical reason is that
E_{\ell,k}
reproduces a Clifford algebra
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
(Corollary
1.67
), which in
\mathit{KO}
-theory gives nothing but a shift in dimension by
\ell-k
.
Bott periodicity does not persist for interacting phases, the only exception being that
E_{\ell,k+4}
and
E_{\ell+4,k}
do still agree (
2.31
), the implications of which we will explain later.

Another important subtlety we have to circumvent regarding symmetry classes is as follows: the pin group
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
also generates the Clifford algebra
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
under addition and multiplication. Freed–Hopkins’ choices of symmetry groups representing the ten Altland–Zirnbauer classes
[
118
, §9.2.1, Tables (9.34), (9.35)]
include six pin groups with
\ell
and
k
small.

E_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
represent the same Altland–Zirnbauer class, yet in general give rise to different classifications of interacting SPTs, as we show in
2.27
. This is an instance of a general phenomenon also discussed in
[
286
,
293
]
.

Therefore, to formulate free-to-interacting maps in examples of interest in physics, we have to confront this ambiguity and determine how to match the symmetry data on the free and interacting sides.

The Bott spiral

Our primary goal in this paper is to compare free and interacting fermionic SPTs in the special case of the
Bott spiral
4
4

4

The term “Bott spiral” is due to Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §5]
.
of fermionic SPTs studied by Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
.
The class of SPTs that they study exhibits drastically different behavior depending on whether interactions are allowed: while the free classification is insensitive to certain changes in dimension and symmetry type, the interacting classification depends strongly on these parameters and specifically experiences an exponential growth in size as dimension increases.

Figure 1
.
The Bott spiral starting with 1d class
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
: our mathematical model for
[
263
, Figure 1(a)]
,
which displays the groups of interacting SPTs admitting free fermion realizations in a specified symmetry class and indicates dimensional reduction maps between them.
Our model for an interacting SPT class is a deformation class of invertible field theory (IFT; see
2.11
), and our models for dimensional reduction are
the spiral maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
defined in
4.51
and calculated in
6.16
and
6.24
.
In each entry of the spiral,
\mathbb{Z}/2^{N}
is the group of IFTs
in the image of a map from
\mathit{KO}
-theory,
calculated in
5.55
and
6.15
.
The bracket below the group denotes the spatial dimension and discrete Altland–Zirnbauer class of this group of IFTs: see
Table

1
.
This symmetry class is represented by the fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
, where the bracket above an entry gives
(\ell,k)
: see
1.36
. The identification
(0,4)=(4,0)
in class
\mathrm{C^{\prime}}
is
2.31
.
We see that every other step in the spiral, the relevant classification order increases by a factor of two, resulting in a 16-fold increase every eight steps around: the remnant of Bott periodicity for interacting theories.
On the outside, we compare with the Bott clock: the twisted group algebras of the eight symmetry groups we use recover all eight Morita classes of super division algebras;
\simeq
indicates Morita equivalence.

Figure 2
.
The Bott spiral starting with 1d class
\mathrm{CII}
: our mathematical model for
[
263
, Figure 1(b)]
.
All notation is as in
Figure

1
.

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s paradigm is very close to the tenfold way discussed above.
Indeed, on the free side, the symmetry groups
E_{\ell,k}
for a fixed
\ell-k\bmod 8
model a certain Altland–Zirnbauer class.
However, on the interacting side,
SPTs with symmetry types
E_{\ell,k}
and
E_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
in the same Altland–Zirnbauer class (i.e. with
\ell-k\equiv\ell^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\bmod 8
) can be measurably different, as we will discuss.

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern connect different SPT-phases in
the spiral using a version of
dimensional reduction
5
5

5

See
0.13
.
similar to that of e.g. Ryu–Schnyder–Furusaki–Ludwig
[
272
]
,
noting that as the dimension of the system increases, the classification of free fermion SPTs under study repeats every eight (resp. two) steps due to real (resp. complex) Bott periodicity.
However, they physically derive that the classification of interacting SPTs connected to these free models
often doubles in size
as the dimension grows,
leading to e.g. a 16-fold increase in the number of distinct interacting SPTs every eight steps in the real spirals
[
263
, (1)]
.

As we mentioned above, Freed–Hopkins’ symmetry types modeling the tenfold way
[
118
, Tables (9.34), (9.35)]
do
not
suffice for describing the Bott spiral: there is a mismatch between their groups of IFTs and what Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern report as the orders of the interacting SPTs in the Bott spiral. See e.g.
2.27
for a relevant comparison.

Goal 0.1
.

Determine the symmetry types for the free and interacting SPTs in the Bott spiral.

Developing and computing a model for the interacting SPTs in the Bott spiral, to complement the
K
-theory model for the corresponding free SPTs, is only part of the story.
To describe the relationship between free and interacting SPTs, we not only wish to compute both classifications, but actually produce a map from one to the other.
Generalizing work of Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9, 10]
, our approach is to define a homotopical map from certain
K
-theory groups—which model the free fermion SPTs in the Bott spiral—to corresponding groups of (deformation classes of) IFTs—modeling the low-energy field theories of the interacting SPTs in the spiral. We call such a map a
free-to-interacting map
.
For each symmetry type of SPT in the Bott spiral, we actually define a map of spectra, whose homotopy in degree
-d
yields the free-to-interacting map for groups of SPTs in spatial dimension
d
.
6
6

6

Heuristically, one can think of this map as arising from an index construction for families of Dirac operators. In this paper, however, we do not use such a description. Instead, we construct the map as a homotopical refinement of the
spin-orientation of
\mathit{KO}
, suitably twisted by the symmetry data.

Free-to-interacting maps encode a great deal of information:
we interpret their kernels as consisting of the free phases that are unstable to interactions, while we interpret their cokernels as the intrinsically interacting phases.
Given a free fermion Hamiltonian, if we can compute its class in
K
-theory, the free-to-interacting map also predicts exactly how many copies of this Hamiltonian must be stacked in order to reach a trivializable phase; if the map on groups is a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/m
, for example, then we expect that
m
stacked copies of the input Hamiltonian is adiabatically connected to the trivial phase.
Therefore, once such a map is defined, we may compute it in low dimensions and answer (assuming
2.11
) a large number of physical questions.

To study the phenomenon of the Bott spiral, it suffices to define free-to-interacting maps and compute their
images
, since the interacting SPTs in the Bott spiral that exhibit the 16-fold periodicity are all connected to free fermion models.
In other words, since the intrinsically interacting SPTs of the relevant symmetry types do not play a role in the behavior we wish to study, we will ignore the cokernel of the free-to-interacting maps in this paper.

Goal 0.2
.

Define and compute a map from
K
-theory to IFTs whose image in the relevant degrees is the group
\mathbb{Z}/2^{a}
computed in
[
263
, (1)]
.

A third goal in this paper, related to the previous two, is to use IFTs to model not only the interacting SPTs, but also the dimensional reduction process.
This operation takes a SPT in spatial dimension
d
to a
d+1
-dimensional SPT with a modified symmetry type. The following goal was raised as a question by Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §5]
.

Goal 0.3
.

Define and compute maps of IFTs that
model the dimensional reduction procedure of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern.

Our inspiration for this goal comes from the success of dualized
Smith homomorphisms
of IFTs toward computing the behavior of SPTs under symmetry defect creation, which has been studied in e.g.
[
155
,
75
,
203
,
96
,
74
,
79
,
181
,
232
]
.
Specifically, Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
]
suggested a relationship between the growing factors of two in Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s real classification and a certain 4-periodic sequence of Smith homomorphisms (see
3.25
and
3.28
).
We find that Smith homomorphisms are not the entire story, but they are a central ingredient in our constructions and aid in our computations.
In fact, we confirm that the periodicity of this family of Smith homomorphisms underlies the 16-fold periodicity of the interacting SPTs in the Bott spiral, as we note in
5.46
.
We call the maps of IFTs we produce
spiral maps
.

We provide precise definitions of the free-to-interacting maps and spiral maps in the next subsection.

Main results

The first challenge in
defining free-to-interacting maps in general is in efficiently encoding the symmetries of the SPTs of interest.
In our chosen framework, we can specify the symmetries of a free fermion system by providing a superalgebra.
On the interacting side, symmetries are encoded by requiring certain tangential structures on the manifolds evaluated by the IFT.
Specifically, for fermionic theories, we will specify twisted spin structures, such as pin structures.

Our approach to fermionic symmetries is to use
fermionic groups
(
1.1
), as first introduced by Benson
[
52
, §7]
. These are
triples
(G_{f},(-1)^{F},\theta)
of a compact Lie group
G_{f}
, a central element
(-1)^{F}
of order
2
, and a homomorphism
\theta\colon G_{f}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
called the
grading
, such that
\theta((-1)^{F})=0
.
Physically, the element
(-1)^{F}
encodes
fermion parity
, while the grading
\theta
distinguishes unitary and antiunitary symmetries. We model the symmetry type of an interacting SPT as a fermionic group.

Fermionic groups come with two useful constructions.

(1)

The fermionic group algebra
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
(
[
52
, §7]
,
Section

1.3
) is defined so that modules over it are those
G_{f}
-representations that are relevant for physical systems with
G_{f}
-symmetry. They may therefore be used as an input to the free fermion classification using the
K
-theory of superalgebras, see
Section

1.4
.

(2)

As we discuss in
Section

1.5
, Stolz
[
300
, §2.6]
associates to any fermionic group
G_{f}
a topological group
H(G_{f})
with a map
H(G_{f})\to\mathrm{O}
, the infinite orthogonal group, generalizing e.g. the assignment
\mathrm{Spin}_{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{Spin}_{2})\cong\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
. Taking classifying spaces, we obtain a stable tangential structure
BH(G_{f})\to B\mathrm{O}
in the sense of Lashof
[
209
]
, and can therefore discuss
H(G_{f})
-structures on manifolds. This models the topological structure on spacetime in a field theory of fermions with a
G_{f}
-symmetry: see
[
293
, §2.1]
. We thus use IFTs of
H(G_{f})
-manifolds to mathematically model interacting fermionic SPTs with
G_{f}
-symmetry.

In
1.64
and
1.108
, we show these constructions are compatible with products
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}K_{f}
of fermionic groups (see
1.7
).
This compatibility allows our formalism to use the same input symmetry data for both the free SPTs (classified by
K
-theory) and interacting SPTs (classified by groups of (deformation classes of) IFTs).

With these
K
-theoretic, resp. field-theoretic models for the free and interacting classifications of SPTs, we now discuss our mathematical models of free-to-interacting maps.

The tangential structure
H(G_{f})
gives rise to a stable Madsen–Tillmann spectrum
\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})
(see, e.g.
[
118
, §7.1]
,
[
49
, §2.9]
), which is a tangential version the Thom spectrum of
H(G_{f})
.
The homotopy groups of
\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})
are bordism groups of manifolds with (tangential)
H(G_{f})
structures.
We will write
\mathit{KO}
and
\mathit{KU}
for the real and complex
K
-theory spectra, resp.

The main construction involved in a free-to-interacting map is a

generalized Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro (ABS) map
: an analog of the spin orientation
\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{r}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\to\mathit{KO}

[
29
]
of
\mathit{KO}
-theory (resp. the spin
c
orientation
\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{c}
of
\mathit{KU}
), but for manifolds with a given notion of twisted spin structure.
Informed by
the approach of Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9,10]
, we define a generalized ABS map for spin manifolds twisted by a pair of bundles.

3.54
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be compact Lie groups. Let
V_{1}\to BG_{1}
and
V_{2}\to BG_{2}
be vector bundles of rank
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, resp.
A
(V_{1},V_{2})
-
twisted real ABS map
is the composition

(0.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}(BG_{2})_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}\mathit{KO}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{r_{2}-r_{1}}\mathit{KO}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{r}\wedge\lambda_{V_{1}}\wedge e_{V_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}^{\wedge r_{1}+1}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}\underline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Above,
\lambda_{V_{1}}
(
3.34
) is a
\mathit{KO}
-class formed from the difference bundle construction applied to
V_{1}
, while
e_{V_{2}}
(
Equation

3.20
) is the map of spectra induced by the zero section map of
V_{2}
.
On homotopy in degree
n
, this map assigns a class in
\mathit{KO}^{-n+r_{1}-r_{2}}
to an input twisted spin
n
-manifold
M
; specifically,
M
is equipped with maps
f_{i}\colon M\to BG_{i}
and a spin structure on the bundle
TM+f_{1}^{*}V_{1}-f_{2}^{*}V_{2}
.

Given such a twisted ABS map, we obtain a free-to-interacting map by taking the
Anderson dual

I_{\mathbb{Z}}

[
20
]
, as we describe in
Sections

3.5
and
3.98
.
The Anderson dual of the sphere implements a general form of the universal coefficient theorem, and when applied to an ABS map, it produces a map from dualized
\mathit{KO}
-theory to dualized spin bordism.
By
[
20
, Theorem
4.16]
(see
3.76
),

\mathit{KO}
-theory is Anderson-self dual up to a fourfold shift, meaning that the source of our map is
\mathit{KO}
-theory.
Meanwhile, Anderson-dual bordism groups, in the target, are exactly what classify deformation classes of our IFTs of interest:

2.19

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Theorem 1.1]
, Grady
[
142
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group.
There is an isomorphism, natural in
G_{f}
, from the abelian group of deformation classes of reflection-positive IFTs on spacetime manifolds with
H(G_{f})
-structure of dimension
n=d+1
to
[\mathit{MTH}(G_{f}),\Sigma^{n+1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
.

Now we can revisit
0.1
,
0.2
, and
0.3
:

(1)

Determine fermionic groups
G_{f}(d)
, depending on the dimension
d
, describing the symmetries of the Bott spiral such that the groups of IFTs of
d
-dimensional
H(G_{f}(d))
-manifolds match Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s classifications of interacting SPTs.

(2)

Define a twisted ABS map of spectra whose Anderson dual models the free-to-interacting map for the phases in the Bott spiral.

(3)

Define maps between the spectra classifying IFTs for different steps of the Bott spiral, modeling Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s dimensional reduction maps.

We begin by answering (
1
). Within the formalism of fermionic groups, the mathematical definition of the symmetry type
E_{\ell,k}
mentioned earlier in the context of the tenfold way is:

1.36
.

The fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
is the pullback of the diagonal subgroup
\{\pm 1\}^{\ell+k}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
under the double cover
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
.
7
7

7

\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
is defined to be the pin group, in the sense of Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro
[
29
, Theorem 3.11]
, associated to the quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{\ell+k}
given by
x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\dotsb+x_{\ell}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}-\dotsb-y_{k}^{2}
. Thus, for example,
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,0}=\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell}^{+}
and
\mathrm{Pin}_{0,k}=\mathrm{Pin}_{k}^{-}

The fermionic group data comes from the pullback as follows:
(-1)^{F}

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern use

for half of the symmetry types they consider.
Physically, given an SPT, its primed version consists of two stacked copies with opposite invariants, whose cancellation is prevented by the addition of a mixed time-reversal and reflection symmetry.

Mathematically, we choose to model the primed symmetry type of a given fermionic group

Here

Before discussing computational results for the free-to-interacting maps, we will introduce the spiral maps of IFTs, answering
0.3
.
Physically, these maps model the (dual process to) the dimensional reduction used by Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern, which, given an SPT in the Bott spiral, produces an SPT in one dimension lower with a modified symmetry type.

Remark 0.13
.

In the context of functorial field theory,
dimensional reduction
is often used synonymously with
compactification
. Let
\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}
denote the bordism (higher) category of
\xi
-manifolds and
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
denote a symmetric monoidal (higher) category.
The compactification of a topological field theory
\alpha\colon\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}\longrightarrow\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
on a
k
-manifold
M
with
1\leq k<n
is the topological field theory
\alpha_{M}\colon\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n-k}\to\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
whose value on any manifold or bordism
N
of dimension at most
n-k
is given by
\alpha(M\times N)
.
8
8

8

For more on dimensional reduction of TFTs, see Schommer-Pries
[
278
, §9]
and Yamashita–Yonekura
[
335
, §7.3]
.

However, dimensional reduction in a physics sense can also be modeled by other kinds of maps of topological field theories. For example, the dimensional reduction of SPTs induced by the condensation of a charged order parameter can be modeled by dualized
Smith homomorphisms
, as studied in
[
155
,
75
,
79
,
80
]
.
It is this second kind of model that we find more applicable to our situation of interest.

These two constructions are related, but we will not detail that here. Instead, we will discuss “dimensional reduction” only in the physics sense, and use the terminology “spiral map” for the mathematical maps of IFTs that we construct.
∎

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern define their spiral maps of SPTs differently depending on whether space is even- or odd-dimensional. As such, we define two different—but related—families of maps of spectra in order to model their spiral maps.
Each has as its first component
e_{\sigma}
, the zero-section map for
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
, which is the same map underlying the Smith homomorphism
\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}
, and which on IFTs induces a process dual to taking a codimension-one submanifold. See
Section

3.2
.
The other two ingredients are
c
, the
crush map
(
4.38
), which enforces forgetting a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-bundle, and
\Delta
(
4.39
), which we use to model the formation of a primed phase.
Physically,
c
forgets the symmetry and
\Delta
is a ‘doubling.’
We provide more of the physical motivation in
Section

4.3
.

4.41
and
4.51
.

For
\ell\geq 0
and
k>0
, let
\phi_{\ell,k}
be the composition

(0.14)

\phi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}=\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge e_{\sigma}}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge c}\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1},

and let
\psi_{\ell,k}
be the composition

(0.15)

\psi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}=\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge e_{\sigma}}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge\Delta}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{1,1}=\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k}.

Let

(0.16)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon\mho^{d+1}_{\ell,k-1}\to\mho^{d+2}_{\ell,k}

,

(0.17)

.
We refer to these maps as

and distinguish one from the other by the superscript.

Next, we discuss the computation of the free-to-interacting and spiral maps.
Before we get into the details,
we need the following definition.

Definition 0.18
.

Let
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\coloneqq\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
. Define

(0.19)

\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\coloneqq\begin{cases}4j+k-1,&\ell\equiv 0\bmod 4\\
4j+k,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We will show in
6.15
that
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
is the lowest degree in which the free-to-interacting map
F2I_{\ell,k}
is nonzero.

The following result describes the “long summands,” the subgroups of
E_{\ell,k}
-symmetric IFTs that we focus on. For the most part, we only study the IFTs in this summand, as they have the most interesting behavior with respect to the spiral maps and the free-to-interacting map.

5.55
and
6.48
.

Suppose at least one of
\ell
or
k
is positive, and write
\ell=4j+i
with
0\leq i\leq 3
. Let
\widetilde{m}(n)\coloneqq\lfloor(n-4j-k)/8\rfloor
.

(1)

If
n\equiv\ell+k-2i-1\bmod 4
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
,
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n}
is the direct sum of a summand
L
of the form

(0.20)

L\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(n)+4-i},&n\equiv\ell+k-2i+3\bmod 8\\
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(n)+5-i},&n\equiv\ell+k-2i-1\bmod 8\end{cases}

and cyclic groups of orders at most that of
L
, and in fact strictly smaller if
\lvert L\rvert>2
.

(2)

If
n\not\equiv\ell+k-2i-1\bmod 4
,
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n}
is simple
2
-torsion.

We call
L
the
long summand
of
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n}
when it exists.
10
10

10

In general,
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n}
has more than one direct-sum splitting satisfying
6.48
, so it is a priori ambiguous to say “the” long summand. However, by
6.15
, there is a canonical choice of long summand: the image of the free-to-interacting map. In what follows, we make this choice.

Our first computational result is that, in the dimensions where they exist, the long summands are precisely the image of the free-to-interacting map.

6.15
.

Let
\ell,k\geq 0
and
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
.
In degrees
d\equiv k-\ell-2\bmod 4
,
d\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-1
, the free-to-interacting map
F2I_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{KO}^{d+\ell-k-2}\to\mho_{\ell,k}^{d+2}
has image equal to the long summand of
\mho_{\ell,k}^{d+2}
.

Next, we calculate the images of the long summands under the two spiral maps.

6.16
.

Let
\ell,k\geq 0
and
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
.
The map

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon\mho_{\ell,k-1}^{n}\to\mho_{\ell,k}^{n+1}
maps the long summand of
\mho_{\ell,k-1}^{n}
isomorphically onto the long summand of
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n+1}
.

6.24
and
6.53
.

Suppose
\ell>0
and
k>0
or
\ell\geq 3
.
The map

\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}\colon\mho_{\ell+1,k}^{n}\to\mho_{\ell,k}^{n+1}
maps the long summand of
\mho_{\ell+1,k}^{n}
injectively into the long summand of
\mho_{\ell,k}^{n+1}
, with cokernel
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Thus we think of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
as “multiplication by
2
” on long summands.

The analogs of these theorems for the complex Bott spiral are
6.82
,
6.83
, and
6.84
.

Equipped with these computations, we can build a Bott spiral starting in any dimension and discrete
11
11

11

Using the computations in
Section

6.2
, one can also begin the Bott spiral from a continuous Altland–Zirnbauer class. However, as we illustrate in
4.73
, if the corresponding discrete and continuous fermionic groups are not isomorphic, as happens in classes AI, AII, C, CI, and CII, this variant of the Bott spiral is not periodic: it terminates after finitely many steps. We interpret this as further evidence that it is the discrete fermionic groups, rather than the continuous ones, that correctly model the Bott spiral.

Altland–Zirnbauer class by alternately applying the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
and using
2.31
where necessary to identify
E_{\ell,k+4}\cong E_{\ell+4,k}
. As mentioned above, this isomorphism is an interacting analog of Bott periodicity. After eight steps, one arrives back at the same Altland–Zirnbauer class, eight dimensions higher. By the above theorems, if one starts in a dimension and class that contains a long summand of order
N
, after eight steps one also has a long summand of order
16N
. Thus the “Bott clock” of periodicity for free fermions has unraveled into a “Bott spiral” that gets bigger and bigger every period.

We specifically consider the three spirals that start with classes
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
, CII, and AIII in dimension
d=1
, as these are the cases Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
consider. Comparing their results (
ibid.
, Figure 1) with ours (
Figure

1
,
Figure

2

5.55
, and
6.15
), we have almost complete agreement. We discuss the discrepancy in
4.70
, where we argue that it arises from a subtlety difficult to detect on the free fermion side of the story, and that our field-theoretic analysis leads to the correct symmetry types in the spiral.

From our models and computations, we gained some insights towards the more general question of studying free-to-interacting maps.

•

Discrete versus continuous Altland–Zirnbauer classes
, or more generally
how to specify the symmetries of a fermionic system.
In order to define and study free-to-interacting maps at all, one must specify the symmetries acting on the phases of interest. We found that
this information flows from the interacting to the free setting,
by choosing a fermionic group
G_{f}
of symmetries on the interacting side, then obtaining the symmetry algebra
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
on the free side. This is opposite to most prior work on free-to-interacting maps, in which one begins with an Altland–Zirnbauer class, perhaps with some extra data, then attempts to find the best-matching fermionic group realizing this data. Because nonisomorphic fermionic groups may have Morita-equivalent fermionic group superalgebras, extracting the group from the Morita class algebra is in general ambiguous.

Though this point was first raised in
[
293
]
, it underlies several of our key observations in this paper, and was the most important conceptual step in modeling the Bott spiral. Specifically, our choice to use the fermionic groups
E_{\ell,k}
to model the ten Altland–Zirnbauer classes (the “discrete,” rather than the “continuous,” choice) is invisible on the free side, but is essential on the interacting side in several ways.

(1)

The groups of IFTs in a given dimension and Altland–Zirnbauer class depend on this choice of fermionic group.
Freed–Hopkins’ computations
[
118
, §9.3]
imply that the continuous fermionic groups do not in general match Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s results; see
2.27
.

(2)

It is unclear how to define the spiral maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
for the continuous fermionic groups. Even trying to make a “mixed discrete-continuous” Bott spiral, by mixing
E_{\ell,k}
with a continuous fermionic group, is doomed: as we explain in
4.73
, such a spiral terminates after finitely many steps. By contrast, Bott spirals using
E_{\ell,k}
fully match the periodicity identified by Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern.

(3)

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
use a parameter
\mu
to modify their general pattern for the classification of SPTs in Altland–Zirnbauer types BDI,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime}
, and DIII. In
4.70
, we clarify this phenomenon as arising from the presence of an additional prime on the fermionic group in these three cases (see
4.4
). This behavior is unexpected, and important for correctly modeling the Bott spiral, but cannot be seen solely from the free side.

It would be interesting to study other examples of this “Morita variance” phenomenon.

•

“Interacting Bott periodicity” and the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation.
One major motivation for studying the Bott spiral is to understand what remains of Bott periodicity when one passes from free to interacting phases: see
[
263
, Figure 1]
.
Since Bott classes in
\mathit{KO}_{8}
, resp.
\mathit{KU}_{2}
, lift to the spin, resp. spin
c
bordism classes of a Bott manifold, resp.
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, compactifying on these manifolds implements an analog of Bott periodicity for interacting SPTs, though this process is generally neither injective nor surjective. See
[
27
, §1.4.4]
. Our computations imply that in the cases relevant for the Bott spiral, namely the long summands of
\mho_{\ell,k}^{*}
, this compactification is surjective, with kernel isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/16
in the real case and
\mathbb{Z}/2
in the complex case. This agrees with Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s results
[
263
]
.

However, there is also the question of the periodicity of the Altland–Zirnbauer classes themselves. Freed–Hopkins’ choices of fermionic groups representing the eight real Altland–Zirnbauer classes
[
118
, (9.25)]
are not obviously periodic; Freed–Hopkins were not to our knowledge trying to construct a periodic sequence of interacting symmetry types.
This once again suggests that, in order to construct a Bott-periodic sequence of interacting symmetry types, we must use a different model.

2.31
, constructing an isomorphism
E_{4,0}\cong E_{0,4}
of fermionic groups, provides the answer. It implies that alternatively decreasing
\ell
and increasing
k
, like in our model of the Bott spiral, produces an
8
-periodic sequence of fermionic groups, hence an
8
-periodic sequence of interacting symmetry types matching the Bott clock on the free side.

As part of our study of the Bott spiral, we proved some other results that may be of independent interest.

•

Section

2
, we review the mathematical models for the kinds of topological phases that we study in this paper: free fermions via
K
-theory in
Section

2.1
, and interacting SPTs in terms of bordism in
Section

2.2
. In
Section

2.3
, we introduce the fermionic groups we use to model the discrete and continuous versions of the ten Altland–Zirnbauer types (
Table

1
).

We define our
free-to-interacting maps
in
Section

3
. We review the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro (ABS) construction and Smith homomorphisms in
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.2
, respectively. Then, in
Section

3.3
and
Section

3.4
, we combine these two ideas to define twisted ABS maps, and apply Anderson duality to define our free-to-interacting maps in
Section

3.5
.

With all of the ingredients in place, we provide our model of the Bott spiral in
Section

4
. We define “primed Altland–Zirnbauer classes” in

Section

4.1
, and interpret them as including a spatial reflection symmetry in
Section

4.1.1
. In
Section

4.2
, we study the images of the free-to-interacting maps from
Section

3.5
on homotopy groups. In
Section

4.3
, we construct the spiral maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
, then use them to model the three Bott spirals of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern: the complex Bott spiral (starting with class AIII) in
Figure

3
, the real Bott spiral starting with class
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
in
Figure

5
, and the real Bott spiral starting with class CII in
Figure

6
.

Our final two sections,
Section

5
and
Section

6
, are devoted to the computations supporting our main results: the computations of (the interesting part of) the free-to-interacting maps and the spiral maps for the discrete Altland–Zirnbauer symmetry types appearing in the Bott spiral.
Section

5
performs an approximation to our computations, in which spin bordism is replaced with
\mathit{ko}
-homology.
Section

5.1
begins with some generalities, and
Section

5.2
introduces four key examples and proves
5.55
. In
Section

5.3
and
Section

5.4
, we prove
5.58
,
5.61
, and
5.88
, calculating
\mathit{ko}
-theoretic approximations to the free-to-interacting and spiral maps. In
Section

6.1
we lift to spin bordism, yielding our main computations in
6.15
,
6.16
, and
6.24
. The remaining sections discuss several variants of the free-to-interacting and spiral maps: “mixed discrete-continuous” variants in
Section

6.2
, complex versions in
Section

6.3
, and the discrete class C maps in
Section

6.4
.

1.
Fermionic groups,
K
-theory, and bordism

In this work, we study the relationship between free fermionic phases, which are classified by
K
-theory groups
[
197
,
121
]
, and interacting fermionic phases, which under the SPT-bordism conjecture
[
187
,
185
,
118
]
are classified by Anderson-dual bordism groups.
We present a unified account of symmetries in both classifications using the language of fermionic groups, which allows us to predict the form of the free-to-interacting maps in
Section

2
.

1.1.
The definition of a fermionic group

Definition 1.1

(Benson
[
52
, §7]
)
.

A
fermionic group
consists of

•

a Lie group
G_{f}
,

•

a central element
(-1)^{F}
of order two called
fermion parity
,

•

and a homomorphism
\theta\colon G_{f}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
,

such that
\theta((-1)^{F})=0
.

A morphism of fermionic groups
(G_{f},((-1)^{F})_{G},\theta_{G})\to(H,((-1)^{F})_{H},\theta_{H})
is a Lie group homomorphism
\varphi\colon G_{f}\to H_{f}
such that
\varphi(((-1)^{F})_{G})=((-1)^{F})_{H}
and
\theta_{G}=\theta_{H}\circ\varphi
. With these morphisms, fermionic groups form a category
\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
.

There is a central subgroup
\{\pm 1\}\subseteq G_{f}
generated by
(-1)^{F}
.
Physically,
G_{f}
represents the group of symmetries of a given physical systems with fermions.
This group potentially contains time-reversing (or anti-unitary) symmetries as indicated by
\theta
.
We denote by
G_{b}\coloneqq{G_{f}}/\{\pm 1\}
the associated bosonic symmetry group.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
(-1)^{F}\in G_{f}\xrightarrow{\theta}\mathbb{Z}/2
be a fermionic group.
The
associated twist

\tau
is the element

(1.3)

(\theta,\omega)\in H^{1}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H^{2}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)

defined as follows.

•

The Hurewicz and universal coefficient theorems imply a natural isomorphism
\Hom(G_{b},\mathbb{Z}/2)\overset{\cong}{\to}H^{1}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We use this isomorphism to identify
\theta\colon G_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
with the class
\theta
in (
1.3
).

•

The class
\omega
in (
1.3
) classifies the extension

(1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\pm 1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1.}}

The terminology ‘twist’ comes from twisted cohomology, as
\tau
also specifies a twist of
G_{b}
-equivariant
\mathit{KO}
-theory (see
[
94
]
,
[
139
, §3.4.2]
) and spin bordism of
BG_{b}
(see
[
158
]
).
We will see in later sections how such twisted
G_{b}
-equivariant cohomology theories classify
G_{b}
-protected SPT phases.
The twist encodes physical properties, including which elements of
G_{f}
act by antiunitary symmetries, and how they mix with fermion parity
(-1)^{F}

(e.g.
T^{2}=(-1)^{F}
in the fermionic group
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
).

Observe that twists are natural in homomorphisms of fermionic groups. More specifically, a homomorphism
G_{f}\to H_{f}
induces a homomorphism
\phi\colon G_{b}\to H_{b}
such that
\phi^{*}(\omega_{G})=\omega_{H}
and
\phi^{*}(\theta_{G})=\theta_{H}
.

Example 1.5
.

If
\rho\colon G_{b}\to\mathrm{O}_{n}
is a representation, there are two associated fermionic groups defined by pulling back the
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
extensions of
\mathrm{O}_{n}
along
\rho
(see Footnote
7
):

(1.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pin}_{n}^{\pm}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{O}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

For the pin
+
twist,
\theta=w_{1}(\rho)
and
\omega=w_{2}(\rho)
; for the pin
-
twist,
\theta=w_{1}(\rho)
and
\omega=w_{2}(\rho)+w_{1}(\rho)^{2}
, where
w_{i}(\rho)
denotes the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
Stiefel–Whitney class of the vector bundle over
BG_{b}
induced by
\rho
.

The universal case
G_{b}=\mathrm{O}_{n}
and
\rho=\mathrm{id}
defines fermionic group structures on
\mathrm{Pin}_{n}^{\pm}
with
(\mathrm{Pin}_{n}^{\pm})_{b}\cong\mathrm{O}_{n}
.
For
G_{b}=\mathrm{SO}_{n}
and
\rho
the inclusion, we obtain a fermionic group structure on
\mathrm{Spin}_{n}
.
∎

The fermionic tensor product of
G
and
H
is a certain fermionic group whose underlying bosonic group is
G_{b}\times H_{b}
:

Definition 1.7

(Benson
[
52
, §7]
)
.

The
fermionic tensor product

G\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H
of
(G,\theta_{G},\omega_{G}),(H,\theta_{H},\omega_{H})
is the set

(1.8)

\frac{G\times H}{\{\pm 1\}}

equipped with the operation

(1.9)

(g_{1}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}h_{1})(g_{2}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}h_{2})=\begin{cases}(-1)^{F}g_{1}g_{2}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}h_{1}h_{2},&\text{ if }\theta(h_{1})=1\text{ and }\theta(g_{2})=1\\
g_{1}g_{2}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}h_{1}h_{2},&\text{ if }\theta(h_{1})=0\text{ or }\theta(g_{2})=0.\end{cases}

G\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H
has the structure of a fermionic group, with the following data.

(1)

\theta(g,h)=\theta_{G}(g)+\theta_{H}(h)
for
g\in G
and
h\in H
; since
\theta_{G}
and
\theta_{H}
vanish on the
\{\pm 1\}
subgroup we quotiented by, this is indeed well-defined.

(2)

The central subgroup
\{\pm 1\}\times\{\pm 1\}\subset G\times H
maps to a central subgroup of
G\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H
isomorphic to
\{\pm 1\}
under the quotient map; we define
(-1)^{F}
of
G\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H
to be the generator of this
\{\pm 1\}
subgroup.

It is straightforward, if a little tedious, to check the following proposition directly.

Proposition 1.10
.

\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
is symmetric monoidal with respect to the fermionic tensor product; the unit is
\{\pm 1\}
and the braiding is given by

(1.11)

g\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}h\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\begin{cases}(-1)^{F}h\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}g&\text{ if }\theta(h)=1\text{ and }\theta(g)=1\\
h\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}g,&\text{ if }\theta(h)=0\text{ or }\theta(g)=0.\end{cases}

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
G_{f}
and
H_{f}
be fermionic groups.

(1)

There is a natural isomorphism
(G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f})_{b}\xrightarrow{\cong}G_{b}\times H_{b}
of groups.

(2)

The associated twist to
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}
is

(1.13)

(\theta_{G}+\theta_{H},\omega_{G}+\theta_{G}\theta_{H}+\omega_{H}).

Proof.

Part (
1
) follows directly from
1.7
. The formula for
\theta
of
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}
follows from the the natural identification
H^{1}(BK;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Hom(K,\mathbb{Z}/2)
of abelian groups, where
K
is a Lie group, and the fact that we defined
\theta
of
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}
by adding
\theta_{G}
and
\theta_{H}
.

The formula for
\omega
is less trivial. It must be a natural formula in the data of two fermionic groups; the only natural cohomology classes associated to this data are
\theta_{G}
,
\theta_{H}
,
\omega_{G}
, and
\omega_{H}
and classes built out of them (e.g. products). Moreover, the formula must be symmetric in
G_{f}
and
H_{f}
, because the fermionic tensor product is symmetric monoidal. Therefore there are constants
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\lambda_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
such that

(1.14)

\omega_{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\lambda_{1}(\omega_{G}+\omega_{H})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}(\theta_{G}^{2}+\theta_{H}^{2})}+{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}(\theta_{G}\theta_{H})}.

It suffices to determine
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\lambda_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}}
. Therefore we may without loss of generality pass to cohomology.
We will show
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\lambda_{1}=1}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}=0}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}=1}
.

(1)

To show
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\lambda_{1}=1}
, let
G_{f}
be any fermionic group with
\theta_{G}=0
but
\omega_{G}\neq 0
and let
H_{f}=\{\pm 1\}
. For example, we could take
G_{f}=\mathrm{SU}_{2}
with the fermionic group structure discussed in
[
293
, Example 9]
. Then
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}\cong G_{f}
as fermionic groups, so
\omega_{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}=\omega_{G}
.

(2)

To show
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}=0}
, let
G_{f}
be any fermionic group with
\theta_{G}\neq 0
,
\theta_{G}^{2}\neq 0
, and
\omega_{G}=0
, and let
H_{f}=\{\pm 1\}
again. For example, we could take
G=\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
. Then
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}\cong G_{f}
again, and now
\omega_{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}=0
and
\theta_{G}^{2}\neq 0
, forcing
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\lambda_{2}=0}
.

(3)

To show
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}=1}
, let
G_{f}=H_{f}=\mathbb{Z}/4\times\{\pm 1\}
, so that
G_{b}=H_{b}=\mathbb{Z}/4
, with
\theta_{G},\theta_{H}\colon\mathbb{Z}/4\to\mathbb{Z}/2
both equal to the unique nontrivial homomorphism. We also have
\omega_{G}=\omega_{H}=0
. Eilenberg–Mac Lane
[
99
,
100
,
101
]
proved an isomorphism
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/4;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x,y]/(x^{2})
with
\lvert x\rvert=1
and
\lvert y\rvert=2
; since
\theta_{G}
and
\theta_{H}
are nontrivial, they both equal
x
, and we deduce
\theta_{G}^{2}=\theta_{H}^{2}=0
.

Thus for this choice of
G_{f}
and
H_{f}
, (
1.14
) simplifies to

(1.15)

\omega_{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}={\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\lambda_{3}}\theta_{G}\theta_{H},

and all we have to do is show that the extension

(1.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\pm 1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/4\times\mathbb{Z}/4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1,}}

which is classified by
\omega_{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}}
, is not split. The split extension
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}/4\times\mathbb{Z}/4
is abelian, so it suffices to show
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}
is not abelian, which can be checked directly from
1.7
. ∎

Remark 1.17
.

It is no coincidence that (
1.13
) looks like the Whitney sum formula. Indeed, recall that
B\mathrm{O}
is a grouplike
E_{\infty}
-space under
\oplus
, and let
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}
denote the cofiber, in the
\infty
-category of
E_{\infty}
-spaces, of the forgetful
E_{\infty}
-map
B\mathrm{Spin}\to B\mathrm{O}
. Then
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}
is again a grouplike
E_{\infty}
-space, and is equivalent to
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
as spaces but not as
E_{1}
-spaces
[
85
, §1.2.3]
—the direct sum structure on
B\mathrm{O}
induces a non-product
E_{\infty}
-structure on
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
, which on cohomology is the Whitney sum formula for Stiefel–Whitney classes. See also
[
48
, Proposition 4.19]
for a related computation.
∎

Sometimes fermionic groups arise as (s)pin covers of representations. Recall from Footnote
7
our conventions for pin groups, and
recall that the direct sum of inner product spaces induces a smooth homomorphism

(1.18)

\oplus\colon\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}\times\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{O}_{\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime}}

for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}\geq 0
. These maps are associative on a triple product
\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}\times\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\times\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}
.

Definition 1.19
.

Let
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
denote the symmetric monoidal category specified by the following data.

(1)

An object of
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
is a Lie group
G
and a smooth homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
.

(2)

A morphism
f
from
(G,\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k})
to
(H,\rho^{\prime}\colon H\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k})
is a smooth group homomorphism
f_{1}\colon G\to H
intertwining the maps to
\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
up to conjugation by an element of
\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
. If
(\ell,k)\neq(\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime})
, there are no morphisms from
(G,\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k})
to
(H,\rho^{\prime}\colon H\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}})
.

(3)

The unit is the trivial group with its unique representation into
\mathrm{O}_{0,0}=\{1\}
.

(4)

The monoidal product is the external product. Specifically, the external product of
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
and
\rho^{\prime}\colon H\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
is the composition

(1.20a)

G\times H\xrightarrow{\rho\times\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}\times\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow[\eqref{oplus_defn_orth}]{\oplus}\mathrm{O}_{\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime}}.

(5)

The braiding is induced from the usual one in the Cartesian symmetric monoidal structure on the symmetric monoidal category of Lie groups.

In addition, let
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
consisting of compact
G
with
\rho
factoring through
\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
.
12
12

12

If
G
is compact,
\rho
factors through
\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
up to conjugation, so the second condition is no loss of generality.

Definition 1.21
.

For
A\in\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
, let
\mathrm{Ad}_{A}\colon\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
be conjugation by
A
:
\mathrm{Ad}_{A}(B)\coloneqq ABA^{-1}
.

A
has exactly two preimages under
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
; since they differ by a central element, they define the same conjugation homomorphism
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}\to\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
; call this map
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ad}}_{A}
.

Definition 1.22
.

Let
\zeta\colon\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}\to\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
be the functor sending
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
to the pullback
G_{f}
in

(1.23a)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

with
\{\pm 1\}\coloneqq\ker(\pi)
and
\theta\coloneqq\det\circ\rho\circ\pi\colon G_{f}\to\{\pm 1\}
.

The definition on morphisms is a little nontrivial.
Let
f\colon(G,\rho_{G}\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k})\to(H,\rho_{H}\colon H\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k})
be a morphism in
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
, meaning
f\colon G\to H
is a group homomorphism and there is an
A\in\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
such that for all
g\in G
,
\mathrm{Ad}_{A}(\rho_{G}(g))=\rho_{H}(f(g))
. Thus we have a commutative diagram whose top and bottom faces are pullback squares:

(1.23b)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{Ad}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ad}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The maps
f\circ\pi_{G}\colon G_{f}\to H
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ad}}_{A}\circ\widetilde{\rho}_{G}\colon G_{f}\to\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
thus commute under the maps to
\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
in the bottom right of the cube, so by the universal property of the pullback they induce a map
\zeta(f)\colon G_{f}\to H_{f}
. Since
\mathrm{Ad}_{A}
intertwines the determinant and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ad}}_{A}
intertwines multiplication by
(-1)^{F}\in\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
,
\zeta(f)
is indeed a morphism of fermionic groups.

This is a mixed-signature generalization of
1.5
.

Lemma 1.24
.

There is a pullback diagram

(1.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}\times\mathrm{O}_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}_{\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime}}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

natural with respect to the associators for triple products and the braidings on both sides.

Proof sketch.

This diagram and the desired naturality statements follow directly from the definition of the pin group of a vector space equipped with a quadratic form, as in
[
211
, Definition I.2.3]
.
∎

See also
[
158
, §3.2]
.

Corollary 1.26
.

\zeta\colon\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}\to\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
admits the structure of a symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof sketch.

Compose the pullback square (
1.25
) with the pullback square

(1.27)

\hbox to170.38pt{\vbox to54.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.1883pt\lower-27.19838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
