Definition 1.28
.

Let
A
be an
E_{\infty}
-space. Then there is a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/A}
, the category of spaces with a map to
A
,
defined as follows:
use the multiplication
\mu\colon A\times A\to A
to define the “external sum” of maps
f_{1}\colon X\to A
and
f_{2}\colon Y\to A
as follows. The product map
f_{1}\boxplus f_{2}\colon X\times Y\to A
is the composition

(1.29)

X\times Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}\times f_{2}}A\times A\overset{\mu}{\longrightarrow}A.

This sum refines to a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category structure on
\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/A}
, and the functor
\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/A}\to\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B}
induced by an
E_{\infty}
-map
A\to B
is symmetric monoidal.

In what follows, we use the bar construction model for the classifying space
BG
of a topological group
G
. With this choice, there is a natural homeomorphism
B(G\times H)\cong BG\times BH
.

Recall that
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}
is the full subcategory of
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
of representations of
compact
Lie groups valued in
\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}\subset\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
.

Definition 1.30
.

Write
V_{j}\to B\mathrm{O}_{j}
for the tautological bundle.
Let
\xi\colon\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\to\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}}
denote the functor sending a representation
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
to the map

(1.31)

BG\xrightarrow{B\rho}B\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times B\mathrm{O}_{k}\xrightarrow{V_{\ell}-V_{k}+k-\ell}B\mathrm{O}.

We include the
k-\ell
term in (
1.31
) so that the virtual bundle has rank zero, hence is classified by
B\mathrm{O}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}\times B\mathrm{O}
.

Lemma 1.32
.

Give
B\mathrm{O}
its direct sum
E_{\infty}
-structure and
\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}}
the induced symmetric monoidal structure as in
1.28
. Then
\xi
is symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

This follows essentially by definition of the direct sum
E_{\infty}
-structure on
B\mathrm{O}
, together with symmetric monoidality of the classifying space functor.
∎

Lemma 1.33
.

Let
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
be an object of
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}
and
f\colon BG\to B\mathrm{O}
be
\xi(G,\rho)
. Then the twisting data
(\theta,\omega)
for
\zeta(G)
equals
(f^{*}(w_{1}),f^{*}(w_{2}))
.

Proof.

The equality to prove is natural in
(G,\rho)
, so it suffices to show it in the universal case
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
. Then the Whitney sum formula and
1.12
reduce the claim to checking for the left and right inclusions
\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
, resp.
\mathrm{O}_{k}\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell}\times\mathrm{O}_{k}
respectively. This then boils down to the claims that the twisting data for
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell}^{-}
, resp.
\mathrm{Pin}_{k}^{+}
, are
(w_{1},w_{2}+w_{1}^{2})
, resp.
(w_{1},w_{2})
, which is standard.
∎

Remark 1.34
.

This
E_{\infty}
-structure on
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
also arises naturally from the following context. Let
\mathit{s\mathscr{L}ine}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the category of real superlines, i.e. one-dimensional real supervector spaces with invertible linear maps.

\mathit{s\mathscr{L}ine}_{\mathbb{R}}
acquires an
E_{\infty}
-structure
\hat{\otimes}
from the fact that it is the maximal Picard groupoid inside the symmetric monoidal category
\mathit{s\mathscr{V}ect}_{\mathbb{R}}
with its Koszul braiding (see (
1.41
)).
This makes the inclusion

(1.35)

B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}\simeq B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{R}^{\times}\simeq B\mathit{s\mathscr{L}ine}_{\mathbb{R}}

into an
E_{\infty}
-map. Like for
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}
, the equivalence
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{R}^{\times}\simeq B\mathit{s\mathscr{L}ine}_{\mathbb{R}}
of spaces is not
E_{1}
.
∎

The next definition will play a crucial role in our physical application.

Definition 1.36
.

The fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
is

(1.37)

E_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\underbracket{\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\dotsb\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}}_{\text{$\ell$ copies}}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\underbracket{\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\dotsb\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}}_{\text{$k$ copies}}.

By
1.12
,
(E_{\ell,k})_{b}
is isomorphic to the elementary abelian
2
-group
13
13

13

Here and throughout this paper, “
2
-groups” are in the sense of
p
-groups, i.e. finite groups of order
2^{s}
for some
s
. We do not use “
n
-groups” in the sense of, e.g.,
[
32
]
.

(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}
.

Remark 1.38
.

One may equivalently define
E_{\ell,k}
as the preimage of the diagonal
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}\subset\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
under the double cover
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
, lifting
E_{\ell,k}
to an object of
\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}
.
This definition makes sense for
\ell=k=0
: in this case,
\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}=1
is the trivial group and so
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}=\{\pm 1\}=E_{0,0}
. If we regard the empty fermionic product as the monoidal unit, then
1.36
also gives
E_{0,0}=\{\pm 1\}
.
∎

Remark 1.39
.

We record the twisting data for
E_{\ell,k}
, which the reader can verify directly from
1.12
.

Use the Künneth formula to write
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x_{1},\dotsc,x_{\ell+k}]
, with
\lvert x_{i}\rvert=1
for all
i
. Under the isomorphism
H^{1}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Hom(\mathbb{Z}/2^{\ell+k},\mathbb{Z}/2)
,
x_{i}
is dual to the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
standard basis vector in
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}
. Then the twisting data of
E_{\ell,k}
is

(1.40)

\theta=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+k}x_{i}\qquad\qquad\omega=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{\ell+k}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell+k}x_{i}x_{j}.\qed

1.2.
Superalgebras and Clifford algebras

In this paper, we frequently work with
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded objects and require the tensor product to obey the Koszul sign rule with respect to the grading. We summarize the definitions we use; the reader who is familiar with superalgebras and Clifford algebras is welcome to jump to
1.53
.

Fix a field
F
of characteristic not equal to
2
; in this paper we will only need
F=\mathbb{R}
and
F=\mathbb{C}
. A
super vector space

V
over
F
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded vector space over
F
. The tensor product
V\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}W
of two super vector spaces
V
and
W
is given the following grading: if
v\in V
and
w\in W
are homogeneous elements, then
v\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}w
has degree
\lvert v\rvert+\lvert w\rvert\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. We use the Koszul sign rule isomorphism
V\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}W\xrightarrow{\cong}W\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}V
, defined on pure tensors of homogeneous elements by

(1.41)

v\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}w\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(-1)^{\lvert v\rvert\lvert w\rvert}w\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}v.

This formula extends uniquely to define an isomorphism on the entire tensor product, and is the symmetry data in a symmetric monoidal category
\mathit{s\mathscr{V}ect}_{F}
of super vector spaces and grading-preserving
F
-linear maps.

Definition 1.42
.

(1)

A
superalgebra

A
over
F
is a super vector space that is also an algebra, such that the unit has even grading and the multiplication
A\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}A\to A
preserves the grading.

(2)

A
superhomomorphism

A\to B
between two superalgebras is a grading-preserving algebra homomorphism.

(3)

A
supermodule
over a superalgebra
A
is a super vector space
M
that is also an
A
-module whose action map preserves the grading.
14
14

14

This does
not
mean that a superalgebra
A
acts on a supermodule
M
by grading-preserving endomorphisms! Instead, it means that if
a\in A
and
m\in M
are odd, then
a\cdot m
has the same grading as
a\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}m
in
A\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}M
: even.

The tensor product of superalgebras is canonically a superalgebra with the product map defined to extend linearly from the following formula on pure tensors of homogeneous elements:

(1.43)

(v_{1}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}w_{1})\cdot(v_{2}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}w_{2})\coloneqq(-1)^{\lvert v_{2}\rvert\lvert w_{1}\rvert}v_{1}v_{2}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}w_{1}w_{2}.

Definition 1.44
.

The
opposite
of a superalgebra
A^{\mathit{op}}
is defined to be equal to
A
as a graded vector space but with multiplication

(1.45)

a{{}^{\mathit{op}}}\cdot b{{}^{\mathit{op}}}=(-1)^{|a||b|}(ba){{}^{\mathit{op}}}.

A straightforward computation shows that
(A\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}B){{}^{\mathit{op}}}\cong A{{}^{\mathit{op}}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}B{{}^{\mathit{op}}}
.

One can thus define superbimodules and module and bimodule homomorphisms to be grading-preserving linear maps that commute with the action maps.

Remark 1.46
.

There is a symmetric monoidal bicategory
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{F}
whose objects are
F
-superalgebras, whose
1
-morphisms are bimodules, and whose
2
-morphisms are bimodule homomorphisms.
The symmetric monoidal structure is the tensor product with Koszul sign rule as in (
1.41
).
This is called the
Morita bicategory of superalgebras
; we refer to
[
77
, §3.2]
for the ungraded case and
[
277
]
for a general construction of a higher Morita category of algebras in a symmetric monoidal category.
The name of this bicategory comes from the fact that its equivalences between objects are precisely the Morita equivalences of superalgebras.

If
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}^{1}_{F}
is the
1
-category of superalgebras, there is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}^{1}_{F}\to\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{F}
given by sending a homomorphism
\phi\colon A\to B
to the associated
(B,A)
-bimodule with action twisted by
\phi
.
It can be convenient to know whether a functor
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}^{1}_{F}\to\mathcal{C}
factors through
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{F}
as it will automatically imply Morita invariance on objects.
∎

Definition 1.47
.

Let
F
be a field whose characteristic is not
2
, and
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space over
F
. Let
q\colon V\to F
be a quadratic form.
The
Clifford algebra

\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q)
is the
F
-algebra

(1.48)

\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q)\coloneqq T(V)/((v^{2}-q(v))\cdot 1),

where
T(V)
denotes the tensor algebra. We make the following notational shortcuts.

•

Let
q_{\ell,k}
denote the quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{\ell+k}
defined by

(1.49)

q_{\ell,k}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{\ell+k})=x_{1}^{2}+\dotsb+x_{\ell}^{2}-x_{\ell+1}^{2}-\dotsb-x_{\ell+k}^{2}.

We will let
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\mathit{C\ell}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{\ell+k},q_{\ell,k})
.

•

If
k=0
in the previous example, we will just write
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell}
; if instead
\ell=0
we will write
\mathit{C\ell}_{-k}
.

•

Let
q_{m}
denote the quadratic form on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
defined by

(1.50)

q_{m}(z_{1},\dotsc,z_{m})\coloneqq z_{1}^{2}+\dotsb+z_{m}^{2}.

We write
\mathbb{C}\ell_{m}\coloneqq\mathit{C\ell}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{m},q_{m})
.

We give
\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q)
the structure of a super vector space by specifying that a product of
n
elements of
V
has grading
n\bmod 2
; since the quotient in (
1.48
) is by an ideal spanned by products of even numbers of elements of
V
, this grading, a priori defined on
T(V)
, descends to
\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q)
as claimed. Moreover, multiplication preserves the grading and therefore
\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q)
is a superalgebra.
There is an isomorphism
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{k,\ell}\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}\ell_{\ell+k}
of superalgebras, since multiplying generators by
i
changes the sign of their square.

Lemma 1.51

(Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro
[
29
, Proposition 1.6]
)
.

Let
V
and
q
be as in
1.47
, and suppose
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. Then the inclusions
V_{1}\to V
and
V_{2}\to V
extend to a natural isomorphism of superalgebras

(1.52)

\mathit{C\ell}(F,V_{1},q|_{V_{1}})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}_{F}\mathit{C\ell}(F,V_{2},q|_{V_{2}})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathit{C\ell}(F,V,q).

In particular, for
m_{1},m_{2},n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0
,
\mathit{C\ell}_{m_{1},n_{1}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{m_{2},n_{2}}\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{m_{1}+m_{2},n_{1}+n_{2}}
.
We also have
\mathit{C\ell}_{m,n}{{}^{\mathit{op}}}\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{n,m}
.

1.3.
Twisted group superalgebras

Every fermionic group has a canonical group superalgebra associated to it:

Definition 1.53

(Benson
[
52
, §7]
)
.

Let
G_{f}
be a finite fermionic group.
The
fermionic group algebra of
G_{f}
is the real superalgebra

(1.54)

\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\coloneqq\frac{\mathbb{R}[G_{f}]}{((-1)^{F}+1)}

graded by
\theta
.

Remark 1.55
.

Let
(\theta,\omega)
be the twisting data for a fermionic group
G_{f}
. Let
\widetilde{\omega}
be a cocycle
15
15

15

The cocycle condition is necessary in order for the multiplication in (
1.56
) to be associative. An alternative approach is to allow all cochains and construct nonassociative algebras; for example, Albuquerque–Majid
[
11
,
12
]
construct the octonion algebra as a twisted group
\mathbb{R}
-algebra for
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
with a cochain that is not a cocycle.
whose cohomology class is
\omega
, and without loss of generality assume
\widetilde{\omega}
is

unital
, meaning
\widetilde{\omega}(e,g)=\widetilde{\omega}(g,e)=0
, where
e\in G_{b}
is the identity. Then
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
as defined in
1.53
is isomorphic as superalgebras to vector space
\mathbb{R}[G_{b}]
with the multiplication modified to satisfy the formula

(1.56)

g_{1}\cdot_{\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f},\widetilde{\omega}]}g_{2}\coloneqq(-1)^{\widetilde{\omega}(g_{1},g_{2})}g_{1}g_{2}.

As
G_{b}
is a natural basis for
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{b}]
, this formula extends uniquely to define a product on the entire vector spae. One can check this product is associative and that
e\in G_{b}
is the unit for it; this latter fact uses that
\widetilde{\omega}
is unital. The
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading is defined in the same way as in
1.53
.
∎

Remark 1.57
.

The major reason we restrict to finite groups is that the group algebras of infinite groups are not suitable for our application.
However, we expect many of our considerations to generalize at least to compact Lie groups if we replace the fermionic group algebra by a twisted group
C^{*}
-algebra.
∎

Example 1.58
.

We calculate
\mathbb{R}^{f}[\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{\pm 1}
using
1.55
. This computation is also done a different way by Stolz
[
300
, Proposition 8.3]
and Albuquerque–Majid
[
13
]
.

Since
(\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm})_{b}\cong\mathrm{O}_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, we need to compute
\mathbb{Z}/2
-valued cocycles on
\mathbb{Z}/2
. For any
n\geq 0
,
H^{n}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. In general, if
M
carries the trivial
G
-action, the group of cocycles
Z^{1}(G;M)
is the group of homomorphisms
G\to M
; thus we may represent the nontrivial element
x\in H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
by
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
. Thus
x^{2}\in H^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is represented by the
2
-cocycle
\mathrm{id}\smile\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
with the formula

(1.59)

(\mathrm{id}\smile\mathrm{id})(a,b)\coloneqq a\cdot b.

The bundle
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
is a nontrivial line bundle, so
w_{1}(\sigma)\neq 0
, which forces
w_{1}(\sigma)=x
, and
w_{2}(\sigma)=0
. Thus
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
has
\theta=x
and
\omega=0
. That is, we do not modify the multiplication in
\mathbb{R}[\mathrm{O}_{1}]
:

(1.60)

\mathbb{R}^{f}[\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}]\cong\mathbb{R}[\mathrm{O}_{1}]\cong\mathbb{R}[a]/(a^{2}-1),

with
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading odd on
a
and even on scalars. This is
\mathit{C\ell}_{1}
by definition.

For
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
, we modify the multiplication by the formula in (
1.59
). This cocycle is only nonzero when evaluated on
(1,1)
, so only has the effect of multiplying
a^{2}
by
(-1)^{(\mathrm{id}\smile\mathrm{id})(1,1)}=-1
. Since
\theta
is the same as it was for
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
, we again have
a
odd and scalars even; thus

(1.61)

\mathbb{R}^{f}[\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}]\cong\mathbb{R}[a]/(a^{2}+1)\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{-1}.\qed

Example 1.62

(Stolz
[
300
, Proposition 8.3]
)
.

Consider the fermionic group
C\coloneqq\{\pm 1,\pm i\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
with
\theta
trivial and
(-1)^{F}=-1\in C
. This fermionic group has
G_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\omega
nontrivial, so following a similar line of reasoning as in
1.58
one learns that as superalgebras,
\mathbb{R}^{f}[C]\cong\mathbb{C}
in purely even grading.
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathit{C\ell}_{-1}
are isomorphic as algebras, but not as superalgebras, as the gradings do not match.
∎

Maschke’s theorem generalizes readily to the setting of twisted group superalgebras.

Lemma 1.63

(Ganter–Kapranov
[
133
, Example 3.5.1]
)
.

For all finite fermionic groups
G_{f}
,
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
and
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\otimes\mathbb{C}
are semisimple.

Proposition 1.64
.

The functor
G_{f}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
from
\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}^{\mathrm{finite}}
to the category
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{k}^{1}
of superalgebras and super algebra homomorphisms is symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

A fermionic group homomorphism
\phi\colon G_{f}\to H_{f}
functorially induces a grading-preserving algebra homomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\to\mathbb{R}^{f}[H_{f}]
since
\phi((-1)^{F}_{G})=(-1)^{F}_{H}
.
The nontrivial assertion is therefore the symmetric monoidality.
There is a unique linear map

(1.65)

\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathbb{R}^{f}[H_{f}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}H_{f}]

extending the assignment
g\otimes h\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}g\mathbin{\hat{\times}}h
.
This map is well-defined since
g(-1)^{F}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}h
and
g\mathbin{\hat{\times}}(-1)^{F}h
both get sent to
-g\otimes h
.
By dimension counting the linear map is an isomorphism.
It is grading-preserving by definition of the grading
\theta_{G}+\theta_{H}
on the fermionic tensor product and the graded tensor product of vector spaces.
It follows directly from the definition of the fermionic tensor product that we have defined an algebra map.
The fact that the functor is braided is immediate for the same reason.
∎

Remark 1.66
.

It follows by
1.46
and
1.64
that there is also a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}\to\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{k}
into the Morita bicategory of superalgebras.
∎

The real Clifford algebras
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
are twisted group algebras of fermionic groups with underlying bosonic groups
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}
.

Corollary 1.67

(Albuquerque–Majid
[
13
]
)
.

With
E_{\ell,k}
as in
1.36
, there is a canonical isomorphism of superalgebras

(1.68)

\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{\ell,k}]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}.

Proof.

This follows by combining
1.51
,
1.58
, and
1.64
.
∎

See also Thiang
[
313
, Remark 6.4]
, Abłamowicz
[
4
]
, and Ichikawa–Tachikawa
[
171
, Lemma 2.7]
.

Explicitly,
E_{\ell,k}
is the extraspecial
2
-group

(1.69)

\displaystyle\langle e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell+k},(-1)^{F}

\displaystyle:e_{1}^{2}=\dotsb=e_{\ell}^{2}=1,e_{\ell+1}^{2}=\dotsb=e_{\ell+k}^{2}=(-1)^{F},

\displaystyle e_{i}e_{j}=(-1)^{F}e_{j}e_{i},~i\neq j,(-1)^{F}e_{i}=e_{i}(-1)^{F},((-1)^{F})^{2}=1\rangle,

isomorphic to the subset of
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
generated by the set
\{-1,e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell+k}\}
as a group under multiplication. The grading
\theta
agrees with the grading on
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
.

Remark 1.70
.

Recall from
1.34
that the interesting
E_{\infty}
-structure on
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
can be interpreted algebraically using the Picard
1
-groupoid of real superlines.
The maximal Picard
2
-groupoid
\Pic(\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{\mathbb{R}})
of the symmetric monoidal Morita bicategory of real superalgebras
\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{\mathbb{R}}
also inherits an
E_{\infty}
-structure. As spaces, but again not as
E_{\infty}
-spaces,
\pi_{\leq 2}\Pic(\mathit{KO})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/8\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2

[
48
, Theorem 5.27]
.
Here
\mathbb{Z}/8\cong\pi_{0}\Pic(\mathit{s\mathscr{A}lg}_{\mathbb{R}})
is the super Brauer group (also called Brauer–Wall group) of the real numbers
[
325
]
.
The connected component of the basepoint of this space is equivalent as
E_{\infty}
-spaces to
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}
, corresponding to the fact that the automorphisms of the trivial algebra
\mathbb{R}
are given by
\mathit{s\mathscr{L}ine}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
See
[
135
, Section 5.2]
for a general definition of the Brauer spectrum over a ring.
∎

Remark 1.71
.

Using the diagonal map
X\to X\times X
and the
E_{\infty}
-space
\pi_{\leq 2}\Pic(\mathit{KO})
, we obtain an
E_{\infty}
-structure on the set of maps
X\to\mathbb{Z}/8\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Beardsley–Luecke–Morava
[
48
]
show that induced group structure on

(1.72)

\mathbb{Z}/8\times H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2),

which is not in general the direct product structure,
is naturally isomorphic to the (real) graded Brauer group of the space
X
introduced in Donovan–Karoubi
[
94
]
.
∎

1.4.
The
K
-theory of fermionic group algebras

The classification of free fermion phases protected by symmetry modeled by a fermionic group
G_{f}
is in terms of the
K
-theory of its fermionic group algebra.
In the case that
G_{f}
is
finite
, this
K
-theory is given by the
Clifford module quotient group

\mathrm{CMQ}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}])
.

Let
A
be a real superalgebra and let
\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A}
be the category of finitely generated projective graded modules over
A
.
The set
\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A})
of isomorphism classes is a monoid under
\oplus
.
Following the ideas of
[
29
]
, we define

Definition 1.73
.

The
Clifford module quotient
group of
A
is the quotient of monoids

(1.74)

\mathrm{CMQ}_{0}(A)\coloneqq\frac{\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A})}{\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{-1}})}.

More generally,
\mathrm{CMQ}_{n}(A)
is defined as
\mathrm{CMQ}_{0}(Cl_{-n}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}A)
.

Even though
\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A})
is not a group, it can be shown that
\mathrm{CMQ}_{n}(A)
is a group.

Remark 1.75
.

For infinite-dimensional trivially graded algebras
A
,
1.73

agrees with
K
-theory
in degree zero.
Indeed, in that case a graded
A
-module is nothing but a pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
of
A
-modules.
It extends to a graded
A\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{-1}
-module if and only if
M_{1}\cong M_{2}
.
Therefore the map

(1.76)

(M_{1},M_{2})\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}M_{1}-M_{2}

defines an isomorphism from
\mathrm{CMQ}_{0}(A)
to the Grothendieck group of the monoid
\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A}^{ug})
of ungraded modules.
∎

Remark 1.77
.

If
A
is a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-superalgebra, then
\mathrm{CMQ}_{n}(A)
models the
K
-theory of
A
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
However, for infinite-dimensional algebras this is not the case.
For example, if
A=C(S^{1},\mathbb{C})
with trivial grading, a short computation shows that
\mathrm{CMQ}_{1}(A)=0
; see
[
292
, Example 3.61]
.
On the other hand, we have

(1.78)

K_{1}(C(S^{1}))\cong\mathit{KU}^{1}(S^{1})\cong\mathit{KU}^{1}(\mathrm{pt})\oplus\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{1}(S^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z},

where
K_{n}(A)
denotes
K
-theory of the (ungraded)
C^{*}
-algebra
A
in degree
n
.
∎

Remark 1.79
.

If
A
is finite-dimensional and semisimple,
\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{A}
is the category of finite-dimensional graded modules over
A
.
Indeed, finitely generated modules are exactly the finite-dimensional modules. These are automatically projective because
A
is semisimple.
∎

Remark 1.80
.

Another convention sometimes seen in the literature is to quotient by graded
A
-modules that extend to
\mathit{C\ell}_{+1}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}A
-modules.
These conventions are related by taking the opposite superalgebra.
∎

We will mainly be interested in the case where the algebra is a real Clifford algebra
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
or a complex Clifford algebra
\mathbb{C}\ell_{k}
.
It follows from
[
29
, Theorem 11.5]
that

(1.81)

K_{p}(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k})\cong\mathit{KO}_{p-\ell+k}(\mathrm{pt})\quad\text{and}\quad K_{p}(\mathbb{C}\ell_{k})\cong\mathit{KU}_{p-k}(\mathrm{pt}).

Our focus in this paper is on the fermionic group algebras of the fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
, whose
K
-theory reduces to the non-equivariant setting via
Equation

1.81
.
However,
we would like to make a connection between the
K
-theory of finite fermionic group algebras and other definitions of equivariant
K
-theory to offer another perspective and illustrate the more general usage.

Remark 1.82
.

Let
(G_{f},\theta,\omega)
be a finite fermionic group. (In particular, this means that
G_{b}
is a finite group.)
We will restrict to the case where the twist arises from an orthogonal
G_{b}
-representation
V
of rank
r_{V}
, meaning that
w_{1}(V)=\theta\in H_{G_{b}}^{1}(\mathrm{pt};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong H^{1}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
w_{2}(V)=\theta\in H_{G_{b}}^{2}(\mathrm{pt};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong H^{2}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Let
S^{V}
denote the one-point compactification of
V
as a
G_{b}
-space, and let
\mathit{KO}_{G_{b}}
denote the
G_{b}
-equivariant
K
-theory functor. Then there is a group isomorphism

(1.83)

K_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{0,p})\cong\mathit{KO}_{G_{b}}^{-p}(S^{V-r_{V}})=\mathit{KO}_{G_{b}}^{-p-V+r_{V}}(\mathrm{pt}).

There is a similar statement in the complex case.

See e.g.
[
189
]
for further discussion and Gomi
[
139
, Theorem 3.19]
for a proof. In this paper, we only consider the case where
G_{b}
is an elementary abelian
2
-group. In this case, all twists can be realized by orthogonal
G_{b}
-representations and the corresponding twisted equivariant
\mathit{KU}
- and
\mathit{KO}
-cohomology groups of a point have been computed by Hu–Kriz
[
169
]
and Balderrama
[
39
]
(see also Karoubi
[
190
]
), allowing for a proof of (
1.83
) for these
G_{b}
by direct comparison.
∎

Example 1.84
.

As a special case of the previous remark, take the finite fermionic group to be
G_{f}=E_{1,0}
.
Then the twist is realized by the orthogonal
G_{b}=\mathbb{Z}/2
-representation
V=\sigma
(see
1.58
).
Using the isomorphism of superalgebras
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{1}
from (
1.60
) and the periodicity of
\mathit{KO}
-theory with respect to tensoring with Clifford algebras, we get

(1.85)

\mathit{KO}^{-p+1}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathit{KO}_{p-1}(\mathbb{R})\cong K_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,0}]\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{0,p})\cong\mathit{KO}^{-p}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}(S^{\sigma-1})\cong\mathit{KO}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}^{-p-\sigma+1}(\mathrm{pt}).\qed

The next result follows from
1.67
and (
1.81
):

Proposition 1.86
.

If
G_{f}=E_{\ell,k}
, then

(1.87)

K_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{\ell,k}])\cong K_{p}(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k})\cong\mathit{KO}_{p-\ell+k}(*).

This can be upgraded to an equivalence of spectra.

Following
1.86
,
(1,1)
-periodicity
[
29
]
takes the form

(1.88)

K_{p}(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k})\cong K_{p}(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell-1,k-1}).

The following example is a special case:

Example 1.89
.

Consider the fermionic group
E_{1,1}
generated by
e
,
f
, and
(-1)^{F}
with

(1.90)

e^{2}=1,~f^{2}=(-1)^{F},~((-1)^{F})^{2}=1,~ef=(-1)^{F}fe,~e(-1)^{F}=(-1)^{F}e,~f(-1)^{F}=(-1)^{F}f.

This group is abstractly isomorphic to the symmetry group of a square with rotation
f
and reflection
e
, but as a fermionic group
e
and
f
are time-reversing, so the fermionic group algebra is
\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,1}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{1,1}\cong M_{1|1}(\mathbb{R})
, the algebra of
2\times 2
real matrices with off-diagonal matrices in odd grading. This algebra is Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
and thus
K_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,1}])\cong K_{p}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathit{KO}_{p}(*)
, consistent with
1.86
.
∎

To accommodate the complex symmetry classes, we will also consider the
K
-theory of the complexified fermionic group algebra,
K(\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
.
In particular, for
G_{f}=E_{\ell,k}
we obtain the
K
-theory of complex Clifford algebras

(1.91)

K(\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{\ell,k}]\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})=K(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})\cong\mathit{KU}_{k-\ell}(\mathrm{pt}),

by
Equation

1.81
.
Over
\mathbb{C}
,
K
-theory becomes
2
-periodic, related to the fact that

(1.92)

\mathit{C\ell}_{1,0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathit{C\ell}_{2,0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\End(\mathbb{C}^{1|1}).

There is an analog of tensoring with the complex numbers at the fermionic group level, by extending the group
\mathbb{Z}/2
of norm one scalars in
\mathbb{R}
to the group
\mathrm{U}_{1}
of norm one scalars in
\mathbb{C}
.
Namely, given a fermionic group
G_{f}
, we can consider
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
, where for
\mathrm{U}_{1}=\mathrm{Spin}_{2}
we take
\theta
to be trivial and
(-1)^{F}=-1
nontrivial.
The group
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
then fits into a short exact sequence

(1.93)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{U}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1.}}

Central extensions of
G_{b}
by
\mathrm{U}_{1}
are classified by
H^{3}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z})
; under this classification, (
1.93
) corresponds to the Bockstein of

\omega\in H^{2}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

One can make analogs of
1.1
and
1.53
in the complex case. The details are similar, so we will be brief.

Definition 1.94

(
[
313
, §5.2]
)
.

Let
\theta\colon K\to\mathbb{Z}/2
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded Lie group with a distinguished central subgroup isomorphic to
\mathrm{U}_{1}
.
The
complex fermionic group algebra

\mathbb{C}^{f}[K]
is the quotient of the group algebra of
K
where we identify the
\mathrm{U}_{1}\subseteq K
with the
\mathrm{U}_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
in the scalars of the algebra.
We consider it as a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded algebra under
\theta
.

Thiang’s definition in
loc. cit.
is more general than what appears here.

For the special case
Equation

1.93
,
1.94
specializes to
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 1.95
.

Physically, we can think of
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
as “
G_{f}
plus an additional
\mathrm{U}_{1}
-charge
Q
.”
Even though we will not discuss fermionic group
C^{*}
-algebras of infinite fermionic groups here, one can think of modules over
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
as “representations of
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
of unit charge.” See
[
295
, Section 5.3]
for further discussion.
∎

Remark 1.96
.

Even more generally, it can be physically relevant to look at non-central extensions, where
\theta
does not need to be induced by a homomorphism
\phi\colon K\to\mathbb{Z}/2
satisfying
zk=kz^{\phi(h)}
for all
k\in H_{f}
and
z\in\mathrm{U}_{1}\subseteq K
.
In more physical language, these groups can contain symmetries that anti-commute with the charge operator.
Such symmetries are called
charge conjugation operators
in high energy physics and are related to particle-hole symmetries in condensed matter
[
349
]
.
Freed–Moore
[
121
]
apply this group-theoretic structure to SPT phases, where they call it an
extended QM symmetry class
.
∎

1.5.
Fermionic group twists of spin bordism

The data of a fermionic group
G_{f}
also defines a variant of the notion of spin structure. This variant is the tangential structure present on spacetime in a fermionic field theory with
G_{f}
-symmetry. We first go over the generalities, then specialize to several examples that will be helpful in our analysis of the Bott spiral.

Definition 1.97
.

Let
X
be a space,
\theta\in H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and
\omega\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. An
(X,\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin structure
on a vector bundle
V\to M
is data of a map
f\colon M\to X
and trivializations of the classes
w_{1}(V)-f^{*}(\theta)
and
w_{2}(V)+w_{1}(V)^{2}-f^{*}(\omega)
. Two
(X,\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin structures are equivalent if they lie in the same path component of such data.

We will consider bordism classes of manifolds equipped with
(X,\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin structures. Bordism groups of such manifolds are modules over the spin bordism ring.
This notion of twisted spin structure was studied by B.L. Wang
[
327
, Definition 8.2]
in the special case
\theta=0
, and is closely related to Kreck’s notion of a normal
1
-smoothing in
[
206
, §2]
.

Example 1.98

(Vector bundle twists)
.

Suppose there is a vector bundle
E\to X
with
w_{1}(E)=\theta
and
w_{2}(E)=\omega
. Then the Whitney sum formula implies an
(X,\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin structure on
V\to M
is equivalent to the data of the map
f\colon M\to X
and a spin structure on
V\oplus f^{*}(E)
. In this case, we will call
(X,\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin bordism a
vector bundle twist
of spin bordism.
We also will also refer to such twists as
(X,V)
-twists.

We learned this notion from Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §4.1]
;
see also
MacAlpine
[
223
]
and Stolz
[
300
, §2.9]
for related but different notions.
∎

Remark 1.99
.

Given
(X,\theta,\omega)
, it is not always possible to find a vector bundle
E\to X
with
w_{1}(E)=\theta
and
w_{2}(E)=\omega
: see the discussion in
[
149
,
264
,
178
,
208
,
290
]
.
This is typically not a problem in practice: all twisted spin structures appearing in
[
64
,
255
,
49
,
150
,
89
,
118
]
can be realized as vector bundle twists, and the same is true for all twists appearing in this paper. See
[
85
,
87
]
for an example of a twisted spin structure that cannot be realized as a vector bundle twist, applied to an anomaly cancellation question in supergravity.
∎

Example 1.100
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group, and, as in
1.2
, let
G_{b}\coloneqq G_{f}/\{\pm 1\}
and
(\theta,\omega)
be the associated twist. Then the data
(BG_{b},\theta,\omega)
defines a notion of twisted spin structure which we call a
G_{f}
-twisted spin structure
.
∎

Thus a
G_{f}
-twisted spin structure is a vector bundle twist if
G_{f}
is in the essential image of
\zeta\colon\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}\to\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
(
1.22
).

Definition 1.101

(Stolz
[
300
, §2.6]
)
.

Given a fermionic group
G_{f}
and an integer
d\geq 0
, define the Lie group

(1.102)

H_{d}(G_{f})\coloneqq(\mathrm{Pin}^{+}_{d}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}G_{f})_{\mathit{ev}},

where “
(\text{--})_{\mathit{ev}}
” means to take
\theta^{-1}(0)
, the even subgroup. The double cover
\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{+}\to\mathrm{O}_{d}
induces a map
H_{d}(G_{f})\to\mathrm{O}_{d}
given by killing the
G_{f}
factor of the fermionic tensor product.

Taking the colimit, we also allow
d=\infty
, defining a topological group
H(G_{f})
with a map to
\mathrm{O}
; apply the classifying space functor and we obtain a tangential structure in the sense of Lashof
[
209
]
, which we call an
H(G_{f})
-structure
.

Proposition 1.103

(Stehouwer
[
293
]
)
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group with associated twist
(\theta,\omega)
. The following are equivalent data on a vector bundle
V\to M
.

(1)

A
(BG_{b},\theta,\omega)
-twisted spin structure.

(2)

An
H(G_{f})
-structure.

Stehouwer does not state the result in exactly this way, but it follows from
[
293
, Proposition 14]
, the definition of the pullback, and the Whitney sum formula.

The maps
H_{d}(G_{f})\to\mathrm{O}_{d}
commute with the inclusions
H_{d}(G_{f})\to H_{d+1}(G_{f})
(induced by
\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Pin}_{d+1}^{+}
) and
\mathrm{O}_{d}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{O}_{d+1}
, so there is a notion of bordism of manifolds with
G_{f}
-twisted spin structures on their tangent bundles. Lashof
[
209
, Theorem C]
shows that the Pontrjagin–Thom map identifies the corresponding bordism groups with the homotopy groups of the Thom spectrum
\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})
.

One can check using the Whitney sum formula that if
M
is a spin manifold and
N
has a
G_{f}
-twisted spin structure, then
M\times N
has a canonical
G_{f}
-twisted spin structure. This refines to define the structure of an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module spectrum on
\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})
, and we obtain a spectrum-level lift of
1.103
.

Proposition 1.104

(Shearing
[
81
, Lemma 10.18]
)
.

Suppose there is a rank-
r
virtual vector bundle
V\to BG_{b}
with
w_{1}(V)=\theta
and
w_{2}(V)=\omega
. Then there is an equivalence of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-modules, natural in the data of
G_{f}
and
V
,

(1.105)

\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(BG_{b})^{V-r}.

Typically the vector bundle
V
is far from unique. Furthermore, the homotopy type of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(BG_{b})^{V-r}
only depends on
w_{1}(V)
and
w_{2}(V)
. See
[
89
, Theorem 1.39]
.

Example 1.106
.

Consider the two fermionic groups
G_{f}=\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm}
. As we showed in
1.58
, for both of these groups,
G_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\theta
is nontrivial, but
\omega=0
for
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
and
\omega=\theta^{2}
for
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
.
[
27
, Proposition 1.26]
constructs isomorphisms
H_{d}(\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm})\cong\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{\mp}
for all
d
including
d=\infty
.

Let
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
denote the tautological line bundle. Then
w_{1}(\sigma)\neq 0
and
w_{2}(\sigma)=0
, so by
1.104
,
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
-twisted spin bordism groups are the homotopy groups of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
. The identification
H(\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+})\cong\mathrm{Pin}^{-}
, together with
1.103
, implies the following equivalence of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-modules, first recorded by Peterson
[
256
, §7]
:

(1.107a)

MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}.

The Whitney sum formula implies that if
V
is either of the virtual bundles
3\sigma
or
-\sigma
, then
w_{1}(V)\neq 0
and
w_{2}(V)=w_{1}(V)^{2}
, so the Stiefel–Whitney classes of
V
match the associated twist of
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
. Thus, following a similar line of reasoning as we did for
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
, we deduce that
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
-twisted spin structures are the homotopy groups of

(1.107b)

MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3\sigma-3}.

This is a theorem of Stolz
[
298
, §8]
.

∎

The most important examples in this paper are the fermionic groups defined in terms of the extraspecial
2
-groups in
1.36
. The following proposition will play an important role.

Proposition 1.108
.

Let
M\colon\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}}\to\mathit{\mathscr{S}pectra}
denote the functor sending a rank-zero virtual vector bundle
V\to X
to its Thom spectrum
X^{V}
. Then the composition

(1.109)

\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}\xrightarrow[\eqref{xi_defn}]{\xi}\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}}\xrightarrow{M}\mathit{\mathscr{S}pectra}\xrightarrow{\wedge MT\mathrm{Spin}}\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}

is a symmetric monoidal functor of
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

Since (
1.109
) comes as a composition of three functors, it suffices to show each of the three is symmetric monoidal. For
\xi
, we did this in
1.32
. For
M
, this is Lewis’ theorem
[
215
, §IX.7.4]
; the
\infty
-categorical version is due to Ando–Blumberg–Gepner
[
24
, Theorem 1.6]
. For the base change
\wedge MT\mathrm{Spin}
, this is in
[
222
, §4.5.3]
given an
E_{\infty}
-ring structure on
MT\mathrm{Spin}
, such as the one constructed by Joachim
[
176
]
.
∎

The upshot is that, for
G_{f}
in the essential image of
\zeta
, we have a description of
\mathit{MTH}(G_{f})
compatible with the fermionic tensor product, strengthening
1.103
and
1.104
. In light of
1.99
, implying that
\zeta
is not essentially surjective, it would be nice to lose the hypothesis of being in its essential image. Thus we make the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.110
.

With notation as in
1.108
,
there is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathit{TW}\colon\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}\to\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}}

(1)

\mathit{TW}
of a fermionic group

(2)

The following diagram of symmetric monoidal functors commutes.

(1.111b)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}^{\mathrm{cpt}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{\mathscr{T}op}_{/B\mathrm{O}}}}

)

(

Here

and

.

Definition 1.112
.

For
k,\ell\geq 0
, recall the fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
from
1.36
and let
16
16

16

The notation
\mathit{ME}
is meant to represent a Thom spectrum (“
M
”) for an elementary abelian group (“
E
”).

(1.113)

\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\coloneqq((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge\ell}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma})^{\wedge k}.

By
1.103
, an
MTH(E_{\ell,k})
-structure on a manifold
M
is equivalent to a spin structure on the virtual bundle
TM+L_{1}+\dots+L_{\ell}-L_{\ell+1}-\dots-L_{\ell+k}
for a collection of line bundles
L_{i}
over
M
.
We will call such a manifold a
spin-
(\ell,k)
manifold
.

In the same way, we define a
spin
c
-
(\ell,k)
-manifold
to be an
H(E_{\ell,k}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{Spin}_{2})
-structure. Building on the equivalence of spin
c
structures on
V
and data of a complex line bundle
E
and a spin structure on
V\oplus E
, one can show that a spin
c
-
(\ell,k)
structure is naturally equivalent to a spin
c
structure on
TM+L_{1}+\dots+L_{\ell}-L_{\ell+1}-\dots-L_{\ell+k}
, where the
L_{i}
are real line bundles as before.

Lemma 1.114
.

There is an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence

(1.115)

\mathit{MTH}(E_{\ell,k})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}.

Proof.

This follows by combining
1.106
and
1.108
.
∎

We elaborate on the case
\ell=k=1
, which we began in
1.89
and which will be a running example throughout this paper.

Definition 1.116

(Kaidi–Parra-Martinez–Tachikawa
[
182
, §6.1]
)
.

There is an isomorphism
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x,y]
with
\lvert x\rvert=\lvert y\rvert=1
specified by asking that, under the canonical isomorphisms

(1.117)

H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Hom(\pi_{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2),\mathbb{Z}/2)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Hom(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2),

x(1,0)=1
,
x(0,1)=0
,
y(1,0)=0
, and
y(0,1)=1
. A
dpin

structure on a vector bundle
V
is a
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2,x,xy)
-twisted spin structure.

Lemma 1.118
.

There is a natural homotopy equivalence between the spaces of dpin structures and of
E_{1,1}
-twisted spin structures on any vector bundle
V
.

Proof.

We have
(E_{1,1})_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. By
1.12
, the associated twist is
(x+y,x^{2}+xy)
, so
E_{1,1}
-twisted spin structures are
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2,x+y,x^{2}+xy)
-twisted spin structures.

There is an automorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
that sends
(1,0)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(0,1)
and
(0,1)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(1,1)
, and whose value on the remaining elements is uniquely specified by the stipulation that
\varphi
is a group homomorphism. It follows from our construction of
x
and
y
that
\varphi^{*}(x)=x+y
and
\varphi^{*}(y)=x
, so if we pull back the twisting data defining a dpin structure along
\varphi
, we get the twisting data of an
E_{1,1}
-twisted spin structure. Thus, precomposing the map to
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
with
B\varphi
or
B(\varphi^{-1})
turns dpin structures into
E_{1,1}
-twisted spin structures and vice versa.
∎

We will discuss spin-
(1,1)
structures and dpin structures interchangeably throughout the rest of this paper, often leaving the automorphism
\varphi
implicit.

Finally, we discuss the complex case.
To define twisted spin
c
bordism associated to a fermionic group, we use the map of spectra
MT\mathrm{Spin}\to MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
, which refines the fact that spin structures induce spin
c
structures.
17
17

17

In terms of obstructions, if
w_{2}=0
, then
W_{3}=\beta(w_{2})=0
as well.
This map gives
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
the structure of an
E_{\infty}
-
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-algebra, guaranteeing that the constructions in this section are natural. The necessary modifications are:

(1)

Exchange
\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{c}
for
\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{+}
in
1.101
.

(2)

Note that the fermionic groups
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm}
both give rise to the same twisted spin
c
structure, namely pin
c
structure.
This is because the Lie groups
\mathrm{Pin}_{d}^{\pm}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
are isomorphic with the isomorphism commuting with the structure map to
\mathrm{O}_{d}
.
Alternatively, one can think about this in terms of group algebras:
\mathbb{R}[\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{\pm 1}
, and the complexifications of these two superalgebras are isomorphic: if
e^{2}=1
, then
(ie)^{2}=-1
. We will see more algebraic consequences of this isomorphism in §
6.3
.

Remark 1.119
.

Under the hood,
there is a more general notion of twisted spin
c
structure defined in terms of a map to
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)\times K(\mathbb{Z},3)
(see
[
125
,
95
,
85
]
), and the twisting data
(\theta,\omega)
associated to a fermionic group only manifests through
\theta
and the integral Bockstein of
\omega
. So different choices of
\omega
with the same Bockstein lead to equivalent notions of twisted spin
c
bordism, as happened for
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{\pm}
.
Up to homotopy,
BG_{b}\to B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
is equivalent to a group homomorphism
\theta\colon G_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
and an isomorphism class of an extension of
G_{b}
by
\mathrm{U}_{1}
; compare with
1.96
.

Similarly to
1.70
, there is a larger
E_{\infty}
-space
\pi_{\leq 3}\Pic(\mathit{KU})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{3}\mathbb{Z}
, and the connected component of the basepoint is equivalent as
E_{\infty}
-spaces to
B\mathrm{O}/B\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
.
Analogously to
1.71
, Beardsley–Luecke–Morava
[
48
]
show that the induced group structure on

(1.120)

\mathbb{Z}/2\times H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z})

is naturally isomorphic to the complex graded Brauer group of
X
as defined by Donovan–Karoubi
[
94
]
.

We also saw in
1.96
that there is a more general notion of twists classified by
H^{1}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H^{3}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}_{\phi})
, where
H^{3}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}_{\phi})
is twisted cohomology for a fixed homomorphism
\phi\colon G_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
.
We expect these to give Real-equivariant twists for the genuine
\mathbb{Z}/2
-equivariant spectrum
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
given by complex conjugation; see also
[
152
]
.
We will not need this level of generality in this paper.
∎

Proposition 1.121
.

Let
R
be one of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
,
\mathit{ku}
, or
\mathit{KU}
. Then there is a canonical
R
-module equivalence
R\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\simeq R\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
.

We are not sure where
1.121
was first proven: it appears to be well-known but not written down. It follows from the fact that
2\sigma
is complex, hence
R
-oriented: see
[
80
, Example 6.13]
.

Definition 1.122
.

Let
\mathit{ME}_{m}\coloneqq\mathit{ME}_{m,0}
.

Corollary 1.123
.

With
R
as in
1.121
, there is a canonical
R
-module isomorphism
R\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+k}
.

2.
Homotopical classifications of SPT phases

In this section, we discuss the classifications of both free and interacting SPTs, in preparation for defining maps between them in
Section

3.5
.
We first introduce free fermion Hamiltonians and their
K
-theoretic classification in
Section

2.1
, then discuss invertible field theories as a model for interacting SPT phases in
Section

2.2
. In
Section

2.3
, we introduce and discuss the fermionic groups we will use to model the ten Altland–Zirnbauer classes.

2.1.
Free fermion phases and
K
-theory

Let
A
be a real
C^{*}
-algebra with a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading. Physically,
A
represents the algebra of (not-necessarily internal) symmetries, and the grading distinguishes time-preserving and time-reversing symmetries.
According to the physics definition, the set of free fermion phases protected by
A
-symmetry are the connected components of the space of free fermion systems—often represented by Hamiltonian operators—with
A
-symmetry.
In
[
295
]
one of us argued for the following ansatz.

Ansatz 2.1

(
[
295
, Definition 3.7]
)
.

The set of free fermionic SPT phases protected by
A
is the set of equivalence classes of triples
[M,H_{1},H_{2}]
, where
M
is a finitely generated
A
-module and
H_{i}\colon M\to M
are operators such that
H_{i}a=(-1)^{|a|}aH_{i}
and
H_{i}^{2}=-1
.

The set of SPT phases is then given an abelian group structure by stacking (see
[
295
, Definition 3.5]
).

Remark 2.2
.

For the interested reader, we quickly run through the motivation for the above ansatz.

(1)

If we model neutral
18
18

18

i.e. phases without charge conservation symmetry.
free fermions in the Bogoliubov–de Gennes framework, a free Hamiltonian
H
is a
skew
-adjoint operator on the real space
M
of Majorana operators. One should think of this operator as
i
times a self adjoint operator acting on
M\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

An
A
-symmetry manifests itself as an action on
M
. Because of the extra factor of
i
in the Hamiltonian, the operator
H
commutes with time-preserving symmetries and anti-commutes with time-reversing symmetries.

(3)

We shift the Fermi energy to zero so that gapped systems correspond to invertible Hamiltonians.

(4)

The space of all gapped Hamiltonians is predicted to be homotopy equivalent to the space of flattened Hamiltonians, which are the Hamiltonians
H
satisfying
H^{2}=-1
.

(5)

We replace individual Hamiltonians by pairs of Hamiltonians, which we think of as formal differences of two systems. Mathematically, this implements the group completion necessary to obtain
K
-theory, while physically this kills fragile phases
[
257
]
.

We refer to
[
295
]
for details.
∎

The following theorem is a generalization and formalization of the arguments in
[
197
]
.
19
19

19

Kitaev’s proposal has been generalized to many settings, using many different formalisms for
K
-theory. See, for example,
[
51
,
160
,
121
,
313
,
192
,
14
,
139
]
.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
295
, Theorem 3.35]
)
.

Assuming
2.1
,
The group of free fermion SPT phases protected by
A
is isomorphic to
K_{2}(A)
.

The general idea of the proof is to observe that the group of SPT phases described in
2.1
is a version of the Karoubi model for the
K
-theory of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras
[
189
]
.

Now, let
G_{f}
be a finite fermionic group and
d
be an integer representing the spatial dimension.
It is argued in
[
295
]
that the corresponding symmetry algebra is given by
A=C_{f}^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times G_{f})
, where
C_{f}^{*}
denotes the fermionic group (
C^{*}
-)algebra.
20
20

20

Because the
group
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times G_{f}
is infinite,
using
\mathbb{R}^{f}[\mathbb{R}^{d}\times G_{f}]
does not suffice: among other things, one has to complete, and that is why
C_{f}^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times G_{f})
appears here.

This allows one to prove a relationship with the
K
-theory of the fermionic group algebra:

Theorem 2.4

(
[
295
, Remark 4.34]
)
.

Free fermion SPT phases with finite fermionic symmetry
G_{f}
are classified by
K_{2-d}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}])
.

Remark 2.5
.

The reason for the choice of algebra is as follows.

(1)

M
should have fermionic symmetry
G_{f}
, i.e. be a module over the fermionic group algebra.

(2)

M
should also have a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
translation symmetry coming from the lattice of atoms, which we can model by an action of the group algebra
C^{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
on
M
.

(3)

If we ignore weak phases—phases protected by the discrete translation symmetry—we should impose
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-symmetry instead of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-symmetry, to allow for continuous translations. See
[
27
]
for more on this perspective on weak phases.

(4)

Strong phases in dimension
d
should thus be symmetric for both
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
G_{f}
, meaning that we have a
C^{*}_{f}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times G_{f})
-action.
∎

When
G_{f}=E_{\ell,k}
we obtain the following
8
-periodic expression by using
1.86
.

Corollary 2.6
.

Free fermion SPT phases protected by
E_{\ell,k}
-symmetry in spatial dimension
d
are classified by
\mathit{KO}_{2-d-\ell+k}(\mathrm{pt})
.

The
8
-periodicity above is related to the
8
real symmetry classes in the tenfold way.
We provide more details on how we model symmetry types in
Section

2.3
, but give a few examples now:

Example 2.7

(Class D)
.

The case of no symmetry,
G_{f}=E_{0,0}
, models class D systems.
For example, for
d=2
, it is predicted (see Kitaev
[
197
]
) that the
p+ip
topological superconductor represents a generator of
\mathit{KO}_{2-d}(\mathrm{pt})=\mathit{KO}_{0}(\mathrm{pt})=\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Example 2.8

(Class BDI)
.

The symmetry algebra
G_{f}=E_{1,0}
consists of fermion parity
(-1)^{F}
and a single time-reversal symmetry
T
with
T^{2}=1
.
For
d=1
, the time-reversal-symmetric Majorana chain
[
196
]
is believed to represent a generator of
\mathit{KO}_{2-1-d}(\mathrm{pt})=\mathit{KO}_{0}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
Any number of stacked copies of the chain is not adiabatically connected to the trivial phase via symmetric, quadratic terms
[
111
]
.
∎

We will deal with the remaining two complex symmetry classes by using the complex version of fermionic group algebras given in
1.94
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose that
G_{f}
is a fermionic group and
\mathrm{U}_{1}\subseteq G_{f}
is a central subgroup such that
G_{f}/\mathrm{U}_{1}
is finite.
Free (charged) fermion SPT phases with fermionic symmetry
G_{f}
are classified by
K_{2-d}(\mathbb{C}^{f}[G_{f}])
.
For
G_{f}=E_{\ell,k}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
this gives the
2
-periodic expression
\mathit{KU}_{2-d-\ell+k}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathit{KU}_{d+\ell+k}(\mathrm{pt})
.

In the above theorem, the
\mathrm{U}_{1}
subgroup should be interpreted as electric charge.

Example 2.10

(Class A)
.

For class A, we have that
G_{f}=E_{0,0}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}=\mathrm{U}_{1}
is charge conservation. The quantum Hall insulator is predicted to represent a generator for
d=2
of
K_{2-d}(\mathbb{C}^{f}[G_{f}])=\mathit{KU}_{0}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathbb{Z}

[
197
]
.
∎

The
K
-groups of a
C^{*}
-superalgebra
A
are Morita invariant, so
2.3
has the consequence that the classification of free fermion phases with symmetry given by a fermionic group
G_{f}
depend only weakly on
G_{f}
through the Morita class of
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
. See
[
293
, §2.3]
and
[
295
, Remark 3.34]
. Work of Müssnich–Vasconcellos Vieira
[
248
]
suggests this Morita invariance phenomenon extends into the weakly interacting regime as well.

For example, by
1.67
,
\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{\ell,k}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
, which is Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime}}]
if and only if
\ell-k\equiv\ell^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\bmod 8
. This gives many examples of nonisomorphic fermionic groups whose groups of free fermion phases in every dimension are isomorphic. Later, in
6.48
, we will prove that the classifications of reflection-positive IFTs for these fermionic groups are in general nonisomorphic.

As we discussed in the introduction, this is an important concern when describing Altland–Zirnbauer classes, which behave more like Morita equivalence classes than isomorphism classes of fermionic groups or superalgebras.

2.2.
Interacting SPT phases and invertible field theories

In the physics literature, the codomain of a free-to-interacting map is the space of all gapped invertible topological phases of matter of a given dimension and symmetry type. Providing a rigorous mathematical construction of this space is a difficult open question, but it is conjectured that
gapped invertible phases are classified by their low energy limits, which are invertible field theories (IFTs).

Ansatz 2.11

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
]
)
.

Interacting SPT phases of a given symmetry type are classified by
deformation classes of reflection-positive, fully extended invertible field theories whose tangential structure corresponds to the symmetry type of the SPTs.

In this paper, we will take this low-energy approximation ansatz as given.
See
[
128
,
118
]
for further discussion on this ansatz and
[
118
,
64
,
186
,
49
,
329
,
132
,
116
,
1
,
89
,
40
,
27
]
for supporting computational evidence.
We view our results in this work, and our ongoing program of constructing homotopical free-to-interacting maps, as further tests of the ansatz.

By results of Freed–Hopkins
[
118
]
and Grady
[
142
]
, reflection-positive IFTs are classified in terms of homotopy theory. So, once we admit the ansatz, we have access to computational methods.
In the rest of this subsection, we give some details on the classification.

Let
\xi\colon B\to B\mathrm{O}
be a stable tangential structure.
Recall that an (
n
-dimensional,
\xi
-structured) topological field theory is a symmetric monoidal functor

(2.12)

\alpha\colon\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}\longrightarrow\mathit{\mathscr{C}},

where
\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}
is the bordism category of
n
-manifolds with
\xi
-structure, symmetric monoidal under disjoint union, and
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
is some symmetric monoidal category, often
\mathit{s\mathscr{V}ect}_{\mathbb{C}}
. One may also take
\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}
and
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
to be symmetric monoidal higher categories. Given two TFTs
\alpha
and
\beta
, their tensor product
\alpha\otimes\beta
is defined by the formula

(2.13)

(\alpha\otimes\beta)(X)\coloneqq\alpha(X)\otimes\beta(X)

for
X
an object, morphism, or higher morphism.

Definition 2.14

(Freed–Moore
[
120
, Definition 5.7]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{1}\colon\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}\to\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
denote the constant functor valued at the monoidal unit of
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
and the identity morphism. A TFT
\alpha
is
invertible
if there is another TFT
\beta
such that
\alpha\otimes\beta\simeq\boldsymbol{1}
.

Equivalently, the image of
\alpha
is contained in
\otimes
-invertible objects and composition-invertible (higher) morphisms.
For example, if
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}=\mathit{s\mathscr{V}ect}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the tensor invertible objects are the one-dimensional vector spaces.

Freed–Hopkins–Teleman
[
113
]
and Schommer-Pries
[
279
]
classify invertible TFTs in terms of the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum
\Sigma^{n}\mathit{MT\xi}_{n}
,
21
21

21

Rovi–Schoenbauer
[
271
]
and Kreck–Stolz–Teichner (unpublished; see
[
301
]
) have also proven related classification theorems for invertible TFTs that are not stated in terms of Madsen–Tillmann spectra.
where
\xi_{n}\colon B_{n}\to B\mathrm{O}_{n}
is the unstable tangential structure associated to
\xi
.
For applications to unitary quantum field theory, we
will want slightly more structure: a
reflection-positive
IFT,
22
22

22

Reflection positivity appears because it is the Wick-rotated version of unitarity. See
[
118
, §3]
.
defined for invertible topological field theories by Freed–Hopkins
[
118
]
and in the invertible, non-topological setting by Grady–Pavlov
[
141
, §5]
(see also
[
140
,
143
]
).
23
23

23

For unitarity and/or reflection positivity in the topological, noninvertible setting, see
[
180
,
247
,
107
,
66
,
294
]
.

To state the classification of reflection-positive IFTs, we introduce a few more spectra.

Definition 2.15

(Brown–Comenetz
[
56
]
)
.

Let
A
be an injective abelian group. The
Brown–Comenetz dual of the sphere (over
A
)
, written
I_{A}
, is defined to represent
the generalized cohomology theory

(2.16)

(I_{A})^{n}(X)\coloneqq\Hom(\pi_{n}(X),A).

In the homotopy theory literature,
A
is most often taken to be
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, following Brown–Comenetz. In the mathematical physics literature,
A=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is more common. If
A
is a field of
characteristic
0
, there is a weak equivalence
I_{A}\xrightarrow{\simeq}HA
.

Definition 2.17

(Anderson
[
20
]
)
.

The
Anderson dual of the sphere
, denoted
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
, is the fiber of the map
H\mathbb{C}\simeq I_{\mathbb{C}}\to I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
induced by
the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
24
24

24

Some authors define
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
in a different but equivalent way, e.g. using another characteristic zero field in place of
\mathbb{C}
. The resulting spectra are all canonically equivalent to
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
as defined in
2.17
.

Using this definition, one can show that
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the following universal property: for any spectrum
X
, there is a natural short exact sequence

(2.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Ext(\pi_{n-1}(X),\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X,\Sigma^{n}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom(\pi_{n}(X),\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0,}}

and this characterizes
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
up to homotopy equivalence. Equation (
2.18
) splits, but not naturally, inducing a
(non-canonical) isomorphism from
[X,\Sigma^{n}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
to the direct sum of the torsion summand of
\pi_{n-1}(X)
and the
free summand of
\pi_{n}(X)
.

Now we return to the classification of reflection-positive IFTs.

Theorem 2.19

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Theorem 1.1]
, Grady
[
142
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group.
There is an isomorphism, natural in
G_{f}
, from the abelian group of deformation classes of
n
-dimensional reflection-positive IFTs on manifolds with
H(G_{f})
-structure to
[\mathit{MTH}(G_{f}),\Sigma^{n+1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
.

Remark 2.20
.

2.19
holds in more generality. Indeed, for
any
tangential structure
\xi\colon B\to B\mathrm{O}
, using the construction in
[
107
, §7]
one can define a notion of reflection-positive
\xi
-structured IFT analogous to Freed–Hopkins’ definition
[
118
, §8]
, and Lukas Müller observed that Freed–Hopkins’ proof immediately generalizes to this setting.

Though we do not need this extra generality in this paper, it is useful for applications of the general version of
2.19
to string theory, such as those in
[
117
,
309
,
337
,
93
,
305
,
306
,
42
,
44
,
90
,
86
,
179
,
308
,
43
,
183
,
275
,
92
]
, which study reflection-positive IFTs on manifolds with various notions of twisted string structure.
∎

Notation 2.21
.

We will often denote the group
[\mathit{MT\xi},\Sigma^{n+1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
by
\mho_{\xi}^{n}
.
The IFTs classified by this group have partition functions defined on
n
-manifolds, where
n
corresponds to the spacetime dimension of the physical theory.
We will often prefer to focus instead on the spatial dimension, which we indicate by
d
; these are related by
n=d+1
.

As we discussed in
Section

1.5
, for a fermionic theory with a symmetry given by a fermionic group
G_{f}
, the tangential structure of the corresponding IFTs is the structure
H(G_{f})
constructed in
[
300
, §2.6]
(here
1.101
).

Remark 2.22
.

The classification of topological field theories
Z\colon\mathit{\mathscr{B}ord}_{n}^{\xi}\to\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
depends on the choice of codomain
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
, and this remains true when restricting to invertible TFTs. Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §5.3]
argue that
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the universal target for classifying deformation classes of invertible IFTs, which is why it appears as the codomain in
2.19
. See also
[
128
, §§6.8, 6.9, 9.4]
, as well as
[
126
,
82
,
123
]
for constructions of example codomain (
d+1
)-categories
\mathit{\mathscr{C}}
in dimensions
d\leq 2
justifying this choice.
∎

Example 2.23

(2d pin
-
IFTs)
.

Let
G_{f}=\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}
, so that the spacetime tangential structure is pin
-
, as we established in
1.106
. To classify reflection-positive pin
-
IFTs, by
2.19
we compute
[MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-},\Sigma^{n+1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
, which by (
2.18
) can be deduced from
\pi_{*}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-})
, the bordism groups of manifolds with pin
-
structures on their tangent bundles.

Now we specialize to
n=2
. Anderson–Brown–Peterson
[
17
, Theorem 5.1]
showed
\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8
and
\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}
is torsion,
25
25

25

The result in
[
17
]
is quite general; see
[
84
, Footnote 6]
for how to extract the case
n=2
, as well as Giambalvo
[
136
, Theorem 3.4(b)]
, Kirby–Taylor
[
195
, Lemma 3.6]
, and Campbell
[
64
, Theorem 6.4]
for additional calculations of
\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8
.

so the group of 2d pin
-
reflection-positive IFTs is also isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/8
.
The field theory
\alpha_{\text{ABK}}
whose partition is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant
[
57
,
195
]
is a generator of
\Hom(\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\cong\mathbb{Z}/8

[
82
, §5]
.
This IFT and its role as the low-energy field theory of the time-reversal symmetric Majorana chain of
2.8
is discussed in detail in
[
82
]
;
see also
[
296
,
322
,
247
]
for more general results on 2d pin
-
TFTs.
∎

2.3.
Fermionic groups and symmetry types

We have seen so far that fermionic groups are useful for describing the symmetries of SPT phases; indeed, their definition is very close to descriptions in the physics literature.
In this section, we construct the fermionic groups we will use to model the tenfold way in the rest of the paper.
However, there is a major subtlety: we need to distinguish between a discrete and a continuous version.

Mathematically, there are two natural families of ten fermionic groups to consider: the
discrete tenfold way
given (except for class C) by certain
E_{\ell,k}
groups, and the
continuous tenfold way
,
which gives rise to the internal symmetry groups
used in
[
118
]
.
These groups can each be
constructed from the ten real superdivision algebras as follows.

By the classification of super division algebras
A
, we know that if
A
is not the quaternions, then it is a Clifford algebra on the vector space
\mathbb{F}^{\ell+k}
with bilinear form of signature
(\ell,k)
, where
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
.
For our application,
not all values of
\ell
and
k
can occur, but we will allow
\ell=k=0
.
We will first discuss the Clifford algebra case, returning to the case
A=\mathbb{H}
momentarily.

Definition 2.24

(Discrete and Continuous Forms)
.

For both of the following cases, the fermionic group structure is given by
(-1)^{F}=-1
and
\theta
coming from the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading of the Clifford algebra.

•

Pick an orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell+k}\}
of the underlying vector space
\mathbb{F}^{\ell+k}
.
The subgroup of
(\mathbb{F}^{\ell+k})^{\times}
generated by the basis
\mathcal{B}
together with the norm-
1
scalars is called the
discrete form

of the fermionic group in this symmetry class.

•

The subgroup of
(\mathbb{F}^{\ell+k})^{\times}
generated by
all
homogeneous norm-
1
vectors
of the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded vector space
\mathbb{F}^{\ell}\oplus\mathbb{F}^{k}
is the group
\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}^{\mathbb{F}}
, which we call the
continuous form
.

Observe that the discrete form is always contained in the continuous form.
In the seven relevant real cases, for which we will we take

(2.25)

(\ell,k)=(0,0),(0,1),(0,2),(0,3),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0),

this procedure gives the fermionic groups
E_{\ell,k}\subseteq\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell,k}
, while in the two complex cases it gives
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Pin}^{c}_{1}
.
Finally, for the
remaining
exceptional case
A=\mathbb{H}
, we take the quaternion subgroup
Q_{8}\subseteq\mathrm{SU}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
generated by
i,j
and
k
and equip it with the fermionic group structure

(-1)^{F}=-1
and
\theta=0
, to get the discrete form, and likewise choose
\mathrm{SU}_{2}
with the same
\theta
and
(-1)^{F}
for the continuous form.

We refer the reader to
Table

1
for a translation to the usual Altland–Zirnbauer class notation.
Additionally, we clarify the relationship with Freed–Hopkins
[
118
]
, whose symmetry types depend on a parameter
s
.
In all cases in the table, the parameter
s
and the choice of
\ell,k
in
E_{\ell,k}
are related by
s=\ell-k
.
Also, the super division algebra
A
is the fermionic group algebra of the
relevant
discrete form fermionic group.

For the classes D, BDI, DIII, A, and AIII, the discrete and continuous forms are equal.
For the remaining 5 real classes (AI, AII, C, CI, CII),
the inclusion
E_{\ell,0}\subset\mathrm{Pin}_{\ell}^{+}
(resp.
E_{0,k}\subset\mathrm{Pin}_{k}^{-}
) is no longer an isomorphism for
\ell>1
(resp.
k>1
), and likewise
Q_{8}\subset\mathrm{SU}_{2}
is not an isomorphism.

Example 2.26

(class AI)
.

Consider the case
A=\mathit{C\ell}_{2}
, corresponding to class AI.
The discrete class AI symmetry group
E_{2,0}
consists of two anticommuting time-reversal symmetries
T_{1},T_{2}
such that
T_{1}^{2}=T_{2}^{2}=1
.
Setting
g\coloneqq T_{1}T_{2}
, this is equivalent to a single time-preserving symmetry with
g^{2}=(-1)^{F}
and a single anticommuting time-reversal symmetry
T_{1}
.
It is also physically reasonable to interpret the unitary symmetry
g=iQ
as charge and to enlarge the symmetry group “generated by”
g
to a whole continuous
\mathrm{U}_{1}
.
The total resulting internal symmetry group is
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}^{+}
, the continuous class AI symmetry group.
∎

The distinction between continuous and discrete symmetry classes is important.
In general, the discrete and continuous fermionic groups for a given Altland–Zirnbauer class give rise to completely different twisted spin bordism groups, and hence also completely different groups of IFTs.

Theorem 2.27
.

In Altland–Zirnbauer classes AI, AII, C, CI, and CII, the twisted spin bordism groups associated to the discrete and continuous fermionic groups in
Table

1
are not isomorphic. For classes AI, AII, and C, they are not even rationally isomorphic.

In the remaining five classes (A, AIII, BDI, D, DIII), the discrete and continuous fermionic groups are equal, as we discussed above. Nevertheless, even in these cases there are other physically motivated choices for a fermionic group representing a given symmetry class giving rise to nonisomorphic twisted spin bordism groups: see
[
293
, §4]
for an example in class D.

Proof of
2.27
.

We compute the twisted spin bordism groups corresponding to our discrete fermionic groups in
5.55
, modulo certain
\mathbb{Z}/2
summands (see
5.10
), except for class C; we do the class C computation in
6.102
(see (
6.104
)).
26
26

26

In this proof, we only need
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}
, which was computed earlier by Pedrotti
[
255
, Theorem 8.0.8]
.

Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9.3]
compute the twisted spin bordism groups corresponding to the continuous fermionic groups in degrees
4
and below, and using their Adams charts (
ibid.
, Figure 5) and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting
[
18
]
(reviewed in
6.1
), one can extend these computations to degrees
\leq 11
. To finish the proof, directly compare the results of these two computations. Explicitly, there are nonnegative integers
w_{\mathrm{AI}}
,
w_{\mathrm{AII}}
,
w_{\mathrm{CI}}
and
w_{\mathrm{CII}}
such that
27
27

27

One can show that
w_{\mathrm{AI}}=2
,
w_{\mathrm{AII}}=1
,
w_{\mathrm{CI}}=8
, and
w_{\mathrm{CII}}=6
by explicitly working out the Adams spectral sequences for these four cases. We do not need the specific numbers, so do not go into detail.

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}-}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{E_{2,0}}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus w_{\mathrm{AI}}}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{E_{0,2}}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus w_{\mathrm{AII}}}

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{h}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{8}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/32\oplus\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\Omega_{8}^{E_{3,0}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/32\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus w_{\mathrm{CI}}}

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/16\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{E_{0,3}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/16\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus w_{\mathrm{CII}}},

corresponding to classes AI, AII, C, CI, and CII, respectively. ∎

For our physical application, discrete symmetry types suffice, simplify computations, and in fact illuminate a version of Bott periodicity, as we discuss further in
Section

.

Table 1
.

Here we list symmetry types corresponding to the tenfold way of fermionic insulators and superconductors. The first subtable lists the complex classes, and the second subtable lists real classes.
In the complex cases, discrete and continuous symmetry types agree, so we make no distinction.
In the real cases, we list the two versions separately.
In all cases, the continuous classes agree with those studied by Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Tables (9.24), (9.25)]
, and for these we include their notation with the parameter
s
and symmetry type
H(s)
for comparison. See also
[
293
, Table 1]
.

The fermionic group algebra
\mathbb{R}^{f}[G_{f}]
of the discrete fermionic group
G_{f}
is used to compute free SPT phases (see
Section

2.1
) in the relevant class.
The corresponding twisted spin structure groups
H(G_{f})
and
H(K_{f})
are used to compute interacting SPT phases (see
Section

2.2
). In physics language,
K_{f}
and
G_{f}
are internal symmetry groups, and
H(\text{--})
defines the tangential structure present on spacetime.

The fermionic group
Q_{8}
is an outlier, as it is is not isomorphic as a fermionic group
28
28

28

As groups,
Q_{8}
is isomorphic to
E_{0,2}
, but this isomorphism does not preserve
\theta
;
Q_{8}
does not have any time-reversing symmetries.
to one of the form
E_{\ell,k}
.
However, it is related to the groups
E_{\ell,k}
in the following way:

Lemma 2.29
.

There are isomorphisms
s_{+}\colon E_{0,3}\overset{\cong}{\to}Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,0}
and
s_{-}\colon E_{3,0}\overset{\cong}{\to}Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
be the canonical odd generators of
E_{0,3}
and
f
the generator of
E_{1,0}
.
We define a map
Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,0}\to E_{0,3}
on generators as follows:

(2.30)

\displaystyle i\mathbin{\hat{\times}}1

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}f_{1}f_{2},\qquad j\mathbin{\hat{\times}}1\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}f_{1}f_{3},\qquad k\mathbin{\hat{\times}}1\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}f_{2}f_{3},

\displaystyle 1\mathbin{\hat{\times}}f

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}.

Note that
f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}
is odd and the
f_{i}f_{j}
are even.
A direct computation shows that the
f_{i}f_{j}
satisfy the quaternion group relations.
It follows that
(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3})^{2}=f_{1}^{2}(f_{2}f_{3})^{2}=1
.
One also checks that
f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}
commutes with
f_{i}f_{j}
as is required from the fermionic tensor product.
Therefore, this map is a well-defined group homomorphism.
By checking surjectivity and comparing cardinalities, one can verify it is a bijection.
Showing that
E_{3,0}\cong Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1}
is completely analogous; if
f_{i}^{2}=1
instead, then we still have
(f_{i}f_{j})^{2}=-1
, but now
(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3})^{2}=-1
.
∎

Tensoring the above isomorphisms with
E_{0,1}
and
E_{1,0}
respectively, we obtain:

Corollary 2.31
.

There is an isomorphism of fermionic groups
E_{0,4}\cong E_{4,0}
.

Similarly, there are isomorphisms of fermionic groups
E_{\ell,k+4}\cong E_{\ell+4,k}
for nonzero
\ell,k
, and as a result there are also equivalences of spectra

(2.32)

\mathit{MTH}(E_{\ell,k+4})\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+4}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+4,k}\simeq\mathit{MTH}(E_{\ell+4,k}),

which will play an important role in
Section

4.3.2
.

Remark 2.33
.

When we apply the fermionic group algebra functor to the isomorphism
E_{\ell,k+4}\cong E_{\ell+4,k}
, we recover the familiar fact that there is an isomorphism (not just a Morita equivalence) of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded algebras
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k+4}\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell+4,k}
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

The second half of
2.29
is equivalent to the assertion that there is a group isomorphism
\varphi\colon(E_{3,0})_{b}\xrightarrow{\cong}(Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1})_{b}
which pulls back the twisting data of
Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1}
to the twisting data of
E_{3,0}
. Such a map
\varphi
can be extracted from its double cover (
2.30
); in the rest of this remark, we make that choice.

The twisting data for
E_{\ell,k}
comes from the virtual vector bundle which is the sum of
\ell
copies of
\sigma
and
k
copies of
-\sigma
, so it is natural to wonder whether there is a virtual vector bundle
V\to B(Q_{8})_{b}
and an isomorphism
\overline{\varphi}\colon V\boxplus(-\sigma)\cong\sigma^{\boxplus 3}
lifting
\varphi
in the sense that taking the first two Stiefel–Whitney classes of the map
\overline{\varphi}
recovers the effect of
\varphi
on twisting data.

Even though the twisting data on
Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1}
can be realized by a vector bundle twist, it turns out no such
\overline{\varphi}
can exist! To prove this, use the Whitney sum formula to calculate the Stiefel–Whitney classes of
V
, assuming it exists, and obtain a contradiction. This is a different problem from the existence of non-vector-bundle twists (
1.99
), and will complicate our definition of the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
in
Section

4.3
.
∎

In the above discussion, we saw how multiple fermionic groups (discrete or continuous) can reasonably be associated to symmetry classes, leading to different interacting SPT classifications.
Another common way in which two distinct fermionic groups are considered to be in ‘the same symmetry class’ is when their respective fermionic group algebras are
Morita equivalent
.
In that case these symmetries generate identical free SPT classifications, but in general inequivalent interacting SPT classifications
[
293
]
.
This is a crucial aspect of the free-to-interacting maps we define in
Section

3
and of our choice of fermionic groups in
Section

4.1
; see specifically
4.5
.

Example 2.35
.

The fermionic group algebra
\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,1}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{1,1}
is Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
(
1.67
), thus the
K
-theory of this fermionic group coincides with that of the trivial fermionic group:
K_{i}(\mathit{C\ell}_{1,1})\simeq\mathit{KO}_{i}
.
In particular, the classification of
E_{1,1}
-protected free fermion phases is the same as
E_{0,0}
-protected (i.e. class D) free phases by
2.4
.
However, this does
not
carry over for twisted spin bordism—the groups are different. In general, they do not even agree rationally. See
Table

2
.
∎

.
Comparing low-dimensional spin bordism groups
[

]
with dpin bordism groups
[
