, Theorem F.1]
.

Exactly the same thing as in
2.35
happens for
E_{k,k}
, whose group algebra
\mathit{C\ell}_{k,k}
(
1.67
) is Morita trivial, but whose spin-
(k,k)
bordism groups in general depend on
k
(see
5.55
). All of these different notions of twisted spin bordism in some sense correspond to class D free fermionic phases.

3.
Defining the free-to-interacting map

As we discussed in the introduction, one of our main goals is to produce homotopy-theoretic models of
free-to-interacting maps
, which relate the group of free fermion SPT phases with the group of interacting SPT phases for a specified symmetry type.

Physically, one can study a free Hamiltonian representing a free fermion phase and compute the order of the group of free fermion SPTs that it generates by stacking copies of the phase and checking whether it can be trivialized via free terms. When one passes to the interacting classification, new terms become available with which to deform the Hamiltonian, which can change the order of the group of phases it generates. In fact, for our examples of interest, quartic interaction terms in the Hamiltonian will always suffice to trivialize the phase
[
263
]
.

A free-to-interacting map should model this process by taking the deformation class of a free Hamiltonian to the interacting deformation class of the same Hamiltonian.
When distinct free phases can be adiabatically connected by interacting terms, we observe that the free-to-interacting map should have a kernel. Conversely, intrinsically-interacting phases, which have no free analog, should form the cokernel of a free-to-interacting map.

Following the approach of Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Sections 9, 10]
, we model free-to-interacting maps as maps from
K
-theory groups, which classify free SPTs by
2.1
and
2.3
, to groups of deformation classes of invertible field theories, which classify interacting SPTs according to
2.11
.
Each element of the
K
-theory group in the source represents a free fermion phase, while each element in the group of deformation classes of invertible field theories in the target represents an interacting fermion phase.

On the free side, there are many approaches in the literature connecting free fermion Hamiltonian models to representatives of
K
-theory classes, including, for example,
[
51
,
160
,
121
,
313
,
192
,
14
,
139
,
295
]
. In
Section

2.1
, we discussed such a model using Karoubi triples. On the interacting side, relating lattice models to invertible field theories is a more subtle problem and an active area of research: see for example
[
82
,
128
,
88
,
109
,
108
,
55
,
118
,
249
,
287
,
124
,
250
,
251
,
291
,
28
,
288
,
104
,
110
,
115
,
106
,
188
,
207
,
289
,
283
,
105
,
303
,
319
]
.
Thus we rely on
2.11
to justify our approach. We view the existence of mathematical free-to-interacting maps as supporting evidence for the homotopical approach to SPT phase classification, and one of our main goals in this paper is to add to this body of evidence.

In this section, we define a homotopical free-to-interacting map for a broader collection symmetry types than in prior work, including the fermionic groups
E_{\ell,k}
.
We compute the image of free-to-interacting maps of type
E_{\ell,k}
in
Section

5
, which we then compare with the physical computations of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
in
Section

4
.

3.1.
Classical Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation

The heart of the free-to-interacting map is a natural transformation between spin bordism and
\mathit{KO}
-theory called the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro (ABS) orientation. Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro
[
29
]
provided formulas for the Thom class in
\mathit{KO}
-theory (resp.
\mathit{KU}
-theory) of a spin (resp. spin
c
) vector bundle.
The choice of Thom classes defines an orientation, which gives a map of spectra
MT\mathrm{Spin}\to\mathit{KO}
(resp.
MT{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\to\mathit{KU}
)
[
18
,
165
]
.

In
[
118
]
, Freed–Hopkins generalized the ABS maps from spin and spin
c
bordism to the Thom spectra
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
corresponding to the
continuous
tenfold way fermionic groups
K_{f}(s)
(see
2.24
and
1
).
We first recall their construction; then, we explain our generalization for twists by pairs of vector bundles.

In the following,
X
will be a locally compact Hausdorff space. For finite
X
, taking
A=C_{0}(X)
and regarding
A
as a purely even superalgebra recovers the setting of
Section

1.4
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
V\to X
be a real Euclidean vector bundle over
X
, so that each fiber is equipped with a metric
h_{x}
. The
Clifford bundle

\mathit{C\ell}(V)
of
V
is the algebra bundle whose fiber at
x
is the Clifford algebra
\mathit{C\ell}(V_{x},-q_{x})
, where
q_{x}(v)\coloneqq h_{x}(v,v)
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
graded Clifford module
of
V\to X
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded real vector bundle
E\to X
equipped with actions

V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}E^{0}\to E^{1}
and
V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}E^{1}\to E^{0}

such that for each
v\in V
and
e\in E
,

(3.3)

v(v(e))=-\lVert v\rVert^{2}e.

Remark 3.4
.

If
X
is compact Hausdorff, graded Clifford modules for
V\to X
are equivalent data to finitely generated, projective modules over the
C^{*}
-algebra
\Gamma(X;\mathit{C\ell}(V))
. This follows from the fact that the Serre–Swan theorem
[
304
]
, that taking sections defines an equivalence of categories from vector bundles on
X
to finitely generated projective
C(X)
-algebras, in fact refines to a monoidal equivalence by Conlon
[
72
, §7.5]
and Faurot–Ferrer
[
103
, Theorem A.7]
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

Let
V\to X
be a vector bundle with a quadratic form.
The exterior power bundle
\Lambda^{*}V
admits the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded
\mathit{C\ell}(V)
-module: its graded pieces are the even and odd powers
E^{0}=\Lambda^{\text{ev}}V
,
E^{1}=\Lambda^{\text{odd}}V
and the action of
V
is given by

(3.6a)

\displaystyle V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{*}(V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}V

(3.6b)

\displaystyle v\otimes e

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}v\wedge e-(v\,\neg\,e).

Here
\neg\,e
is the adjoint of
\wedge\,e
with respect to the bilinear form on
\Lambda^{*}(V)
induced from the quadratic form on
V
.
∎

In (
1.74
), we defined the Clifford module quotient of a graded algebra by considering graded modules modulo those that extend to the action of one more positive Clifford generator.
For the vector bundle setting, let
V\to X
be a rank-
k
vector bundle with quadratic form
q_{V}
and let
E
be a graded
\mathit{C\ell}(V)
-module. Equip
V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
with the quadratic form
q_{V}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathbb{R}}}
.
Then if
E
extends to a
\mathit{C\ell}(V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}})
module, each fiber
E_{x}
admits the action of
\mathit{C\ell}(V_{x})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathit{C\ell}_{-1}
, by
1.51
.
As a special case of (
1.74
), we thus get:

Definition 3.7
.

Let
V
and
V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
be as above, and let
\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{\mathit{C\ell}(V)})
be the commutative monoid
of isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional graded
\mathit{C\ell}(V)
-modules under
\oplus
.
The Clifford module quotient
29
29

29

This corresponds to the group Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro call
A(V)

[
29
, §11]
.
is the abelian group

(3.8)

\mathrm{CMQ}^{0}(V)\coloneqq\mathrm{coker}\,(\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{\mathit{C\ell}(V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}})})\to\pi_{0}(\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{\mathit{C\ell}(V)})).

where the map of monoids is induced by the inclusion
V\hookrightarrow V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
.
More generally, define
\mathrm{CMQ}^{-n}(V)
as
\mathrm{CMQ}^{0}(V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, where the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is equipped with the standard quadratic form.

In the complex case, there is an analogous group built from
\mathbb{C}\ell(V)
-modules, where
V
is a complex vector bundle with a Hermitian metric.

As noted in
1.77
, the Clifford module quotient group does not in general give a model for
K
-theory. Instead, there is a homomorphism

\mathrm{CMQ}^{0}(V)\to\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{0}(X^{V})
, which Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro call the
Euler characteristic map

[
29
, Definition 8.1]
. To discuss it, we will use the L-form of
K
-theory classes in the case of a 2-term complex, meaning that we specify classes in
\mathit{KO}^{0}(X,Y)
by a pair of vector bundles
V,W\to X
and a map
s\colon V\to W
between them, written
[V,W;s]
,
where
s
restricts to an isomorphism over
Y
.
See e.g.
[
29
, §7]
,
[
211
, Definition 9.23]
for the details of this model.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
V\to X
be a real vector bundle, let
D(V)
and
S(V)
be the disk bundle and sphere bundle, resp., and let
\pi\colon D(V)\to X
be the projection. The Euler characteristic map makes the assignment

(3.10)

\displaystyle\chi_{V}\colon\mathrm{CMQ}^{0}(V)

\displaystyle\to\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{0}(X^{V})

\displaystyle E=E^{0}\oplus E^{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}[\pi^{*}E^{1},\pi^{*}E^{0};\sigma]

where
\sigma

is given by multiplication by minus the vector in the base.
That is,
\sigma|_{v\in V}\colon e\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}-v\cdot e
for
e\in\mathit{C\ell}(V)|_{v}
.

Here, we used that
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{0}(X^{V})\cong\mathit{KO}^{0}(D(V),S(V))
.
See e.g.
[
29
, §8, §9]
for more details. The complex case is analogous.

A complex vector bundle
V
always admits a spin
c
structure, meaning that it is oriented for
K
-theory. We next recall the Clifford module construction for the
K
-theory Thom class from
[
29
, §11, §12]
.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
29
, Theorem (12.3)]
)
.

Let
V
be a rank-
2k
spin
c
vector bundle. Then there is a class
u_{V}^{KU}\in\widetilde{KU}^{2k}(X^{V})
such that

\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{*}(X^{V})
is a free
\mathit{KU}^{*}(X)
-module of rank one generated by
u^{\mathit{KU}}_{V}
.

In particular, for each degree
i

there is an isomorphism

(3.12)

\mathit{KU}^{i}(X)\xrightarrow{-\cdot u^{\mathit{KU}}_{V}}\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{i+2k}(X^{V}).

Remark 3.13
.

We choose to put the Thom class in degree
2k
, but many sources discuss this class as living in degree zero, using periodicity of
KU
-theory.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

Let
V
be a rank-
k
complex vector bundle.
Then
V
admits a spin
c
structure, meaning an isomorphism

(3.15)

V\cong P_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}_{2k}^{c}}\mathbb{R}^{2k}

for
P_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
a principal
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{2k}
bundle.
From this bundle and the Clifford module
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
, we may form the Clifford module
P_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{2k}}\Lambda^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
, using the action of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{2k}\subset\mathbb{C}\ell_{2k}^{\times}
.
Then there is an equivalence

\chi_{V}(\Lambda^{*}(V))=\chi_{V}(P_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{2k}}\Lambda^{*}(\mathbb{C}^{k}))
, so we may use either construction to form the Thom class. See
[
29
, Proposition 11.6]
.
∎

Complex vector bundles have canonical spin
c
structures and even (real) rank, so are particular cases of the above construction. To specify the
\mathit{KU}
-theory orientation of an
odd-rank
spin
c
vector bundle
V
, one can stabilize to the even-rank bundle
V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
, whose Thom space is naturally homotopy equivalent to
\Sigma X^{V}
, and use the suspension isomorphism on
\mathit{KU}
-theory to obtain the Thom class over
X^{V}
.

For real
\mathit{KO}
-theory, the construction is analogous, except that the representation theory of real Clifford modules is richer than that of complex ones. In this case, we will need to stabilize to a rank that is a multiple of
8
by adding trivial bundles.

Theorem 3.16

(
[
29
, Theorem (12.3)]
)
.

Let
V\to X
be a rank-
8k
spin vector bundle, let
\lambda^{k}\in\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}(\mathit{C\ell}_{8k})
be an irreducible graded module, and let
P_{\mathrm{Spin}}
be a principal
\mathrm{Spin}_{8k}
-bundle such that
V\cong P_{\mathrm{Spin}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}_{8k}}\mathbb{R}^{8k}
as spin vector bundles.
Write
\beta\in\mathit{KO}^{-8}
for the real Bott class. Define

(3.17)

u^{\mathit{KO}}_{V}\coloneqq\chi_{V}(P_{\mathrm{Spin}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}_{8k}}\lambda^{k}){\cdot\beta^{-k}}\in\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{{8k}}(X^{V}).

Then
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{*}(X^{V})
is a free
\mathit{KO}^{*}(X)
-module of rank one generated by
u^{\mathit{KO}}_{V}
.

In particular, for each degree
i

there is an isomorphism

(3.18)

\mathit{KO}^{i}(X)\xrightarrow{-\cdot u^{\mathit{KO}}_{V}}\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{i+8k}(X^{V}).

See
[
29
, Cor. (6.6), Section 11]
.

Above, we discussed the construction of Thom classes for spin
c
and spin vector bundles, but we will ultimately use maps of spectra.
Returning to the complex case,
to specify the orientation map
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\to\mathit{KU}
it suffices to provide classes in the
KU
-theory of the Thom spaces that make up the spectrum
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
that are compatible with the structure maps of the spectrum.
In more detail,
recall that the spaces in the spectrum
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
are the Thom spaces
M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{n}=\mathrm{Th}(B\mathrm{Spin}_{n}^{c};V^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}_{n})
,
30
30

30

A priori, this process builds a different spectrum,
M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
;
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
is the Thom spectrum of
-V^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\to B\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
. The Pontrjagin–Thom theorem identifies the homotopy groups of
M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
, resp.
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
, with the bordism groups of manifolds with spin
c
structures on their stable normal bundles, resp. stable tangent bundles. Fortunately, there is an equivalence
M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
: see
[
245
, Remark 3.3]
. This uses the well-known fact that the map
B\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\to B\mathrm{O}
is
A_{\infty}
, in fact
E_{\infty}
. See
[
86
, Example 2.58]
for a non-original reference. The story for
M\mathrm{Spin}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}
is completely analogous. For more general twisted spin structures, though, including spin-
(\ell,k)
structures, this equivalence does not generalize.
where
V^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}_{n}
is the pullback of the universal spin
c
bundle along the map
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{n}\to{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
. We write
\mathit{KU}_{n}
for the classifying space for the functor
\mathit{KU}^{-n}
.
To produce a collection of compatible maps
M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{n}\to\mathit{KU}_{n}
, we will take the classifying maps of a set of
\mathit{KU}
-theory classes. Specifically, in each dimension
n
, we take the class
u_{V_{n}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{\mathit{KU}}
, which gives the Thom class in
\mathit{KU}
-theory by
3.14
:

(3.19)

u^{\mathit{KU}}_{V_{n}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}}\in\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{0}(M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{n})~\rightsquigarrow~M\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{n}\to\mathit{KO}_{n}.

See e.g.
[
175
]
for more details on the map of spectra. The real case is analogous.

3.2.
Smith homomorphisms

Smith homomorphisms are maps on bordism groups induced by zero section maps between Thom spaces. They were first introduced by Conner–Floyd
[
73
, Theorem 26.1]
, and the full theory was worked out in
[
80
]
. See there for further background, references, and examples.

Smith homomorphisms have the effect of twisting the tangential structure on manifolds between their domains and codomains, which allows them to capture certain forms of symmetry breaking, as studied in
[
155
,
75
,
203
,
96
,
74
,
79
,
181
,
232
]
and as we will discuss in
Section

4.3.1
.
We will introduce them as induced from maps of Thom spectra, following
[
80
, §3.2]
.

Let
X
be a space and let
W\to X
be a
k
-dimensional real vector bundle. Taking the zero section of
W
defines a map
X_{+}\hookrightarrow X^{W}
from the base space into the Thom space. Similarly, if
V
is a (possibly virtual) vector bundle on
X
, the inclusion
V\hookrightarrow V\oplus W
also defines a map on Thom spectra:

(3.20)

e_{W}\colon X^{V}\longrightarrow X^{V+W}.

In the case that
V
is an actual vector bundle, the map descends to Thom spaces.

Smith homomorphisms
are induced from maps
e_{V}
as in (
3.20
) by smashing with a bordism spectrum
MT\xi
and taking homotopy, then using the Pontrjagin–Thom theorem to identify the resulting groups with bordism groups.

Definition 3.21
.

The
Smith homomorphism
in
\xi
-bordism in degree
n
associated to (
3.20
) is the induced map to (
3.20
) on
\Omega_{n}^{\xi}
:

(3.22)

\mathrm{sm}_{W}\colon\Omega^{\xi}_{n}(X^{V})\to\Omega^{\xi}_{n}(X^{V+W})\cong\Omega^{\xi}_{n-k}(X^{V+W-k})

On the right side we used the suspension isomorphism in bordism to normalize degrees.

Lemma 3.23

(
[
80
, Proposition 3.17, Corollary 4.39]
)
.

The Smith homomorphism associated to the data above can be equivalently described as

(1)

the map sending
the class of a closed
n
-manifold
[M]
to the bordism class
[N]
, where
N\subset M
is the
(n-k)
-dimensional submanifold defined as the intersection of the zero section of
W
(restricted to
M
) with a generic section
s
, or

(2)

the map sending a class
[M]
to the Poincaré dual of the cobordism
31
31

31

By “cobordism” we mean the generalized cohomology theory associated to the spectrum
\mathit{MT\xi}
, whose homology theory is called bordism. This terminology is due to Atiyah
[
30
]
.
Euler class

e^{\mathit{MT\xi}}(f^{*}W)
.

To type-check description (1), first
invoke the preimage theorem to ensure that
N=s^{-1}(0)
is a closed
(n-k)
-manifold, assuming
s
is transverse to the zero section, which is true for generic
s
. Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §4.2]
show that, in that case, the bordism class of
N
is independent of the choice of
s
.
By definition,
M
is a closed
n
-manifold with an
(X,V)
-twisted spin structure, so there is a map
f\colon M\to X
and a spin structure on
TM\oplus f^{*}V
. Equip
N
with the map
g\colon N\hookrightarrow M\xrightarrow{f}X
. The normal bundle
\nu
to the embedding of
N
is isomorphic to
g^{*}W
by construction,
so the exact sequence

(3.24)

0\to TN\to TM|_{N}\to\nu\to 0

implies that
TN
carries a
(X,V\oplus W)
-twisted spin structure.

See
[
80
, §3, §7]
for a general discussion and an extensive collection of examples. The examples relevant to this article are the Smith maps for which
X=B\mathbb{Z}/2
,
V
and
W
are direct sums of some number of copies of the universal bundle
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\xi
is spin or spin
c
structure, which are discussed in (
ibid.
, Examples 7.8, 7.13, and 7.37) as well as the references therein.

Example 3.25
.

Let
X=B\mathbb{Z}/2
,
V=0
, and
W=\sigma
. The zero section map
0\hookrightarrow\sigma
defines a map of spectra

(3.26)

e_{\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma}.

Taking spin bordism yields homomorphisms

(3.27)

\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{n}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{n-1}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\cong\Omega_{n-1}^{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}.\qed

The isomorphism
\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{n-1}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\cong\Omega_{n-1}^{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}
was first observed in
[
256
, §7]
; it can also be proven using the shearing isomorphism of
1.104
.
Restricting the domain of (
3.27
) to
reduced
spin bordism defines an isomorphism for all
n

[
17
]
.

The previous map fits into a family of four types of Smith homomorphisms for
X=B\mathbb{Z}/2
,
W=\sigma
, and
V=n\sigma
. Since four copies of any bundle, and in particular
4\sigma
, is spin, the twisted spin tangential structures appearing in iterated Smith homomorphisms forms a periodic family depending only on
n\bmod 4
. The four symmetry types, starting from
V=0
, go as follows:

(3.28)

\mathrm{Spin}\times\mathbb{Z}/2~\rightsquigarrow~{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}~\rightsquigarrow~\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathbb{Z}/4~\rightsquigarrow~{\mathrm{Pin}^{+}}~\rightsquigarrow~\dots

See
[
185
,
307
,
155
,
326
,
79
,
80
]
for more on this
4
-periodic family of Smith homomorphisms.

Example 3.29
.

Similarly, we may take spin
c
bordism of (
3.26
) to get Smith homomorphisms

(3.30)

\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}_{n}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\Omega^{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}_{n-1}.

Like in the previous example,
1.104
can be used to show
\Omega_{n}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\cong\Omega_{n}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}
, a theorem of Bahri–Gilkey
[
34
,
35
]
. Bahri–Gilkey also showed that, if we use
reduced
spin
c
bordism on the left-hand side of (
3.30
),
e_{\sigma}
is an isomorphism for all
n
.

Meanwhile, starting with
V=\sigma
defines a map

(3.31)

e_{\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma}\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2\sigma}.

For any vector bundle
V
,
V\oplus V
has a complex structure, hence a spin
c
structure. Therefore there is a Thom isomorphism
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2\sigma-2})\cong\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and we get Smith homomorphisms

(3.32)

\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}_{n}\to\Omega^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}_{n-1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2).

In general, these are not isomorphisms.

Overall, we get a 2-periodic family of twisted spin
c
structures:

(3.33)

{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\times\mathbb{Z}/2~\rightsquigarrow~{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}\rightsquigarrow\dots

This family of Smith homomorphisms also appears in
[
35
,
153
,
79
,
80
]
.
∎

In the next section, we will use Smith homomorphisms to give explicit descriptions of certain twisted ABS maps. Later, in
Section

4.3.1
, we will explain the role that
Anderson-dualized
Smith homomorphisms play in symmetry breaking, motivating our construction in
4.51
.

3.3.
Twisted ABS maps

Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9.2.2]
define twisted ABS maps for the ten continuous symmetry types
H(s)
and
H^{c}(s)
from
2.24
(see
Table

1
for this notation). We start off by describing their constructions in the real case with the parameter
|s|\leq 3
.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
V\to B\mathrm{O}_{s}
be the universal vector bundle. Using the Clifford module construction of
3.5
, define

(3.35)

\lambda_{V}\coloneqq\chi_{V}([\Lambda^{\text{ev}}(V),\Lambda^{\text{odd}}(V);\sigma_{V}]),

where
\sigma_{V}
is the map sending
(v,w)\in V\times\Lambda^{*}V
to
(v,{v\wedge w+v\mathbin{\lrcorner}w})
in the other graded piece of
V\times\Lambda^{*}V
.

Remark 3.36
.

If
V
were complex, the class
\lambda_{V}
described above would give the
\mathit{KU}
-theory Thom class of
V
(see
3.11
), since any complex vector bundle is also spin
c
, and thus oriented for
\mathit{KU}
-theory. However, the universal bundles over
B\mathrm{O}_{s}
are not spin, and thus do not have Thom isomorphisms in
\mathit{KO}
-theory.
32
32

32

For refinements to equivariant
\mathit{KO}
-theory, see
[
94
,
114
,
139
,
53
]
.

∎

The classifying map of
\lambda_{V}
can be described as a map
B\mathrm{O}_{s}^{V}\to\mathit{KO}
. We may desuspend
s
times so that the domain is the Thom spectrum of a rank-zero virtual bundle:

(3.37)

\Sigma^{-s}\lambda_{V}\colon B\mathrm{O}_{s}^{V-s}\to\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}.

Freed–Hopkins use this class to define the twisted ABS maps for
s=1,2,3
.

Definition 3.38

(
[
118
, Proposition 10.27]
)
.

Let
s=1
,
2
, or
3
, and let
V\to B\mathrm{O}_{s}
be the universal bundle. Then
H(s)
-structures can be identified with
(B\mathrm{O}_{s},V)
-twisted spin structures (this is implicit in Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §10]
), so by
1.104
,
\mathit{MTH}(s)\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{O}_{s})^{V-s}

[
118
, (10.2)]
.

The twisted ABS map for
\mathit{MTH}(s)
is the composition

(3.39)

\mathit{MTH}(s)\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{O}_{s})^{V-s}\xrightarrow{{\mathit{ABS}}_{0}^{r}\wedge\Sigma^{-s}\lambda_{V}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}.

where
\mathit{ABS}_{0}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\to\mathit{KO}
is the classical ABS map described in
Section

3.1
.

See
[
118
, Proposition 10.27]
for a comparison to an index-theoretic definition. We will mostly focus on the cases
s=1
in
3.38
(and
s=-1
in
3.43
, below). For these, see also the more recent discussion in
[
129
]
.

Example 3.40
.

For
s=1
, the twisted ABS map is

(3.41)

\mathit{ABS}_{s=+1}\colon MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge\lambda_{\sigma}}\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}.\qed

For
s<0
, there is a different construction. In the following, for
V
the universal bundle over
B\mathrm{O}_{\lvert s\rvert}
, we will use
e_{V}
to denote the zero section map

(3.42)

e_{V}\colon(B\mathrm{O}_{\lvert s\rvert})^{-V}\to(B\mathrm{O}_{\lvert s\rvert})_{+}.

In what follows, denote by
\underline{1}
the classifying map for the trivial line bundle, in this case over
B\mathrm{O}_{|s|}
.

Definition 3.43

(
[
118
, Proposition 10.24]
)
.

For
s=-1
,
-2
, or
-3
, the twisted ABS map for
\mathit{MTH}(s)
is the composition

(3.44)

\mathit{MTH}(s)\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{O}_{|s|})^{|s|-V}\xrightarrow{ABS_{0}\wedge e_{V}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{|s|}(B\mathrm{O}_{|s|})_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}\Sigma^{|s|}\mathit{KO}.

Like for positive
s
, Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, (10.2)]
prove
\mathit{MTH}(s)\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{O}_{|s|})^{|s|-V}
, implicitly identifying
H(s)
-structures with
(B\mathrm{O}_{\lvert s\rvert},-V)
-twisted spin structures.

On homotopy, (
3.44
) factors as

(3.45)

\Omega_{n}^{H(s)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sm}_{V}}\Omega_{n+s}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathrm{O}_{-s})\xrightarrow{c}\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{n+s}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}}\mathit{KO}_{n+s},

where
V\to B\mathrm{O}_{-s}
is the universal bundle,
\mathrm{sm}_{V}
is the Smith homomorphism, and
c
is the map forgetting the data of the auxiliary
B\mathrm{O}_{-s}
-bundle.

The factorization (
3.45
) was used in
[
27
, Appendix A]
to evaluate the map
\mathit{ABS}_{-s}
on a set of generators for
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}}
.

Definition 3.46

(
[
118
, Proposition 10.24]
)
.

Let
\mathit{KSp}=\Sigma^{4}\mathit{KO}
denote the quaternionic
K
-theory spectrum. There is a unique homotopy class of maps
\psi\colon(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{3-V}\to\mathit{KSp}
whose composition with the map
\mathbb{HP}^{1}\to(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{3-V}
is in the homotopy class of the standard generator of
\widetilde{\mathit{KSp}}{}^{0}(\mathbb{HP}^{1})
under the diffeomorphism
\mathbb{HP}^{1}\cong S^{4}
.

For
s=4
, the twisted ABS map for
\mathit{MTH}(s)
is the composition

(3.47)

\mathit{MTH}(4)\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{3-V}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge\psi}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-4}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{-4}\mathit{KO}.

The
s=4
twisted ABS map has also been studied by Hu
[
168
, §6.1]
and Buchanan–McKean
[
63
]
.

Example 3.48
.

For
s=-1
, the twisted ABS map is

(3.49)

\mathit{ABS}_{s=-1}\colon MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge e_{\sigma}}\Sigma\mathit{KO}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}\Sigma\mathit{KO}.\qed

Remark 3.50
.

These definitions are asymmetric: the generalized ABS maps constructed by Freed–Hopkins for
s>0
and
s<0
are different, and they are carefully chosen to land in the desired degrees. This in particular uses more information than just the fermionic group of symmetries.

For example, for
G_{f}=\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
, corresponding by
[
27
, Proposition 1.26]
to pin
+
structure,
3.48
used the equivalence
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
. Using the Whitney sum formula, one can show that
4\sigma-4
is spin, so that (see, e.g.,
[
89
, Theorem 1.39]
or
[
79
, §6]
) there is also an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3\sigma-3}
.
33
33

33

In fact, this is how Stolz
[
298
, §8]
first decomposed
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}
; the
1-\sigma
description came later.

Thus we could have defined a map

(3.51)

MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3\sigma-3}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge\lambda_{3\sigma}}\Sigma^{-3}\mathit{KO}

but this choice of target is not appropriate
for our free fermion theories of interest—we land in class CII, whereas Freed–Hopkins place pin
+
structures in class DIII
[
118
, (9.2.5)]
. The corresponding free fermion phases have different classifications in most dimensions, and therefore the choice (
3.51
) would contradict the established classification of these free fermion phases.
∎

Example 3.52
.

The Klein bottle
K
has four pin
+
structures; two of them are bounding and two are nonbounding, and either of the two nonbounding ones generates
\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{+}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

[
195
, Proposition 3.9]
. In this example, we compute
\mathit{ABS}_{1}(K,\mathfrak{p})
for each pin
+
structure
\mathfrak{p}
of
K
.

The Klein bottle is a circle bundle over the circle, explicitly described as the quotient of
S^{1}\times[0,1]
by the reflection
(z,0)\sim(\overline{z},1)
. If
t
denotes the coordinate on
[0,1]
, then the vector field
\partial_{t}
on
S^{1}\times[0,1]
descends to
K
and is nonvanishing, so
TK\cong\sigma\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
for a real line bundle
\sigma\to K
, which is the pullback of the Möbius bundle on the base
S^{1}
. Thus
w_{1}(K)
is the pullback of the top-degree class on the base, meaning that the inclusion
j\colon S^{1}\to K
of any fiber is a representative of the Poincaré dual of
w_{1}(K)
, which can be identified with the intersection of a generic section and the zero section. The vector field
\partial_{t}
also trivializes the normal bundle to
j
, so any choice of pin
+
structure
\mathfrak{p}
on
K
induces a pin
+
structure on the fiber, hence (after choosing once and for all an orientation on the fiber) a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

The composition

(3.53)

\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{+}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}}\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{c}\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}},

in which the map
c
forgets the principal
\mathbb{Z}/2
-bundle, sends
(K,\mathfrak{p})\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(S^{1},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. Kirby-Taylor (
ibid.
) show that
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is nonbounding if and only if
\mathfrak{p}
is: (
3.53
) is an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}/2
.

The last step in the computation of the twisted ABS map is to apply
\mathit{ABS}_{0}
, which is an isomorphism
\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}\to\mathit{KO}_{1}
. That is:

•

If
\mathfrak{p}
is one of the two nonbounding pin
+
structures on
K
,
\mathit{ABS}_{1}(K,\mathfrak{p})=\mathit{ABS}_{0}(S_{\mathit{nb}}^{1})=\eta
, the nonzero element of
\mathit{KO}_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.

•

If
\mathfrak{p}
is one of the two bounding structures on
K
,
\mathit{ABS}_{1}(K,\mathfrak{p})=\mathit{ABS}_{0}(S_{b}^{1})=0
.

Since
\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{+}}
is torsion
[
136
]
, this also implies that the Anderson dual of
\mathit{ABS}_{1}
is an isomorphism in degree
2
, as originally shown by Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Corollary 9.83]
. In light of
2.11
, we interpret this isomorphism as saying that the nontrivial free phase in one spatial dimension, the class DIII superconductor, survives the incorporation of interactions and generates the group of interacting phases.
∎

3.4.
Generalization for Spin-
({\ell,k})

We now generalize the ABS orientation to assign
\mathit{KO}
- and
\mathit{KU}
-classes to certain twisted spin manifolds, including spin-
(\ell,k)
and spin
c
-
(\ell,k)
manifolds (see
1.112
). This will be the main ingredient in the generalization of the free-to-interacting map to spin-
(\ell,k)
and spin
c
-
(\ell,k)
theories.
Our generalization combines the techniques for the twisted ABS maps discussed above in
3.38
and
3.43
, i.e. the cases
s\neq 4
.

Definition 3.54
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be compact Lie groups. Let
V_{1}\to BG_{1}
and
V_{2}\to BG_{2}
be vector bundles of ranks
r_{1}
, resp.
r_{2}
.
The
(V_{1},V_{2})
-
twisted real ABS map
is the composition

(3.55)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}(BG_{2})_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}\mathit{KO}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{r_{2}-r_{1}}\mathit{KO},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{r}\wedge\lambda_{V_{1}}\wedge e_{V_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where
\mu
is the multiplication induced by the ring spectrum structure on
\mathit{KO}
.

Analogously, we define the
(V_{1},V_{2})
-twisted complex ABS map
to be the composition

(3.56)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge(BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KU}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KU}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}(BG_{2})_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KU}\wedge\Sigma^{-r_{1}}\mathit{KU}\wedge\Sigma^{r_{2}}\mathit{KU}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{r_{2}-r_{1}}\mathit{KU}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{c}\wedge\lambda_{V_{1}}\wedge e_{V_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Remark 3.57
.

We refer to these maps as twisted ABS
maps
rather than a twisted ABS
orientations
in order to follow the usual convention that an orientation is multiplicative, i.e. a map of ring spectra.
For
X
a spectrum, the spectrum
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge X
need not be a ring spectrum, but only an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module spectrum. For example, when
s\neq 0
, one can show that
\mathit{MTH}(s)
is not a ring spectrum.
∎

Remark 3.58
.

Just as the twisted ABS map
3.43
factors through
e_{V}
, as in (
3.45
), the
(V_{1},V_{2})
-twisted ABS maps factor through the composition

(3.59)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n-r_{2}}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BG_{1}^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge BG_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n-r_{2}}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sm}_{V_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

where
\mathrm{sm}_{V_{2}}
is a Smith homomorphism, which cuts out a submanifold, and
c
is the crush map, which forgets the
G_{2}
-bundle. There is a similar factorization in the complex case.

We may interpret this factorization as a localization of the twisted index to a codimension-
r_{2}
,
(BG_{1},V_{1})
-twisted spin submanifold. In this way, we recover some results of Fast–Ochanine
[
102
]
,
Hayashi
[
156
]
, and Miyazawa
[
243
]
for various choices of
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
V_{1}
, and
V_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 3.60

(
[
31
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

There is a homotopy equivalence

(3.61)

(BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}}\simeq B(G_{1}\times G_{2})^{(V_{1}-r_{1})\boxplus(r_{2}-V_{2})}.

For our application to free-to-interacting maps, the choice of
G_{1},G_{2}
and the pair of bundles will be determined by the symmetries of the system. In this work, we will elaborate further only for the special case of the Bott spiral. In this case, we model the symmetries of the relevant SPTs using spin-
(\ell,k)
-structures, so we specialize to
G_{1}=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell}
,
G_{2}=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{k}
,
V_{1}=\sigma^{\boxplus\ell}
, and
V_{2}=\sigma^{\boxplus k}
. Recall the spectrum
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
from
1.112
; then, by
3.60
,
(BG_{1})^{V_{1}-r_{1}}\wedge(BG_{2})^{\,r_{2}-V_{2}}\simeq\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
.

Definition 3.62
.

The twisted ABS map
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}
is the composition

(3.63)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge\ell}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma})^{\wedge k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO})^{\wedge\ell}\wedge(\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+})^{\wedge k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-\ell}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{k}\mathit{KO}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{k-\ell}\mathit{KO}.}
(
1.112
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge\lambda_{\sigma}^{\wedge\ell}\wedge e_{\sigma}^{\wedge k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Remark 3.64
.

By definition,
\mathit{ABS}_{1,0}
(resp.
\mathit{ABS}_{0,1}
) from
3.62
coincides with Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABS map in the case
s=1
(resp.
s=-1
): see
3.40
and
3.48
.
∎

Example 3.65
.

Return to the case of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,1}\simeq MT\mathrm{DPin}
. Then
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
is the composition

(3.66)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{DPin}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{KO}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KO}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge\lambda_{\sigma}\wedge e_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\wedge\underline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

On homotopy, we get a map

(3.67)

\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}\colon\Omega^{E_{1,1}}_{d}\longrightarrow\mathit{KO}_{d}.

For degree reasons, this is the zero map except for
d\equiv 0,1,2,4\bmod 8
. When
d\equiv 0\bmod 4
,
\mathit{KO}_{d}\cong\mathbb{Z}
but
\Omega_{d}^{E_{1,1}}
is torsion, by
6.6
, so the map is also
0
. This leaves
d\equiv 1,2\bmod 8
.

For
d=1
, we have
\Omega_{1}^{E_{1,1}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

[
182
, Theorem F.1]
. By
1.112
, an
E_{1,1}
-structure on a manifold
M
is a pair of line bundles
L_{1},L_{2}\to M
and a spin structure on
TM+L_{1}-L_{2}
; thus
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
with
L_{1}=L_{2}=\sigma
admits an
E_{1,1}
structure, and this represents the nonzero element of
\Omega_{1}^{E_{1,1}}
, as it is detected by the bordism invariant
\int w_{1}(L_{1})
. The Smith homomorphism sends
\mathbb{RP}^{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\mathbb{RP}^{0}
with some
\mathbb{Z}/2
-bundle, and
c
forgets the bundle, giving us
\mathbb{RP}^{0}\cong\mathrm{pt}
, the nonzero element of
\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}
. Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Corollary 9.85]
showed that
\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}
of
\mathit{ABS}_{s=1}
is an isomorphism in degree
0
, and since the domain and codomain of
\mathit{ABS}_{s=1}
are both torsion, this implies
\mathit{ABS}_{s=1}
is an isomorphism in degree
0
. Thus
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
sends
\mathbb{RP}^{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\eta\in\mathit{KO}_{1}
.

Because
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
is a map of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-modules, it commutes with the action of any element of
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}
. Using this, we can immediately conclude that in all degrees
d\equiv 1,2\bmod 8
,
d>0
,
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
is surjective.

•

A
Bott manifold
is a closed spin
8
-manifold whose image under the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map is the Bott class
\beta\in\mathit{KO}_{8}
. Freed–Hopkins
[
117
, §5.3]
construct a Bott manifold
B
.
Thus if
d=8k+1
,
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}(B^{k}\times\mathbb{RP}^{1})=\beta^{k}\eta
, which is the unique nonzero element of
\mathit{KO}_{d}
.

•

The same argument works in degrees
8k+2
as soon as we show that
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
is surjective in degree
2
. For this, we use that
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
must commute with
\eta\in\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}
. The nonbounding spin circle
S_{\mathit{nb}}^{1}
represents
\eta
in
\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}
, so this tells us
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
sends
\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times S_{\mathit{nb}}^{1}
to
\eta^{2}
, which is the unique nonzero element of
\mathit{KO}_{2}
—and likewise,
B^{k}\times\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times S_{\mathit{nb}}^{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\beta^{k}\eta^{2}
, the unique nonzero element of
\mathit{KO}_{8k+2}
.

One can show that
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
has an
E_{1,1}
structure with which it is linearly independent to
\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times S_{\mathit{nb}}^{1}
in
\Omega_{2}^{E_{1,1}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
, given by the line bundles
(\sigma,\underline{\mathbb{R}})
. Said differently, there are two
E_{1,1}
structures on
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
induced from its two pin
-
structures. Choose either of these two
E_{1,1}
structures. We will see that the Smith homomorphism kills the bordism class of this manifold, implying
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})=0
.

To compute the Smith homomorphism
\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{E_{1,1}}_{2}\to\Omega_{1}^{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
, we may use the Smith long exact sequence of bordism groups
[
80
]
; specifically, per
[
80
, (7.1)]
with
k=-1
, there is a fiber sequence

(3.68)

\mathbb{S}\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{e_{\sigma}}\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2),

which, after smashing with
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\simeq MT{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}
, becomes

(3.69)

MT{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\to MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge ME_{1,1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}}MT{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2).

Taking homotopy groups yields a long exact sequence of bordism groups. One can work out the groups and homomorphisms in this long exact sequence in low degrees, using the
E_{1,1}
bordism groups computed in
[
182
, Appendix F]
and the groups
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
computed in low degrees in
[
161
, Theorem 3.2.1]
and
[
150
, Theorem 16]
; we leave this as an exercise for the reader. However, for our goal of computing on
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, we do not need to do this—by exactness, if the
E_{1,1}
structure on a closed
d
-manifold
M
was induced from a pin
-
structure, then
\mathrm{sm}_{\sigma}(M)=0\in\Omega_{d-1}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Since this is how we constructed the
E_{1,1}
structure on
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, we conclude its image under the Smith homomorphism—and therefore also under
\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
—is
0
.
∎

Now we turn to spin
c
-
(\ell,k)
structures. By
1.123
, in these cases the two indices
\ell,k
collapse to just
m\coloneqq\ell+k
, so it suffices to consider twisted ABS maps with domain
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m}
, where
\mathit{ME}_{m}=((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge m}
(
1.122
).

Definition 3.70
.

The twisted ABS map
\mathit{ABS}^{c}_{m}
is the composition

(3.71)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{KU}\wedge\Sigma^{-m}\mathit{KU}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-m}\mathit{KU}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ABS}_{0}^{c}\wedge\lambda_{\sigma}^{\wedge m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

At this point, we have defined twisted ABS maps for all continuous symmetry types and all discrete symmetry types from
2.24
, except for the class C discrete case. We will use the continuous ABS map defined by Freed–Hopkins (
3.46
) to define the discrete analog.

Definition 3.72
.

Let
\rho\colon Q_{8}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SU}_{2}
be the map of fermionic groups induced by the inclusion of
\{\pm 1,\pm i,\pm j,\pm k\}
into the norm-one quaternions, where both fermionic groups have trivial map to
\mathbb{Z}/2
and
(-1)^{F}=-1
. Then, the discrete class C twisted ABS map is the composition

(3.73)

\mathit{ABS}^{\delta}_{s=4}\colon\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})\xrightarrow{\rho}MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\xrightarrow{\eqref{spinh_ABS_eqn}}\Sigma^{-4}\mathit{KO}.

Remark 3.74
.

Because the maps
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}
defined in
3.62
are
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module maps, and the codomain
\Sigma^{k-\ell}\mathit{KO}
acquires its
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module structure through a sequence of
E_{\infty}
-ring maps
\mathit{ABS}_{0}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\to\mathit{ko}
followed by
\mathit{ko}\to\mathit{KO}
(i.e. the ABS map factors through
\mathit{ko}
), the maps
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}
naturally factor as

(3.75)

\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{0}\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}\mathrm{id}}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\xrightarrow{abs_{\ell,k}}\Sigma^{k-\ell}\mathit{KO},

where the maps
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}
are
\mathit{ko}
-module maps. In the same way, the map
\mathit{ABS}_{m}^{c}
from
3.70
factors through a
\mathit{ku}
-module map
\mathit{ku}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{m}\to\Sigma^{-m}\mathit{KU}
. ∎

3.5.
Free-to-interacting maps from ABS maps

To get from ABS maps to maps between groups of free fermion theories and deformation classes of invertible field theories, we Anderson-dualize. Recall from
2.17
that the Anderson dual to the sphere spectrum is the spectrum
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
defined as the fiber of the
exponential map

I_{\mathbb{C}}\to I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
. By
2.19
, invertible field theories are classified by Anderson-dual
\xi
-bordism: maps from
MT\xi
to a shift of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Meanwhile, free fermion theories are classified by
K
-theory, as discussed in
Section

2.1
for the case of finite group superalgebra symmetries. This classification may be recast in terms of Anderson-dual
K
-theory using the Anderson self-duality of
K
-theory:

Theorem 3.76

(
[
20
, Theorem
4.16]
)
.

There are equivalences
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Sigma^{4}\mathit{KO}
and
\varphi^{c}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{KU}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathit{KU}
of
\mathit{KO}
, resp.
\mathit{KU}
-modules.

Because
-1\colon\mathit{KO}\to\mathit{KO}
is a
\mathit{KO}
-module equivalence not homotopic to the identity,
\varphi
and
\varphi^{c}
are not unique. Choose one of each.
34
34

34

It is possible to choose
\varphi
and
\varphi^{c}
to be compatible with respect to the realification and complexification maps between
\mathit{KO}
and
\mathit{KU}
, which follows from the fact that
3.76
refines to a shifted Anderson self-duality of
\mathit{KR}

[
157
, Theorem 8.2]
.
The specific choice is not important for what follows, but we need to make
a
choice.

For proofs of
3.76
, see Anderson’s unpublished notes (
ibid.
), or any of Yosimura
[
340
, Theorem 4]
, Freed–Moore–Segal
[
119
, Proposition B.11]
, Heard–Stojanoska
[
157
, Theorem 8.1]
, Ricka
[
268
, Corollary
5.8]
, Greenlees–Meier
[
145
, Example 1.5]
, Greenlees–Stojanoska
[
146
, Example 6.1]
, or Hebestreit–Land–Nikolaus
[
159
, Example 2.8]
. (Some of these proofs only show an equivalence of spectra.) Heard–Stojanoska
[
157
, Theorem 8.2]
lifted
3.76
to a proof of shifted Anderson self-duality for
\mathit{KR}
-theory, with additional proofs given by Ricka
[
268
, Theorem 5.4]
and Greenlees–Meier
[
145
, Example 1.5]
.

Example 3.77
.

In our case of interest for the Bott spiral, free theories in dimension
d
with
\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k}
-symmetry are classified by the group
K_{2-d}(\mathit{C\ell}_{\ell,k})\cong\mathit{KO}^{d+\ell-k-2}(\mathrm{pt})
(see
2.6
). There is an identification
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{-d-\ell+k+2}\mathit{KO}\simeq\Sigma^{d+\ell-k+2}\mathit{KO}
.
∎

We may express Anderson self-duality as a map of spectra called the Pfaffian.

Definition 3.78

(
[
118
, §9.2.5]
,
[
128
, §11.3]
)
.

Consider the map
u\colon\mathbb{S}\to\mathit{KO}
representing
1\in\pi_{0}(\mathit{KO})
. By applying the Anderson self-duality
\varphi
from
3.76
,
u
induces a map
u^{\vee}\colon\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{-4}I_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The
real Pfaffian
is the map
\mathrm{Pfaff}\colon\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{4}I_{\mathbb{Z}}
obtained from
\Sigma^{8}(u^{\vee})\colon\Sigma^{8}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{4}I_{\mathbb{Z}}
by Bott periodicity.
35
35

35

Freed–Hopkins define their map in differential cohomology. This is important for non-topological invertible field theories: see Freed
[
128
, Lecture 9]
. We will not need this level of generality in this paper, hence work with ordinary generalized cohomology.

The Pfaffian is so-called because for a Dirac operator on a spin manifold, this map conjecturally gives the determinant line or Pfaffian line of the Dirac operator.
See
[
118
, §9.2.5]
for further physical discussion.

The free-to-interacting maps for the tenfold way, proposed by Freed–Hopkins, are the following.

Definition 3.79

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Conjecture 9.70]
)
.

Let
\mathit{ABS}_{s}\colon\mathit{MTH}(s)\to\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}
be the twisted ABS map of (
3.38
), (
3.43
), or (
3.47
), depending on
s
. The free-to-interacting map for symmetry type
H(s)
is the dualized map

(3.80)

\Sigma^{s-2}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow[\eqref{and_KO}]{\simeq}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO})\xrightarrow{I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{s}}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTH}(s).

Equivalently, for a fixed degree
d
, thought of as the spatial dimension, the free-to-interacting map is the map sending a free theory
x\in\mathit{KO}^{d+s-2}(\mathrm{pt})
to the class of the composition

(3.81)

\mathit{MTH}(s)\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{s}\wedge x}\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}\wedge\Sigma^{d+s-2}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow{\mu}\Sigma^{d-2}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Pfaff}}\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}

in
[MTH(s),\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
.

Ansatz 3.82

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9.2.6]
)
.

(1)

Under the identifications in
2.3
and
2.19
identifying the groups of
d
-dimensional free fermion Hamiltonians of Altland–Zirnbauer type
s
, resp. reflection-positive IFTs on
H(s)
-manifolds with
\mathit{KO}^{d+s-2}
, resp.
\mho_{H(s)}^{d+2}
, (
3.80
) is the homomorphism assigning to a free fermion Hamiltonian its low-energy effective field theory.

(2)

The same is true mutatis mutandis with
H(\mathrm{U}_{1}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}K_{f}(s))
in place of
H(s)
,
\mathit{KU}
in place of
\mathit{KO}
, and Freed–Hopkins’ complex analog of (
3.80
), written explicitly in
[
27
, (1.57)]
, in place of (
3.80
).

Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §9.3]
provide computational evidence supporting
3.82
.
[
27
, Ansatz 1.73, §3]
generalizes
3.82
to weak symmetry-protected topological phases and provides additional supporting evidence.

Example 3.83
.

As we discussed in
2.8
, the time-reversal-symmetric Majorana chain
[
196
]
is a
1
-dimensional free fermion phase with an
E_{1,0}
symmetry, i.e. fermion parity and a time-reversal symmetry squaring to
1
. It is nontorsion as a free fermion phase with respect to stacking. Fidkowski–Kitaev
[
111
,
112
]
36
36

36

See also
[
320
,
148
,
341
,
185
,
333
,
82
,
202
,
172
,
322
,
118
,
321
]
for additional derivations of this or closely related facts.
argued that, if we allow deformations by Hamiltonians with quartic terms, eight copies of this phase can be deformed to the trivial phase, thus giving the first example of a free fermion phase whose order as a free versus as an interacting phase differs.

To support
3.82
, we would like for the corresponding free-to-interacting map to be a surjective map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/8
.

Mathematically, this map is modeled by the case
s=1
of
3.79
on homotopy in degree
1
:

(3.84)

\mathit{KO}^{0}\cong\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{+1}}\mho^{2}_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8.

Freed–Hopkins computed
[
118
, Corollary 9.83]
that this map is a reduction modulo eight, reproducing the physical computation.
The low-energy TFT describing the interacting Majorana chain is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire IFT of
2.23
, and the deformation class of this IFT generates the group
\mho^{2}_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}

[
82
,
118
]
.
∎

Example 3.85
.

Consider the case where
n=4
and
s=-2
, where the ABS map has domain
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}}
and codomain
\mathit{KO}_{2}
. Theories on manifolds with pin
{}^{\tilde{c}+}
structures model physical theories with both a time reversal symmetry and particle number conservation, and include the famous topological insulators studied by Fu–Kane
[
131
]
and Fu–Kane–Mele
[
130
]
. Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Theorem 9.87]
computed the free-to-interacting maps in low dimensions for this symmetry group.
With Antolín Camarena, Pacheco-Tallaj, and Sheinbaum, we computed the ABS map in degree four on the level of manifold generators in
[
27
, Appendix A]
, with an application toward free-to-interacting maps for theories with discrete translation symmetry.

We found that the map

(3.86)

\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathit{ABS}_{-2}}\mathit{KO}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

sends the bordism generators
\mathbb{RP}^{4}
and
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
with appropriate pin
{}^{\tilde{c}+}
structures to
0\in\mathit{KO}_{2}
, while the class of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with pin
{}^{\tilde{c}+}
structure induced from its complex structure is sent to the generator of
\mathit{KO}_{2}
, which is dual to
\eta^{2}\in\mathit{KO}^{-2}
.
This means that the invertible field theory that arises from the unique nontrivial free theory has a nontrivial partition function when evaluated on the spacetime manifold
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.
∎

Equipped with the generalized ABS map (
3.62
), we may now define the free-to-interacting map for the symmetry types we built from the fermionic groups

from

.

On homotopy, this is the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge\ell}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma})^{\wedge k}}

mult

.

.
