,
\mathit{KU}
in place of
\mathit{KO}
, and the map
F2I_{m}^{c}
from
3.92
in place of
F2I_{\ell,k}
, where
m=\ell+k
.

Remark 3.97
.

As usual, discrete class C must be handled separately. Apply Anderson duality to the map
\mathit{ABS}_{s=4}^{\delta}
from
3.72
to obtain a map
F2I_{s=4}^{\delta}\colon\Sigma^{2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}))
. Following
3.96
we interpret
F2I_{s=4}^{\delta}
as the map sending a discrete class C free fermion theory to the interacting field theory it describes.
∎

Remark 3.98
.

In a completely analogous way, we may also define free-to-interacting maps for any twisted ABS map of the form in
3.54
by applying Anderson duality, and we predict analogously to
3.96
that these describe the process of obtaining a low-energy IFT from a free fermion Hamiltonian for a more general class of symmetries.
∎

Remark 3.99
.

By applying Anderson duality to
3.74
, we see that the maps
F2I_{\ell,k}
, as well as
F2I_{s=4}^{\delta}
, factor as the composition of a map
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}\colon\Sigma^{-k+\ell-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
, followed by the Anderson dual of the ABS map, and analogously in the complex case.
A similar factorization exists for the general case mentioned in the previous remark.
∎

Remark 3.100
.

Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli
[
230
, §IV.I]
use methods quite different from the ones in this paper to study a free-to-interacting map for 2d phases with symmetry given by the fermionic group
G_{f}
with
G_{b}=\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
and twisting data
(x,x^{2}+xy)
, where
x
and
y
are as in
1.116
. Similarly to the proof of
1.118
, one can use an automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
to prove that
G_{f}\cong E_{1,1}
as fermionic groups, so we can check whether our map
F2I_{1,1}
matches Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli’s.

Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli (
ibid.
) argue that in 2d, the free-to-interacting map is a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
. By
6.15
and the computation
\mho^{3}_{1,1}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8
in
[
182
, Appendix E]
, our model for this free-to-interacting map,
\pi_{-2}(F2I_{1,1})\colon\mathit{KO}^{0}\to\mho_{\mathrm{DPin}}^{3}
, is also a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
. Thus our model agrees with Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli’s result, providing evidence supporting
3.96
.
∎

Remark 3.101
.

Ryu
[
274
, §3.1]
and Rosch
[
269
]
study free-to-interacting maps for 2-, resp. 1-dimensional phases with symmetries given by the fermionic groups
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, resp.
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{+}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, where
\theta
vanishes on the
\mathbb{Z}/2
factor. One can directly check that these correspond to the tangential structures
\mathrm{Spin}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, resp.
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
(see
[
27
, Proposition 1.26]
). Ryu, resp. Rosch provide physical arguments that the free-to-interacting maps are surjective maps of the form
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
, resp.
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/4
.

Though we have not defined free-to-interacting maps for these symmetries, our computations suggest the following models for these maps:

(3.102a)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-2}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow[\eqref{eqn:Elk_F2I}]{\Sigma^{-1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{1,0}}\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\xrightarrow[\eqref{first_smith_isom}]{I_{\mathbb{Z}}e_{\sigma}}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+})

(3.102b)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow[\eqref{eqn:Elk_F2I}]{\Sigma^{-1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{2,0}}\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{2,0})\xrightarrow[\eqref{first_smith_isom}]{I_{\mathbb{Z}}e_{\sigma}}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}).

In (
3.102a
),
e_{\sigma}
is smashed with
MT\mathrm{Spin}
before applying
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
; in (
3.102b
),
e_{\sigma}
is smashed with
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}
.

Applying
\pi_{-2}
, resp.
\pi_{-1}
to these maps, we obtain maps
\mathit{KO}^{0}\to\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{3}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
\mathit{KO}^{0}\to\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By construction of
e_{\sigma}
, the images of these maps lie in the reduced versions of these Anderson-dualized bordism groups; see
[
80
, §7.1]
. There are isomorphisms
\widetilde{\mho}_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{3}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/8

[
224
]
and
\widetilde{\mho}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
(
[
161
, Theorem 3.2.1]
or
[
150
, Theorem 16]
), and Freed–Hopkins’ computation of
F2I_{1,0}

[
118
, Corollary 9.85]
and our computation in
6.15
of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{1,1}
, together with the fact that
I_{\mathbb{Z}}e_{\sigma}
is an isomorphism onto the reduced
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTH}
-cohomology of
B\mathbb{Z}/2
(see
3.25
and
[
80
, §7.1]
), implies that on
\pi_{-2}
, resp.
\pi_{-1}
, the maps in (
3.102
) are surjections
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
, resp.
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/4
, onto the reduced Anderson-dualized bordism groups. This matches Ryu and Rosch up to the second
\mathbb{Z}
free fermion summand of the former, which we interpret as Ryu allowing fermions to transform in a larger class of representations of the symmetry group.

In both cases, the reduced classification is a strict subset of the total classification, as
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}^{2}(\mathrm{pt})
and
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{3}(\mathrm{pt})
are nonzero
[
118
, Corollaries 9.81 and 9.85]
; following
3.98
we interpret these as representing intrinsically interacting SPT phases that Ryu and Rosch’s methods do not detect.
∎

4.
Modeling the Bott spiral

We now apply the mathematical framework of the previous sections to the physical situation
of the
Bott spiral

of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern
[
263
]
.
In contrast to the “Bott clock” or periodic table of free fermion phases in the tenfold way, where the classification of free SPTs (
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
, or
0
) repeats periodically in the dimension and symmetry type, the Bott spiral consists of interacting SPTs whose classification order grows with the dimension. For example, Kitaev’s Bott clock
[
197
]
reports that the groups of free fermion phases in class CII in dimensions
1
and
9
are both isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. But Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern report that the corresponding interacting phases form a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in dimension
1
and
\mathbb{Z}/32
in dimension
9
.

There are two key differences between the Bott spiral and the Bott clock. First, the phases in the Bott spiral are interacting; specifically, they are interacting SPTs that are adiabatically connected to free fermion models. This means that in our homotopical model, these phases should lie in the image of free-to-interacting maps.
Second, the symmetry types in the Bott spiral are a mild generalization of those in the tenfold way: half of the Altland–Zirnbauer classes considered are
primed
, meaning that an additional
E_{1,1}
-symmetry is present.

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern computed the classification of SPTs in three different spirals: starting from spatial dimension
d=1
in either class BDI, CII, or AIII, since these are the three cases in which the classification of free SPTs is a
\mathbb{Z}
. Their methods included a form of
dimensional reduction
that relates an SPT in spatial dimension
d
with an SPT in dimension
d+1
with a modified symmetry type, interchanging primed and unprimed classes as one goes up the spiral.
This approach allowed them to reduce to dimension 0, where they studied explicit interacting Hamiltonians and computed their classification orders via a similar approach to that of Fidkowski–Kitaev in the original class BDI case
[
111
]
.
Their results are as follows.

Physical Result 4.1

(
[
263
, (1)]
)
.

Consider the minimal chiral Dirac Hamiltonian in spatial dimension
d
whose dimensional reduction to
d=1
is a generator of the group of free SPT phases in class BDI, CII, or AIII.
The subgroup of interacting SPTs generated by the interacting class of this Hamiltonian is a cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}/n
, where

(4.2a)

n=\begin{cases}4\cdot 2^{\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor}\cdot\mu&\text{for class BDI in $d=1$}\\
2\cdot 2^{\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor}\cdot\mu&\text{for class CII in $d=1$}\\
4\cdot 2^{\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor}&\text{for class AIII in $d=1$}\end{cases}

and

(4.2b)

\mu=\begin{cases}2&\text{for class BDI, D${}^{\prime}$, or DIII}\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Example 4.3
.

Let us spell out
4.1
a bit, following
[
263
, Figure 1]
.

(1)

If one dimensionally reduces to class BDI in
d=1
, (
4.2a
) gives a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/8}
classification of
d=1
BDI phases, a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/8}
of
d=2

\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
phases, a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/16}
of 3d DIII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/8
of 4d
\mathrm{AII}^{\prime}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/16
of 5d CII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/16
of 6d
\mathrm{CII}^{\prime}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/32
of 7d CI phases, and so on.

(2)

If one dimensionally reduces to class CII in
d=1
, (
4.2a
) gives a
\mathbb{Z}/2
of 1d CII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
of 2d
\mathrm{C^{\prime}}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of 3d CI phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of 3d CI phases, a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/16}
of 5d BDI phases, a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/16}
of 6d
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
phases, a
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/32}
of 7d DIII phases, and so on.

(3)

If one dimensionally reduces to class AIII in
d=1
, (
4.2b
) gives a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of 1d AIII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of 2d
\mathrm{A^{\prime}}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/8
of 3d AIII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/8
of 4d
\mathrm{A^{\prime}}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/16
of 5d AIII phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/16
of 6d
\mathrm{A^{\prime}}
phases, a
\mathbb{Z}/32
of AIII phases, and so on.

In these examples, underlined groups correspond to
\mu=2
, and the remaining cases to
\mu=1
.
∎

If we ignore the effect of
\mu
, which distinguishes triplet superconductors, this classification doubles in size in every other dimension step. In particular, this is true for the AIII series, which does not use
\mu
.

One of our goals is to reproduce this result and explain it by alternative methods, providing a test of our homotopical ansatz.
To do so, we use
3.82
and
3.96
to model the passage from a free fermion theory to the corresponding interacting theory as the action of the free-to-interacting maps of
3.87
and
3.92
. We model the interacting theory through the (deformation class of) its low-energy reflection-positive IFT, following
2.11
.
Then, to determine the group of interacting SPTs connected to these free fermion models, it suffices to compute the image of the free-to-interacting map.
Our mathematical version of
4.1
is given in
4.27
and
4.21
.

Additionally, motivated by work of Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §5]
, who noted the relationship between the order of the large 2-torsion summands in the 4-periodic family of Smith homomorphisms of
3.25
, we also define an invertible field theory model for the
dimensional reduction
process. To avoid overloading the term,
37
37

37

See
0.13
.
we will henceforth call the maps of invertible field theories modeling this process
spiral maps
(
4.51
). We actually define two different maps for this process, whose application depends on whether we begin in a primed phase or unprimed phase.

Consider the complex cases; i.e. the spiral starting from class AIII. As an immediate corollary of
4.1
, the interacting classification order doubles at every even-to-odd dimension step. For this reason, we desire that our complex spiral maps are a multiplication by two at every even-to-odd dimension step and an isomorphism in the other cases. In
4.64
, we observe that this is the case, and in
4.67
we observe the real analog.

4.1.
Primed Altland–Zirnbauer classes

The classification of free fermion phases in a tenfold way class in a certain dimension corresponds to one of the groups in the
\mathit{KO}
-theory or
\mathit{KU}
-theory of a point. For simplicity, we first consider the cases (
\mathit{KU}^{0}
,
\mathit{KO}^{0}
, and
\mathit{KO}^{-4}
) where this group is
\mathbb{Z}
.

Tenfold way free fermion phases classified by
\mathbb{Z}
in even dimensions host chiral edge modes, which are gapless.
In order to successively produce lower-dimensional
gapped
theories,
Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern define
primed
Altland–Zirnbauer classes, generalizing the definitions of class D
′
and class DIII+
\mathcal{R}
put forward in
[
273
,
262
,
336
]
, and related to Song–Schnyder’s extended Altland–Zirnbauer-style classification in
[
286
, §II.A.1]
.
To physically produce a primed Altland–Zirnbauer class, one stacks two systems in the same Altland–Zirnbauer class with opposite invariants. Normally, these invariants would add and the phases would cancel, but to prevent this one imposes an additional symmetry formed from an antiunitary symmetry combined with a reflection symmetry.

Correspondingly, to form the symmetry group of a primed class,
one starts with the symmetry group of the original class and enlarges it by adding generators
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
.
Here

\mathcal{T}
is an internal symmetry that is antiunitary, squares to the identity, and commutes with the rest of the symmetry group, while
\mathcal{R}
is a unitary symmetry that squares to
(-1)^{F}
and also commutes with the rest of the symmetry group.
Our mathematical model for primed phases encodes the additional symmetry generators
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
as the generators of the fermionic group
E_{1,1}
of
1.89
:

Definition 4.4
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group. Define
G_{f}^{\prime}\coloneqq G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,1}
.

In our mathematical model, if
G_{f}
describes a physical symmetry type, the
primed
symmetry type will be described by
G_{f}^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.5
.

Recall the discussion of the failure of Morita invariance for interacting SPTs from
Section

2.3
.
While the
\mathit{C\ell}_{1,1}
periodicity of
\mathit{KO}
-theory (
1.86
) ensures that the free classifications of primed and unprimed phases are isomorphic, the same is not true for the interacting classifications.
The tangential structure
H(G_{f}\hat{\times}E_{1,1})
is measurably different from
H(G_{f})
: as explained in
2.35
, the corresponding bordism groups and, dually, the groups of IFTs, are
not
the same.
∎

Example 4.6
.

The simplest example of a primed class is class D
′
. For both the discrete and continuous forms of Altland–Zirnbauer class D (see
2.24
and
1
),
G_{b}=1
and
G_{f}=E_{0,0}=\mathrm{Spin}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}/2
with trivial grading and nontrivial
(-1)^{F}
, so for class D
′
we have
G_{f}^{\prime}\cong E_{1,1}
. Recall from
1.89
that
the fermionic group algebra
\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,1}]\cong\mathit{C\ell}_{1,1}\cong M_{1|1}(\mathbb{R})
is Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
, which is the fermionic group algebra corresponding to class D.
Correspondingly,

K_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{f}[E_{1,1}])\cong K_{p}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathit{KO}_{p}
, and the classification of free class D
′
phases is the same as that for free class D phases.

On the interacting side, there is a clear difference:
E_{1,1}
corresponds to spin-
(1,1)
structures (or dpin structures by
1.118
), while
\mathbb{R}
corresponds to spin structures. For example, in spatial dimension 2, interacting class D superconductors are classified by
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}

[
118
, Corollary 9.81]
, while interacting class D
′
superconductors are classified by
\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{DPin}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8

[
182
, Appendix E]
.
38
38

38

See
[
293
, §4]
for another fermionic group,
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}^{+}
with
\theta
trivial, giving rise to the same class D classification for free fermion phases and groups of IFTs not isomorphic to the groups of spin nor dpin IFTs.

∎

Example 4.7
.

In
Section

2.3
, we discussed how our discrete model for class C was exceptional because it was not of the form
E_{\ell,k}
. Thanks to
2.29
, however,
the discrete class
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
fermionic group is isomorphic to
E_{0,4}
and
E_{4,0}
.
∎

Example 4.8
.

The only complex primed class we will need to consider is class A
′
. As for class D, we have
G_{f}^{\prime}=E_{1,1}
, but now the complex fermionic group algebra is
\mathbb{C}^{f}[E_{1,1}]\cong\mathbb{C}\ell_{2}
, oblivious to the signature of the quadratic form.
From
1.121
, we also know that the Thom spectrum of the primed symmetry type simplifies as

(4.9)

MT{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,1}\simeq MT{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{2}.

In
6.86
, we show that
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}(\mathit{ME}_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
, but
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

[
118
, Corollary 9.89]
.
∎

4.1.1.
Crystalline equivalence principle for primed classes

According to Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern, primed classes may be defined assuming that
\mathcal{R}
is an internal unitary
\mathbb{Z}/2
-symmetry (following D
′
as defined by Qi
[
262
]
and Ryu–Zhang
[
273
]
), or alternatively a spatial
\mathbb{Z}/2
reflection symmetry (following DIII+
\mathcal{R}
as defined by Yao–Ryu
[
336
]
). In this section, we show that these two definitions agree, using
the
crystalline equivalence principle
(CEP).
This principle, introduced by Thorngren–Else
[
315
]
, posits an equivalence between the classifications of invertible phases respecting a
G
-symmetry acting on space and those of invertible phases with a purely internal
G
-symmetry. The details of the internal symmetry depend on the specific
G
-action on space, how it mixes with time-reversal or other internal symmetries, etc. In particular, for fermionic phases, through the work of many groups of researchers
[
116
,
150
,
233
,
346
,
89
,
281
,
344
,
67
,
170
,
343
,
347
,
230
,
345
,
201
,
212
,
231
,
266
,
285
,
282
,
297
,
68
,
220
,
213
,
229
]
, it has become clear that the fermionic groups acting on the spatial and internal sides have the same
G_{b}
but different associated twists.

Of the various formulations of the fermionic crystalline equivalence principle in the above references, we found the proposal of Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli
[
230
, §III.A]
most applicable to the problem at hand.

Let
\mathrm{Euc}_{d}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{d}\rtimes\mathrm{O}_{d}
denote the group of isometries of
d
-dimensional Euclidean space and
p\colon\mathrm{Euc}_{d}\to\mathrm{O}_{d}
denote the quotient by the normal subgroup of translations.
The data that goes into Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli’s ansatz is a (not necessarily compact) fermionic group
G_{f}
and a group homomorphism
\lambda\colon G_{b}\to\mathrm{Euc}_{d}
, where
d
is the dimension of space. This data captures both how the symmetry group acts on space (through
\lambda
) as well as how it mixes with fermion parity (through
G_{f}\to G_{b}
).

Definition 4.10

(Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli
[
230
, (30)]
)
.

Let
G_{f}
be a fermionic group with associated bosonic group
G_{b}
and
\lambda\colon G_{b}\to\mathrm{Euc}_{d}
be a homomorphism. Let
(\theta,\omega)
be the associated twist of
G_{f}
. Define another fermionic group
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}
to have the bosonic group
G_{b}
and the associated twist
(\theta^{\mathrm{int}},\omega^{\mathrm{int}})
given by the following formulas:

(4.11a)

\displaystyle\theta^{\mathrm{int}}

\displaystyle=\theta+(p\circ\lambda)^{*}(w_{1})

(4.11b)

\displaystyle\omega^{\mathrm{int}}

\displaystyle=\omega+(p\circ\lambda)^{*}(w_{1})\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{int}}+(p\circ\lambda)^{*}(w_{2}).

This defines
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}
only up to isomorphism, but that will suffice for our applications in this paper.

Ansatz 4.12

(Fermionic CEP of Manjunath–Calvera–Barkeshli
[
230
, §III.A]
)
.

Given a fermionic group
G_{f}
and a group homomorphism
\lambda\colon G_{b}\to\mathrm{Euc}_{d}
, the abelian group of
d
-dimensional invertible interacting fermionic phases with symmetry group
G_{f}
acting on space by
\lambda
is isomorphic to the group of
d
-dimensional interacting invertible phases with symmetry group
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}
.

Where this overlaps in scope with the proposals of
[
116
,
89
]
, the three proposals make equivalent predictions. In general, their predictions are in line with what has been computed in the physics literature by other methods. Compare for example the computations in
[
89
,
343
]
.

Let us start from the perspective that
\mathcal{R}
is a reflection symmetry.
If
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathrm{Euc}_{d}
is the inclusion of a reflection
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathrm{proj}_{3}\colon A\times B\times C\to C
is the projection onto the third factor, then the spatial symmetry data is that of the group
G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,1}
acting spatially by

(4.13)

\lambda=\rho\circ\mathrm{proj}_{3}\colon(G_{f}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,1})_{b}=G_{b}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\mathrm{proj}_{3}}\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathrm{Euc}_{d}.

Proposition 4.14
.

If
(G_{f},\lambda)
are as in (
4.13
) above, then there is an isomorphism of fermionic groups
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}\cong G_{f}^{\prime}
.

In other words, the fermionic group is agnostic as to whether
\mathcal{R}
is spatial or internal.

Proof.

Both
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}
and
G_{f}^{\prime}
have bosonic group isomorphic to
G_{b}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, so it suffices to use
1.12
and (
4.11
) to compute their associated twists. Specifically, if
(\theta,\omega)\in H^{1}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H^{2}(BG_{b};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the associated twist for
G_{f}
, and
x_{1}
, resp.
x_{2}
represent the classes in
H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
corresponding to the first, resp. second factors of
\mathbb{Z}/2
, then
1.12
implies the associated twist of
G_{f}^{\prime}
is

(4.15a)

(\theta+x_{1}+x_{2},\,\omega+\theta(x_{1}+x_{2})+x_{1}^{2})

and (
4.11
) implies the associated twist for
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}
is

(4.15b)

(\theta+x_{1},\,\omega+\theta x_{1}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}).

Though
\eqref{primetwist}\neq\eqref{inttwist}
, they differ by an automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. Specifically, the automorphism defined by extending
(1,0)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(1,1)
and
(0,1)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(0,1)

\mathbb{Z}/2
-linearly sends
x_{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}x_{1}+x_{2}
and
x_{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}x_{2}
, and one can check that this carries the data (
4.15b
) to the data (
4.15a
). Therefore this automorphism lifts to an isomorphism of fermionic groups
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}\xrightarrow{\cong}G_{f}^{\prime}
.
∎

Example 4.16
.

Return to class D
′
.
By the discussion above, this also corresponds to the spatial fermionic symmetry generated by commuting operators
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
, with
\mathcal{T}^{2}=1
and
\mathcal{R}^{2}=(-1)^{F}
, such that
\mathcal{T}
is antiunitary and
\mathcal{R}
is a reflection.

Qi
[
262
]
, Ryu–Zhang
[
273
]
, and Yao–Ryu
[
336
]
studied (2+1)d SPT phases with this fermionic symmetry data; they found a
\mathbb{Z}
classification of free fermion phases, which maps surjectively onto a
\mathbb{Z}/8
group of interacting phases. These SPTs were subsequently studied by several collaborations, including
[
147
,
70
,
246
,
274
,
263
,
286
,
338
,
10
,
230
]
, who reproduced Yao–Ryu’s results by different methods. Thus we can give another derivation of this
\mathbb{Z}/8
classification using the CEP to identify it with
\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{DPin}}\cong\mho^{3}_{1,1}
, which was calculated to be
\mathbb{Z}/8
in
[
182
, Appendix E]
.
We also get a mathematical model for the class
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
free-to-interacting map: first apply
F2I_{1,1}\colon\Sigma^{-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,1})
, then apply the fermionic CEP to identify the codomain with the spectrum classifying phases in class
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
. By our computations in
6.15
, this is indeed a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
in dimension
2
.
∎

Example 4.17
.

Generalizing
4.16
, there are several more examples in the physics literature of computations of free-to-interacting maps for a symmetry group
G_{f}
that acts on space as above such that under the fermionic CEP (
4.12
),
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}\cong E_{\ell,k}
for some
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
. Therefore in these cases we may mathematically model the free-to-interacting map as
F2I_{\ell,k}
followed by the CEP identification, like we did in
4.16
. This includes the following examples.

(1)

Cho–Hsieh–Morimoto–Ryu
[
70
, §IV.D]
and Song–Schnyder
[
286
]
study the free-to-interacting map for class “
\mathrm{BDI}+R_{++}
,” given by a time-reversal symmetry
\mathcal{T}
and a spatial reflection symmetry
\mathcal{R}
that commute and both square to
1
. Using methods different from this paper’s and from each other, Cho–Hsieh–Morimoto–Ryu compute the free-to-interacting map in 2d and Song–Schnyder compute it in all dimensions. They conclude that the free-to-interacting map has image
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4k+3}
in dimensions
d=8k+2
,
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4k+4}
in dimensions
d=8k+6
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
in dimensions
d=8k
and
8k+1
, and
0
otherwise. Under
4.12
, we get
G_{f}^{\mathrm{int}}\cong E_{1,1}
again, so just like in
4.16
,
6.15
computes that our free-to-interacting map
F2I_{1,1}
has the same behavior as the one in in
[
70
,
286
]
. These authors do not discuss the cokernel of the free-to-interacting map.

(2)

Morimoto–Furusaki–Mudry
[
246
, §IV.B]
and Yoshida–Furusaki
[
339
, §III]
consider three-dimensional phases in class “
\mathrm{AII}+R
,” arguing that the corresponding free-to-interacting map is a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
. In more detail, they consider the continuous class AII fermionic group
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}^{-}
together with a spatial reflection symmetry
R
squaring to
(-1)^{F}
and commuting with the internal
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}^{-}
symmetry. Passing through the fermionic CEP (
4.12
), one obtains the tangential structure
\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,0}
. We model the corresponding free-to-interacting map by the “mixed discrete-continuous F2I map” defined in
6.40
; roughly speaking, it is the map obtained by combining Freed–Hopkins’ free-to-interacting map for
\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}

[
118
, §9.2]
(
3.79
, case
s=-2
) with our
F2I_{1,0}
(
3.87
):

(4.18)

F2I_{1,0,-2}\colon\Sigma\mathit{KO}\xrightarrow{\eqref{eqn:mixed_F2I}}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,0}).

Our model for the free-to-interacting map in
[
246
,
339
]
is
\pi_{-3}
of (
4.18
). In
6.49
, we show
\pi_{-3}F2I_{1,0,-2}
is a map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{s}
, for some
s>0
, which surjects onto the
\mathbb{Z}/8
summand. Thus our model for this free-to-interacting map agrees with Morimoto--Furusaki--Mudry and Yoshida--Furusaki’s; they do not discuss the cokernel.
39
39

39

By studying the Anderson–Brown–Peterson-style splitting for
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}
in
6.31
more carefully, one can prove that
s=3
, predicting a
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}
of intrinsically interacting invertible phases for this collection of symmetries.

Tin telluride (
\mathrm{SnTe}
) is predicted to be in class
\mathrm{AII}+R
: see
[
167
,
98
,
310
,
334
,
252
]
and
[
246
, §IV.C]
. It would be interesting to determine whether the behavior of this class’s free-to-interacting map could be studied experimentally.

(3)

Song–Schnyder
[
286
]
studied the free-to-interacting map in all dimensions for many symmetry classes including spatial reflections or twofold rotations. Seven of these correspond under the fermionic CEP (
4.12
) to free-to-interacting maps newly defined in our paper:
\mathrm{BDI}+R_{++}
as studied above in part (
1
), and also
\mathrm{AIII}+R_{+}
,
\mathrm{AI}+R_{+}
,
\mathrm{DIII}+R_{++}
,
\mathrm{AII}+R_{+}
,
\mathrm{CII}+R_{++}
, and
\mathrm{CI}+R_{++}
. Each of has an internal symmetry group given by the continuous fermionic group
K_{f}(s)
(see
Table

1
) from the corresponding Altland–Zirnbauer class, together with a spatial reflection commuting with the internal symmetry and squaring to
1
. Under
4.12
, the corresponding internal symmetry group is
K_{f}(s)\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{-}
, so we can model Song–Schnyder’s free-to-interacting maps by the maps we defined in
3.87
,
3.92
, and
6.40
. In order left-to-right, then top-to-bottom, the six remaining examples are modeled by

(4.19)

\displaystyle F2I_{2}\colon

\displaystyle\mathit{KU}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{2})\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle F2I_{0,1,-2}\colon

\displaystyle\Sigma^{3}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1})

\displaystyle F2I_{0,2}\colon

\displaystyle\Sigma^{4}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,2})

\displaystyle F2I_{0,1,2}\colon

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1})

\displaystyle F2I_{0,1,-3}\colon

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1})

\displaystyle F2I_{0,1,3}\colon

\displaystyle\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1}).

The singly-indexed maps
F2I_{m}
are the ones from
3.92
; the doubly-indexed maps
F2I_{\ell,k}
are from
3.87
, and the triply-indexed maps
F2I_{\ell,k,s}
are from
6.40
.

In
6.15
,
6.49
, and
6.82
, we computed these maps, and comparing those results with
[
286
, Table 1]
, we see complete agreement in the cases we consider.

We can also model Song–Schnyder’s
\mathrm{AIII}+R_{-}
example, consisting of an internal
\mathrm{Pin}_{1}^{c}
symmetry together with a spatial reflection squaring to
1
and
anti
commuting with time-reversal, in a manner similar to that of
3.101
. After applying the fermionic CEP, we obtain the tangential structure
\mathrm{Pin}^{c}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, so following (
3.102
), we can define a free-to-interacting map to be the composition

(4.20)

\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KU}\xrightarrow[\eqref{complex_F2I_map_defn}]{\Sigma^{-1}I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ABS}_{2}}\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{2})\xrightarrow[\eqref{first_smith_isom}]{I_{\mathbb{Z}}e_{\sigma}}\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{c}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+})

together with the identification in the fermionic CEP. Combining our computation of
F2I_{2}
in
6.82
with a similar argument to the one in
3.101
, one can show that on
\pi_{-d}
, corresponding to
d
-dimensional phases, the image of (
4.20
) consists of
\mathbb{Z}/2^{k+2}
if
d=2k+1
and is
0
if
d
is even, matching what Song–Schnyder obtain. As usual, they do not discuss the cokernels of their free-to-interacting maps.

There are also a number of examples in the literature, including some of those studied in
[
173
,
286
,
213
]
, where applying the fermionic CEP (
4.12
) produces one of the ten continuous fermionic symmetry groups
K_{f}(s)
(see
Table

1
), and Freed–Hopkins’ corresponding free-to-interacting map
[
118
, §9.2]
(
3.79
) produces isomorphic domain and image groups in all dimensions to the example. As these are accounted for by previous literature, we do not go into the details.
∎

Despite these examples, in general, work of Sheinbaum–Antolín Camarena
[
282
]
shows that one must be careful when discussing the CEP in the context of the free-to-interacting map.

4.2.
Image of the free-to-interacting map for primed phases

Equipped with our model for primed symmetry types and with the free-to-interacting maps for
E_{\ell,k}
- and
E_{m}
-type twists (
3.87
and
3.92
, resp.), we can now describe our mathematical version of
4.1
: the interacting classification order
n
is given by the order of the image of the free-to-interacting map of the appropriate symmetry type.
We compute these orders in
Section

5
and
Section

6
, and present a summary of our results here.

First, consider the real cases. The following is a corollary of
5.58
and
6.15
.
Note that
d
should be interpreted as the spatial dimension of the initial free fermion SPT, while
\ell
and
k
set the symmetry type to be associated with the fermionic group
E_{\ell,k}
.
The variable
i
tracks
\ell\bmod 4
, while
\widetilde{m}
is an auxiliary variable used to simplify the theorem statement; essentially,
\widetilde{m}
tracks how many times we have gone fully around the spiral.

Recall the definition of
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
from
0.18
.

Corollary 4.21
.

Let
\widetilde{m}\coloneqq\lfloor(d-\ell-k+i+1)/8\rfloor
, where
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
with
i\equiv\ell\bmod 4
. If
d\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-2
, the image of the type-
E_{\ell,k}
free-to-interacting map

(4.22)

F2I_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{KO}^{d+\ell-k-2}\to\mho^{d+2}_{\mathrm{Spin}}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})

is

(4.23)

\mathrm{im}\,(F2I_{\ell,k})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2^{4+4\widetilde{m}-i}&d\equiv\ell+k-2i+2\bmod 8\\
\mathbb{Z}/2^{5+4\widetilde{m}-i}&d\equiv\ell+k-2i+6\bmod 8\\
\mathbb{Z}/2&d\equiv\ell+k-2i\bmod 8\text{ or }\ell+k-2i+1\bmod 8.\end{cases}

If
d<\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-2
,
F2I_{\ell,k}
vanishes in degree
d
.

We give many more details in
Section

5
and
Section

6
.
Here, note that the first two cases describe the breakdown of a
\mathbb{Z}
’s worth of free phases into a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{n}
’s worth of interacting phases, which are the cases considered in
[
263
]
.
We also computed the third case, for which the domain of the free-to-interacting map is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
instead. In these cases, the free-to-interacting map is actually injective.

Remark 4.24
.

We don’t compute the entirety of
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
—as we describe in
5.10
, we leave out some IFTs that do not correspond to interesting fermionic SPTs, which substantially simplifies the computation. As we show in
5.58
, the image of the free-to-interacting map never intersects these uninteresting IFTs nontrivially, so for the purposes of modeling the Bott spiral, this suffices:
Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s results do not fully characterize the interacting classification of phases, but only those phases connected to free fermion models (i.e. those in the image of the F2I map).
∎

To illustrate the theorem statement, we will consider a couple of examples.

Example 4.25
.

Return to the running example: class BDI superconductors. As discussed in
Section

2.3
, the usual choice of fermionic group for the symmetry class BDI is
E_{1,0}
. Setting
\ell=1
,
k=0
, and setting
i=1
to match
\ell\bmod 4
, we can read off the results. When
d\equiv-1,0\bmod 8
, the image is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
, while when
d=1
, for example, we observe the
\mathbb{Z}/2^{3}=\mathbb{Z}/8
interacting classification generated by the TRS Majorana chain (see e.g.
[
111
]
).

This result recapitulates the computation of
[
118
, Corollary 9.85]
and the physics references cited therein for
d=-1
to
d=4
, since indeed our free-to-interacting map for
E_{1,0}
matches their pin
-
free-to-interacting map by definition. Although it is not directly relevant for condensed matter applications, our computation holds for arbitrarily high dimensions
d
. Above dimension
d=4
, we see for example
\mathbb{Z}/2
images in
d=7,8
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{7}=\mathbb{Z}/128
in
d=9
, and so on. We see the
16
-fold increase in the classification at each increase in eight dimensions for dimensions
1,5\bmod 8
.
∎

Example 4.26
.

Consider now class D
′
superconductors, for which we set
\ell=k=1
and
i=1
. Then,
\widetilde{m}=\lfloor d/8\rfloor
and we can read off that the image of
F2I_{1,1}
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in spatial dimensions 0 and 1, a
\mathbb{Z}/8
in
d=2
, zero in the next three dimensions, and a
\mathbb{Z}/16
in
d=6
. For
d\equiv 2,6\bmod 8
, there is an increase in the classification order by an order of
16
every eight dimension steps.

This example is related to the previous one. In particular, there is an isomorphism between the
\mathbb{Z}/8
classification of class D
′
interacting phases in
d=2
(studied e.g. by
[
262
,
336
,
263
]
) and the
\mathbb{Z}/8
classification of class BDI interacting phases in
d=1
; the latter is a
dimensional reduction
of the first. We discuss this more in the next subsection.
∎

The complex cases follow a similar but simpler pattern.
The following is a corollary of
6.82
, which we prove in
Section

6.3.2
.

Corollary 4.27
.

The image of the type-
E_{m}
free-to-interacting map

(4.28a)

F2I_{m}\colon\mathit{KU}^{d+m-2}\to\mho^{d+2}_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(\mathit{ME}_{m})

is

(4.28b)

\mathrm{im}\,(F2I_{m}^{c})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2^{2+(d-m)/2},&d-m\text{\rm{} even, }d-m\geq-2\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

Example 4.29
.

Consider class AIII, for which we take
m=1
.
4.27
implies that in odd dimensions
d\geq-1
, the free-to-interacting map has domain
\mathbb{Z}
and surjects onto a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{(d+3)/2}
summand: a
\mathbb{Z}/2
for
d=-1
,
\mathbb{Z}/4
for
d=1
,
\mathbb{Z}/8
for
d=3
, etc. For
d
even the free-to-interacting map is trivial in degree
d
. Looking at the homotopy groups of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1}\simeq MT\mathrm{Pin}^{c}
computed by Bahri–Gilkey
[
35
, Theorem 0.2(b)]
, we see that this free-to-interacting map is surjective in degrees
2
and below:
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}(\mathit{ME}_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
, and the second summand is not in the image of
F2I_{1}
. In higher dimensions, the cokernel is even larger.

This free-to-interacting map coincides with Freed–Hopkins’ free-to-interacting map
\Sigma\mathit{KU}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{c})

[
118
, §9.2.4]
,
akin to the real case from
3.64
. Thus
4.27
recovers as a special case Freed–Hopkins’ computation of their free-to-interacting map in low degrees
[
118
, Corollary 9.91]
; as usual their
n
is our
d+1
. The computations for
m>1
are new.
∎

Remark 4.30
.

Consider the cases connected to the class AIII insulator in
d=1
. Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern argue that the interacting classification order relies on the dimension of the minimal massless Dirac Hamiltonian with chiral symmetry, which is
\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
for the operator acting in
d
spatial dimensions.

To unpack this a bit, consider the algebra
\mathbb{C}\ell_{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}\ell_{1}
, which is physically represented by the gamma matrices and a chiral symmetry operator.
The complex spinor representation is the minimal-dimensional representation of
(\mathbb{C}\ell_{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}\ell_{1})_{\mathit{ev}}\cong\mathbb{C}\ell_{d}
.

For
d=2k
, there is an isomorphism of superalgebras
\mathbb{C}\ell_{2k}\cong\End(\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\mid 2^{k-1}})
, which as an ungraded algebra is isomorphic to the matrix algebra
M_{2^{k}}(\mathbb{C})
. The minimal-dimensional representation is given by acting as matrices on
\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\mid 2^{k-1}}
.
For
d=2k+1
, there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{C}\ell_{2k+1}\cong\mathbb{C}\ell_{2k}\otimes\mathbb{C}\ell_{1}\cong\End(\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\mid 2^{k-1}})\oplus\End(\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\mid 2^{k-1}})
, and the minimal-dimensional representation acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\mid 2^{k-1}}
. ∎

4.3.
Spiraling out

In this section, we develop an invertible field theory model for the dimensional reduction procedure used by Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern to derive their classification.
With this model, we not only recover the physical picture, but also clarify the role of Bott periodicity on the interacting side, recognizing the 16-fold multiplication that occurs going fully around the spiral as being induced by taking a product with a Bott manifold.

Here, we define the spiral maps of IFTs and describe their behavior—the actual computation of these maps appears in
Section

5
.
We begin by providing the motivation for our construction of the spiral maps: Smith homomorphisms and symmetry breaking.

4.3.1.
Smith homomorphisms and symmetry breaking

In
Section

3.2
, we defined Smith homomorphisms as the homomorphisms of bordism groups induced by the zero section map of a given vector bundle. Anderson-dualizing such maps produces group homomorphisms of (deformation classes of) invertible field theories, which by the ansatz discussed in
Section

2.2
model interacting SPTs.

In this section, we follow the discussion in
[
80
, §8]
and
[
79
, §III.B]
. See also
[
155
,
75
]
for more detail.

The next definition follows
[
80
]
and
[
155
, §4.2]
:

Definition 4.31
.

Let
W\to X
be a vector bundle of rank
k
,
V\to X
a virtual vector bundle,
X^{V}\hookrightarrow X^{V\oplus W}
be the map
e_{W}
induced by the zero section of
W
as in
Equation

3.20
, and let
\xi
be a stable tangential structure. The
defect map
corresponding to this data is the map

(4.32)

\mathrm{Def}_{W}\colon[MT\xi\wedge X^{V\oplus W},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]\to[MT\xi\wedge X^{V},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}].

induced by smashing
e_{V}
with
MT\xi
and applying
[-,\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
.
Rewritten using
2.21
and using the suspension isomorphism on the source, this is

(4.33)

\mathrm{Def}_{W}\colon\mho_{\xi}^{d+2-k}(X^{V\oplus W-k})\to\mho_{\xi}^{d+2}(X^{V}).

Remark 4.34
.

In physical applications,
X
will often come in the form
BG
for
G
a compact Lie group, and
W
will often be an associated vector bundle to some representation of
G
. To describe invertible field theories modeling of real (resp. complex) fermionic SPTs, we will often take
\xi=\mathrm{Spin}
(resp.
\xi={\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
),
G=G_{b}
, and
V
inducing the desired internal fermionic symmetry group through the procedure described in
1.5
.
∎

This map of invertible field theories models a map between the anomaly of a
d
-dimensional QFT and the anomaly of a
(d-k)
-dimensional
W
-defect
theory. According to the theory of
anomaly inflow
(see e.g.
[
127
,
332
,
316
]
), the anomalies of these QFTs are described by SPTs in one dimension higher, which following
2.11
we will mathematically model using invertible field theories.
So, the anomaly of the
W
-defect QFT, which is modeled by a (deformation class of a)
(d-k+1)
-dimensional invertible field theory, lives in the source of
\mathrm{Def}_{W}
, while the image of the map classifies the anomalies of the possible bulk
d
-dimensional QFTs that can admit the source
W
-defect. When
k=1
, such defects are called domain walls
[
174
,
155
]
, while vortices
[
317
,
323
]
and hedgehogs are examples in
d=2
and
d=3
, resp.

Physically, one begins with the bulk
d
-dimensional theory, often specified by a Hamiltonian or Lagrangian, whose symmetry type is encoded by
X
,
V
, and
\xi
. Then, one modifies the theory by adding and then condensing a charged order parameter, whose symmetry charge (i.e. representation) is encoded by the bundle
W
.
The submanifold of spacetime where the order parameter vanishes corresponds to the spacetime of the defect theory, and, for a given starting bulk spacetime manifold
M
, to the image of the bordism class
[M]
under the homomorphism
\mathrm{sm}_{W}
. Ground states on this defect generally possess a different symmetry from those of the original theory—the symmetry of the original theory has been spontaneously broken.

Mathematically, the modification to the symmetry in the theory is achieved by twisting the tangential structures on spacetime by the bundle
W
, and this is exactly what the Smith homomorphism achieves.
For more details on the creation of
W
-defects, see e.g.
[
155
, §4]
and
[
79
, §III.B]
, where they are called
\rho
-defects.

Example 4.35

(
[
79
, §IV.C]
)
.

Consider our running example, the time-reversal symmetric Majorana chain, whose low energy field theory corresponds to the theory
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}
of
2.23
. This theory generates the group
\mho^{2}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\cong\Hom(\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\cong\mathbb{Z}/8
.
The Anderson-dual map to the Smith homomorphism of
3.25
is
the defect map

(4.36)

\mathrm{Def}_{\sigma}\colon\mho^{2}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\to\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cong\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2).

This turns out to be given by inclusion into the first factor, meaning that
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}
arises as the defect theory to the theory that generates the
\mathbb{Z}/8
factor in
\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
. The symmetry type of this theory,
\mathrm{Spin}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, encodes fermions with a unitary
\mathbb{Z}/2
internal symmetry
U
that squares to the identity.

Under the anomaly inflow hypothesis, this computation shows that under this form of symmetry breaking, the anomaly of the 2d fermions with unitary
\mathbb{Z}/2
symmetry is sent isomorphically to the anomaly of the domain wall fermions, while the gravitational anomaly (corresponding to the
\mathbb{Z}
factor) is forgotten.
∎

For an example concerning
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{+}}^{4}\cong\mathbb{Z}/16
, which is known at the physics level of rigor to be generated by the low-energy theory of a Majorana fermion, and a more involved anomaly matching computation, see
[
79
, III.C.3]
.

Remark 4.37
.

In this section, we described how Anderson-dualized Smith homomorphisms model defect maps of SPTs. In fact, one can extend
Equation

4.32
to an entire long exact sequence of groups of deformation classes of invertible field theories, which was studied in
[
79
,
80
]
. The physics processes corresponding to the other two maps in the sequence involve
residual anomalies
, which determine when it is possible to construct a
W
-defect, and the pumping of
Berry phases
.
∎

4.3.2.
The spiral maps of interacting SPTs

Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern only considered the dimensional reductions of free theories, incorporating interactions
after
the reduction was performed.
However,
inspired by the Smith homomorphism models for symmetry breaking for SPT phases discussed in the previous section (
4.3.1
), and following a suggestion of Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren
[
155
, §5]
, we define two maps of Anderson-dual bordism groups that model analogs of dimensional reduction on the interacting side.
To avoid conflict with other uses of the term
dimensional reduction
for field theories,
40
40

40

Again, see
0.13
.
we will refer to our mathematical interacting versions of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s dimensional reduction maps as
spiral maps
.

As observed by Hason–Komargodski–Thorngren, the dimensional reduction procedures of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern are quite similar to the
W
-defect creation described in
Section

4.3.1
. However, there are at least two confounding aspects at play.

The first aspect to notice is that a Smith homomorphism simply does not have the correct type signature.
A map modeling Queiroz--Khalaf--Stern’s procedure should raise the dimension (of IFTS) by 1
41
41

41

So that the dual map (on bordism classes of manifolds)
lowers
dimension by 1.
and change the tangential structure by one hour of the Bott clock. However, no Smith homomorphism recovers this situation in our model. More precisely, our choice of model for primed phases involves twisting not just over
\mathbb{Z}/2
, but over products
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}
: for this reason, our model for the Bott spiral map must compare groups of twisted spin IFTs for twists over two different base spaces (e.g. twists over
B\mathbb{Z}/2
versus over
B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
).
Since Smith homomorphisms modify the twist but not the base space, they alone do not suffice for this application.

A second aspect of the problem is that our maps for Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s process should have two different behaviors: they should be either isomorphisms or multiplication by two. In the case of spiraling up from class AIII in
d=1
, for example, each map starting from an unprimed phase should be an isomorphism, while each map starting from a primed phase should be multiplication by two.

With the above in mind, we define two different spiral maps, each involving a Smith homomorphism component. The other components will be the following:

Definition 4.38
.

Let
X
be a space. The
crush map

c_{X}\colon\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}X\to\mathbb{S}

is

\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}
applied to the unique map
X\to\mathrm{pt}
.
When
X
is clear from context, we will write
c
instead of
c_{X}
.

Definition 4.39
.

The
diagonal map
is the map

\Delta\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}=\mathit{ME}_{1,1}

induced by the diagonal map
B\mathbb{Z}/2\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Note that for this definition to parse we use that the bundle
(\sigma-1)\boxplus(1-\sigma)
over
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
pulls back to the bundle
(\sigma-1)\oplus(1-\sigma)=0
over
B\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Remark 4.40
.

The crush map
\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{S}
is induced by the unique homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\{\pm 1\}\twoheadrightarrow\{\pm 1\}
of fermionic groups.
Meanwhile, let
e_{1},e_{2}
be the generators of the fermionic group
E_{1,1}
. The diagonal map is induced by the map of fermionic groups
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathrm{Spin}_{1}\to E_{1,1}
sending the nontrivial element of
\mathbb{Z}/2
to the product
e_{1}e_{2}
.
Multiplied with the identity on a fermionic group
G_{f}
,
\Delta
induces a map
G_{f}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to G_{f}^{\prime}
.
∎

Now we are ready to define the two spiral maps.
Recall from
1.112
that for nonnegative integers
\ell
and
k
,
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}=((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})^{\wedge\ell}\wedge((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma})^{\wedge k}
, and recall from
Equation

3.20
that
e_{\sigma}
is the map of spectra induced by taking the zero section of the bundle
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
, which in turn induces Smith homomorphisms of the form in
3.21
.

Definition 4.41
.

For
\ell\geq 0
and
k>0
, let
\phi_{\ell,k}
be the composition

(4.42)

\phi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}=\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge e_{\sigma}}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge c}\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1},

and let
\psi_{\ell,k}
be the composition

(4.43)

\psi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}=\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge e_{\sigma}}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge\Delta}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{1,1}=\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k}.

Note that
\phi_{\ell,k}
changes
(\ell,k)
by
(0,-1)
while
\psi_{\ell,k}
changes
(\ell,k)
by
(1,0)
.

We will also want maps
\psi_{\ell,0}
for
\ell\geq 3
in order to model the Bott spiral. Since
4.41
does not immediately generalize to these values of
(\ell,k)
, we give a different definition.

Recall the functor
\zeta\colon\mathit{\mathscr{R}ep}\to\mathit{\mathscr{F}erm\mathscr{G}rp}
(
1.22
) sending a representation
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{\ell,k}
to its pin cover, as well as the functor from
1.108
sending
(G,\rho)
to
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(BG)^{\rho-r}
, where
r
is the rank of the virtual representation induced by
\rho
. By
1.26
and
1.108
, both of these functors are symmetric monoidal.

The fermionic groups
E_{\ell,k}
and
Q_{8}
are in the essential image of
\zeta
: for
E_{\ell,k}
, this is in
1.38
, and for
Q_{8}
, use
G=\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
acting as the dihedral symmetry
\mathrm{D}_{2}
(in Schönflies notation) as a subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}_{3}\subset\mathrm{O}_{3}\times\mathrm{O}_{0}
. Thus by
1.26
,
1.108
, and
1.114
, there is an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence

(4.44)

m\colon\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{\ell-3,k-1})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-3,k-1}\simeq\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-3,k-1}.

Definition 4.45
.

Let
\ell\geq 3
.
2.29
gives an isomorphism of fermionic groups
s_{-}\colon Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{0,1}\overset{\cong}{\to}E_{3,0}
; by tensoring with
\mathrm{id}_{E_{\ell-3,k}}
, we get an isomorphism of fermionic groups
s_{\ell,k}\colon Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{\ell-3,k+1}\overset{\cong}{\to}E_{\ell,k}
. Define
\overline{s}_{\ell,k}
to be the following composition, which is an equivalence of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-modules:

(4.46)

\overline{s}_{\ell,k}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\eqref{lem_shearing_Elk}}\mathit{MTH}(E_{\ell,k})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{s_{\ell,k}^{-1}}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{\ell-3,k+1})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{m}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-3,k+1},

where
m
is as in (
4.44
).

Remark 4.47
.

This is an example where a positive answer to
1.110
would simplify our construction, by allowing us to avoid the auxiliary data of representations in defining
m
.
∎

Definition 4.48
.

Let
\ell\geq 3
. Define
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,0}
to be the following composition:

(4.49)

\overline{\psi}_{\ell,0}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,0}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\overline{s}_{\ell,0}}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-3,1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\wedge\psi_{\ell-3,1}}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell-2,1}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\overline{s}_{\ell+1,1}{}^{-1}}\Sigma MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,1}.

For
k>0
and
\ell\geq 0
, define
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}\wedge\psi_{\ell,k}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k}
and define
\overline{\phi}_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}
analogously.

Remark 4.50
.

We would have preferred to define
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,0}
as
\mathrm{id}_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}
smashed with a map of Thom spectra, like in (
4.43
). To do so, we would need
an equivalence of Thom spectra
(B(Q_{8})_{b})^{V}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-3,1}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,0}
, for some rank-zero virtual vector bundle
V
, ideally induced by an isomorphism of vector bundles. This is obstructed by
2.34
.
∎

By taking Anderson duals, we obtain two maps of groups of (deformation classes of) invertible field theories. In other words, we take the Anderson-dual bordism cohomology of the previous two maps to obtain our maps of interest. For simplicity, we state the following definitions for the real case, using twists of spin. The definitions for spin
c
are analogous.

Definition 4.51

(Spiral maps)
.

Let

(4.52)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon[MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]\to[MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]

be the map induced by applying
[\text{--},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
to
\overline{\phi}_{\ell,k}
,
and let

(4.53)

\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}\colon[MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]\to[MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k},\Sigma^{d+2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]

be the map induced in the same way from
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,k}
.
Rewritten using
2.21
and the suspension isomorphism, the maps are of the form

(4.54)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{d+1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\to\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})

and

(4.55)

\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}\colon\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{d+1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k})\to\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}}^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}).

We compute the relevant portion of each of these maps in
Section

5
. The computation for
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
appears in §§
5.3
,
6.1
, and
6.3
, while that for
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
appears in §§
5.4
,
6.1
,
6.2
, and
6.3
.

Remark 4.56

(Dimension check)
.

Observe that, as desired, each map increases the dimension on invertible field theories by one. Just like the
W
-defect map of
4.31
, these maps increase dimension, while the physical process of condensing to a defect decreases the dimension; each of the spiral maps is meant to run from the defect SPT to the one-dimension-higher bulk SPT.
∎

Remark 4.57

(Physical Intuition)
.

The guiding intuition for the definitions of our two maps, other than the fact that they behave computationally as we desire, is that the primed-to-unprimed phase physical process involves adding and condensing a charged order parameter, while the unprimed-to-primed physical process first requires doubling to form the primed phase, then forming a defect.
In the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
, we view
e_{\sigma}
as forming the defect, as in the Smith homomorphism story reviewed in
Section

3.2
, and we view the crush map
c
as forgetting an auxiliary
\mathbb{Z}/2
-gauge field.
In the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
, we view
\Delta
as forming the primed phase in class A
′
from two copies of the phase in class A, and then
e_{\sigma}
as concentrating to the defect submanifold.
We will not attempt to connect more closely to the Hamiltonian picture in this paper, instead pursuing a complementary picture via
2.11
.
∎

As part of our computations, we will sometimes want to approximate
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
using spectra other than
MT\mathrm{Spin}
.

Definition 4.58
.

For a spectrum
E
,
\ell\geq 0
, and
k>0
, let
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(E)\colon\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(E\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\to I_{\mathbb{Z}}(E\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
, resp
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(E)\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(E\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k})\to I_{\mathbb{Z}}(E\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
be the maps defined in the same way as
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
, resp.
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
, except with
MT\mathrm{Spin}
replaced with
E
.

If
k=0
and
\ell\geq 3
, we must assume
E
is an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module to define
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,0}^{\psi}(E)
. In this case, define
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,0}^{\psi}(E)
analogously, except applying
E\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}
to
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,k}
, then taking the Anderson dual.

Example 4.59
.

We return to the running example between symmetry types dpin and pin
-
for an example of the map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
.
Let us specialize to dimension
d=3
and consider the dual map on bordism. Recall from
1.112
that a spin-
(\ell,k)
structure on a manifold
M
is equivalent to data of real line bundles
L_{1},\dotsc,L_{\ell+k}\to M
and a spin structure on
TM+L_{1}+\dotsb+L_{\ell}-L_{\ell+1}-\dotsb-L_{\ell+k}
.
On manifold representatives, the map
\phi_{1,1}
in bordism is given by
[
182
, Lemma F.15]

(4.60)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\mathrm{DPin}}_{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/8\cong\Omega^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{RP}^{3},L_{1},L_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{RP}^{2},L_{1})}

where the line bundles are such that
T\mathbb{RP}^{3}+L_{1}-L_{2}
is spin and such that
T\mathbb{RP}^{2}+L_{1}
is spin. Recall that
T\mathbb{RP}^{n}\simeq(n+1)\sigma-1
, and that for any vector bundle
V
,
V^{\oplus 4}
is spin, to see that we can choose
L_{1}=L_{2}=\sigma
.

The dual map on (groups of deformation classes of) IFTs is also an isomorphism:

(4.61)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}}^{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho_{\mathrm{DPin}}^{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Physically, we interpret
a generator of the domain
\mathbb{Z}/8
as the
class BDI superconductor in spatial dimension
d=1
, created as a line defect in the class D
′
superconductor in spatial dimension
d=2
.
∎

Alternately iterating these two spiral maps, we construct our mathematical models for entire Bott spirals of phases.
We begin our discussion with the complex case.
The physical story that we wish to reproduce is the following.
In
[
263
, Figure 1 (c)]
, the complex Bott spiral begins from the base case of the
d=1
class AIII topological insulator, which generates a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of interacting SPT phases. It is a dimensional reduction of the
d=2
class A
′
topological insulator, which generates a
\mathbb{Z}/4
of interacting phases, which in turn is a dimensional reduction of the
d=3
class AIII insulator, which generates a
\mathbb{Z}/8
.
Continuing upward, with each dimension increase by one, we exchange classes AIII and A
′
, and at every even-to-odd dimension step, the order of the interacting SPT grows by a factor of two.

In our IFT model, we begin (in the top right of
Figure

3
) with the group
\mho_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}}^{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/4

[
35
,
34
]
, which under
2.11
classifies SPTs with pin
c
symmetry in spatial dimension 1. We may rewrite, using the Smith isomorphism of
3.29
,
\mho_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}}^{2}\cong\mho_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{2}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})=\mho_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{2}(\mathit{ME}_{1,0})
.
The spiral map

(4.62)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{{1,1}}\colon\mho_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{2}(\mathit{ME}_{1,0})\to\mho^{3}_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})

is an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/4\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}/4
(see
6.86
for the isomorphism
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
). Thus (
4.62
) maps a generator of the source—predicted to be the
d=1
class AIII insulator—to a generator of the target—the
d=2
class A
′
insulator, modeled as an invertible field theory on spin
c
-(1,1) manifolds.
The intermediate step shown in
Figure

3
, dual to the crush map, is the addition of an auxiliary
\mathbb{Z}/2
-bundle on the IFT side, which we interpret as the formation of two copies of the initial class AIII SPT.

Figure 3
.
This figure shows the sequence of interacting SPT phases in the complex version of the Bott spiral, starting with the
d=1
class AIII insulator, which is predicted to generate the group
\mho_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}}^{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
in the top right. The leftmost column indicates the Altland–Zirnbauer class of the SPT at each step. The middle column encodes spectra of the form
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and the maps between them. The last column indicates the
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
-cohomology of the middle column; i.e. the groups of (deformation classes of) invertible field theories that provide our model for the groups of interacting SPTs.
The spatial dimension of the SPT increases as the rightmost column is read downward; see
4.56
.

Pink, underlined text
indicates the image of the free-to-interacting map and maps restricted to those summands.
The maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
are defined in
4.51
and are the composites of the vertical maps shown, while the last map, in the bottom right, is the Thom isomorphism in
\mho_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{*}
-cohomology. Bahri–Gilkey
[
34
, Theorems 1 and 2]
compute the bordism groups corresponding to
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}^{*}
and
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
;
[
89
, Theorem 4.57]
does the same for
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and
6.86
computes
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})
.

The next spiral map,

(4.63)

\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{0,1}\colon\mho^{3}_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})\to\mho_{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}^{4}(\mathit{ME}_{0,1})\cong\mho_{{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}}^{4}\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2,

sends any generator of the
\mathbb{Z}/4
in the source to twice a generator of the
\mathbb{Z}/8
summand in the classification of
d=3
class AIII insulators, missing the extra
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand.

To continue up the spiral, we use
1.121
to reset from
(\ell,k)=(0,1)
to
(\ell,k)=(1,0)
using the Thom isomorphism for
2\sigma
in
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{*}
-cohomology. In other words, because
2\sigma
is a complex vector bundle, it admits a spin
c
structure, allowing us to exchange the
\mathbb{Z}/2
twist by
1-\sigma
with the twist by
2\sigma-2+1-\sigma=\sigma-1
.
Equivalently, we are using that
E_{0,1}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}\cong E_{1,0}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
, a complexified version of
2.31
.
To continue to arbitrary higher dimensions, we may then iterate
\mathrm{sp}_{1,1}^{\phi}
,
\mathrm{sp}_{0,1}^{\psi}
, and the Thom isomorphism.

The physics we wish to describe is only
that of the interacting SPTs connected to free fermion models—the image of the free-to-interacting map—not the entire groups of invertible field theories. By the results of our computation (
6.82
), we know that the image of the free-to-interacting map is a large two-torsion summand—called the long summand in
Section

5
.
Combining this with our computation of the spiral maps (
6.83
and
6.84
), we have the following.

Corollary 4.64
.

The spiral maps restricted to the image of the free-to-interacting map (
3.92
) take the form

(4.65)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}_{1,1}^{\phi}|_{\mathrm{im}\,F2I^{c}}\colon\mathbb{Z}/2^{2+(d-1)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2^{2+(d-1)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}^{d+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}

for
d\geq-1
odd
and

(4.66)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}_{0,1}^{\psi}|_{\mathrm{im}\,F2I^{c}}\colon\mathbb{Z}/2^{1+d/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2^{2+d/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{d+1}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}^{d+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}

for
d\geq-2
even.

In other words, as we continue up the spiral, we indeed see the doubling of the invariant at every other dimension step in our invertible field theory model. That is, our model of the Bott spiral beginning in 1d class AIII is in agreement with Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s from
4.1
.

The real cases—spiraling up from class BDI and class CII, resp., in
d=1

[
263
, Figure 1 (a), (b)]
—are similar, but require more care in our choices of fermionic group assignments to symmetry classes. As in
Section

2.3
, we will use discrete fermionic group models for the Altland–Zirnbauer classes, consisting (except class C) of
E_{\ell,k}
’s. And as explained in
Section

4.1
, for the primed classes (except C
′
), we will simply form the fermionic tensor product with
E_{1,1}
. For class C
′
, we must invoke
2.29
to see that
E_{4,0}\cong Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,1}
or
E_{0,4}\cong Q_{8}\mathbin{\hat{\times}}E_{1,1}
are both valid choices. See

As in the complex case, by alternately applying the two different spiral maps, we can draw out spirals of phases. When the dimension increases from
2m-1
to
2m
, we get an isomorphism on the subgroup of IFTs in the image of the free-to-interacting map. Going from dimension
2m
to
2m+1
, the map is multiplication by
2
. We computed these results in

6.16
,
6.24
, and
6.53
.
This replicates
4.1
if we omit the parameter
\mu
.

Recall the definition of
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
from
0.18
.

Corollary 4.67
.

Let
\widetilde{m}
and
i

4.21
, and suppose
d\equiv\ell+k-2i+2\bmod 4

.

Thus our models for the Bott spirals starting in 1d classes BDI and CII are in
nearly
complete agreement with Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s
4.1
. We discuss the differences below in
4.70
.

We find for the real cases that it is actually more illuminating to draw an entire grid of possible sequences of spiral maps. We do so in
Figure

4
, identifying symmetry types for spin-
(\ell,k)
IFTs according to
Table

3
plus following the rule that increasing
(\ell,k)
by
(1,1)
adds a prime. For example, the fermionic group
E_{2,2}
models a class we call D

.

Remark 4.70
.

Our analysis of the interacting Bott spiral clarifies an aspect of Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s analysis that is invisible from the free fermion side of the problem. Specifically, Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern use the parameter
\mu
to double the size of the groups of interacting SPTs in classes BDI, D
′
, and DIII, and thus obtain their desired interacting classification in a slightly different way from the behavior of the other five real Altland–Zirnbauer classes. In general, the Bott spiral computations suggest that when one increases the dimension by
2
, the order of the image of the free-to-interacting map doubles. This suggests that, for example, in
d=1
class BDI, there is a
\mathbb{Z}/4
classification of interacting phases. However, Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern change their parameter
\mu
to obtain a
\mathbb{Z}/8
of phases, matching the classification in, e.g.,
[
111
,
112
,
320
,
148
,
341
,
185
,
333
,
82
,
202
,
172
,
322
,
118
,
321
]
.

In our model for the Bott spiral, using
\mu
in this way corresponds to removing a prime: the spiral we most naturally obtain includes the discrete Altland–Zirnbauer types
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathrm{DIII}^{\prime}
, corresponding to
(\ell,k)=(2,1)
,
(2,2)
, and
(1,2)
respectively. The classification of
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
phases in 1d is
\mathbb{Z}/4\subset\mho_{2,1}^{2}
(
4.67
), matching the expected behavior of the Bott spiral but not Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s modification. The same factor of
2
discrepancy occurs in classes BDI vs
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
in all dimensions
4m+1
,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime}
vs
\mathrm{D}^{\prime\prime}
in dimensions
4m+2
, and DIII vs
\mathrm{DIII}^{\prime}
in dimensions
4m+3
.

Which of these two approaches correctly models the Bott spiral? We use the spiral maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
to answer this question. We defined the spiral maps to model the processes Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern describe to pass between different entries in the Bott spiral. Therefore we believe
the correct model for the Bott spiral is one in which it makes sense to alternate the spiral maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
through a full period of the spiral.

As the spiral maps are defined on groups of IFTs, this ansatz is visible only on the interacting side of the free-to-interacting correspondence.

Using this, we argue that the Bott spiral must include
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathrm{DIII}^{\prime}
instead of BDI,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime}
, and DIII. For the sake of contradiction, begin the spiral in dimension
d
and class BDI. Then, applying
\mathrm{sp}_{1,1}^{\phi}
, we land in dimension
d+1
class
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
. Next, apply
\mathrm{sp}_{0,1}^{\psi}
to land in
(d+2)
-dimensional class DIII. In
Figures

4
and
6
, we color these three steps in
orange
.

So far there is no contradiction. By
4.67
, if
d=8j+1
we have a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4j+3}
summand in
\mho_{1,0}^{8j+1}
(class BDI), a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4j+3}
summand in
\mho_{1,1}^{8j+2}
(class
\mathrm{D^{\prime}}
), and a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4j+4}
summand in
\mho_{0,1}^{8j+3}
(class DIII), in agreement with the
\mu=2
case of
[
263
, Figure 1(a)]
. The case
j=0
specializes to the
\mathbb{Z}/8
of 1d pin
-
IFTs (
2.23
),
\mathbb{Z}/8
of 2d dpin IFTs, and
\mathbb{Z}/16
of 3d pin
+
IFTs. Likewise,
d=8j+5
reproduces the
\mu=2
cases of
[
263
, Figure 1(b)]
.
4.67
implies that the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{1,1}
, resp.
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{0,1}
are an isomorphism, resp. multiplication by
2
on those summands, as predicted.

Next we apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{0,2}
to land in
(d+3)
-dimensional class AII. This is a slight problem, as we expected class
\mathrm{AII}^{\prime}
, and the groups of IFTs continue to be off by a factor of
2
. There does not appear to be a physically meaningful map from classes DIII or AII to
\mathrm{AII}^{\prime}
. But then we have a bigger problem: since
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
lowers the value of
\ell
, it is undefined on class AII, for which
\ell=0
. Therefore if we started the spiral in class BDI, we cannot continue past here. By contrast, if we began with
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
, then after three steps we are in class
\mathrm{AII}^{\prime}
in accordance with Queiroz–Khalaf–Stern’s prediction, and
\ell=1
, so we can continue the spiral. We interpret this as evidence that the correct model for the Bott spiral uses classes
\mathrm{BDI}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathrm{DIII}^{\prime}
instead of BDI,
\mathrm{D}^{\prime}
, and DIII, and therefore has a slightly different classification of IFTs in those cases.
∎

Lemma 4.71
.

The diagram in
Figure

4
commutes.

Proof.

Zero section maps are natural in maps of vector bundles.
More precisely, if
f\colon X\to Y
is a map,
V\to Y
is a virtual vector bundle and
W\to Y
an honest vector bundle, then

(4.72)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{f^{*}V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{f^{*}V\oplus f^{*}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{V\oplus W}}

commutes.
∎

Remark 4.73

(Discrete versus continuous models for Altland–Zirnbauer classes)
.

Unlike for classes BDI and AIII, we have two fermionic group models for class CII: the discrete choice
E_{0,3}
and the continuous choice
\mathrm{Pin}_{3}^{-}
. For both of these choices, the group of IFTs in (spatial) dimension
1
in the image of the free-to-interacting map is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
: see
6.15
for the discrete case and
[
118
, Corollary 9.103]
for the continuous case. In
Figure

6
we chose the discrete symmetry group. This is because, if we start with the continuous symmetry group instead, we soon enough run into a problem defining
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
.
See
Figure

7
for a chart of these spiral maps. Similar issues occur when beginning a spiral with any of the continuous symmetry groups not isomorphic to their discrete counterparts.

The first step of the pin
h-
Bott spiral is not the issue: apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
as usual, and it is an isomorphism on the long summands. We called this map
\mathrm{sp}_{0,1}^{\phi}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h-})
in
4.58
. This, and the other spiral maps we use in this remark, are computed in
6.50
and
6.60
.

After this, it is unclear how to apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
, because we do not seem to have an
\mathit{ME}_{1,0}
factor. Fortunately, though, by
[
84
, (3.11)]
there are
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalences

(4.74)

MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,1}\simeq MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,0}.

With this identification, we can apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{0,0}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+})
, and land in
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}^{4}\cong\mathbb{Z}/4

[
118
, Theorem 9.97]
, then apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{0,1}(MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+})
and land in
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}^{5}(\mathit{ME}_{0,1})
, which also contains a
\mathbb{Z}/4
long summand. However, now we are stuck: as far as we can tell, there is no way to rearrange
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1}
to obtain an
\mathit{ME}_{1,0}
factor, hence no way to apply
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
and continue the spiral. We interpret this fact as evidence that the correct model for the Bott spiral uses the discrete fermionic groups, not the continuous ones.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}}(\mathit{ME}_{0,1})\cong\mho^{3}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}(\mathit{ME}_{1,0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mho^{4}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mho^{5}_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}(\mathit{ME}_{0,1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{?}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2}}
