{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{?}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Figure 7
.
If one tries to model the Bott spiral starting with
continuous
class CII in dimension
1
, the first several maps in the Bott spiral work out the same as in the discrete case, but once we get to dimension
5
, there is no map
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
that would allow us to continue the spiral. Here
\phi
and
\psi
mean the corresponding
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
maps smashed with
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h\pm}
. We discuss this further in
4.73
, where we interpret this as evidence that, when modeling the Bott spiral, the correct symmetry types to use to represent Altland–Zirnbauer classes are the discrete ones.
The groups of IFTs in this spiral are computed in
[
118
, Theorem 9.97]
and
6.49
. The spiral maps are computed in
6.50
and
6.60
. The isomorphisms
\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}}^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{0,1})\cong\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{h}}^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1})\cong\mho_{\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}}^{*}
appear in (
4.74
).

A theme of this paper has been the breakdown of Bott periodicity as we allow interactions, the tension between the “Bott clock” of free tenfold way SPTs and the “Bott spiral” of related interacting SPTs.
Now, we can finally describe what remains of Bott periodicity on the interacting side. Instead of an isomorphism after eight steps around the spiral—meaning, eight steps in zigzag within
Figure

4
—we find a multiplication by 16 on (deformation classes of) groups of IFTs.
In fact, the dual process to the multiplication by 16 is another multiplication by 16 on the bordism side. On the level of manifold representatives, it is given by multiplication by a real
Bott manifold
, such as the one constructed in
[
117
, §5.3]
, specifically.
As we mentioned in
3.65
, a real Bott manifold is any closed spin manifold whose bordism class maps to the real Bott class
\beta\in\mathit{KO}_{8}
under the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map.
We see that Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro, which was the central ingredient in our free-to-interacting maps (
Section

3.5
), is also the underlying reason that we observe the interacting spiral.

5.
Computations over
\mathit{ko}

Let
\mathit{ko}
denote the connective cover of the real
KO
-theory spectrum. For a few different reasons,
\mathit{ko}
is a reasonable approximation to the spin bordism spectrum
MT\mathrm{Spin}
: in degrees
7
and below, they are equivalent, and in general
\mathit{ko}
is a summand of
MT\mathrm{Spin}

[
18
]
. Therefore, we will separate our computations into two steps: in this section, we study the free-to-interacting maps and the spiral maps with
MT\mathrm{Spin}
replaced with
\mathit{ko}
. Then, in §
6
, we lift our computations to
MT\mathrm{Spin}
.

Our main results in this section are:

(1)

5.55
, computing
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
modulo simple
2
-torsion
Whitney summands
, which do not play a role in the Bott spiral (see
5.10
).

(2)

5.58
, computing the image of the
\mathit{ko}
-module analog of the free-to-interacting map that we constructed in
3.99
:

(5.1a)

\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{KO}^{d+\ell-k-2}\to(I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko})^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}),

for all
\ell,k
not both equal to
0
.

(3)

5.61
, computing the
\mathit{ko}
version of
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
(see
4.58
),

(5.1b)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})\colon(I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko})^{d+1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to(I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko})^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1}),

again modulo Whitney summands, for all
\ell,k
not both equal to
0
.

(4)

5.88
, computing the
\mathit{ko}
version of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
(
4.58
) modulo Whitney summands:

(5.1c)

\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})\colon(I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko})^{d+1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k})\to(I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko})^{d+2}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}),

for all
\ell,k>0
. (We will tackle
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}(\mathit{ko})
, where
\ell\geq 3
, in
Section

6.2
.)

Our computation is entirely homotopy-theoretic, which has advantages and disadvantages. Our computations build on and generalize previous work computing the
\mathit{ko}
-theory of
BV
for
V
an elementary abelian
2
-group
[
253
,
342
,
177
,
61
,
58
,
60
,
62
,
228
,
284
,
259
,
258
,
267
,
59
,
64
]
: we show that these computations generalize to
some
twists of
\mathit{ko}
-theory over
BV
, giving many new computations of twisted
\mathit{ko}
-homology for these spaces. Taking mod
2
cohomology, we obtain a connection between the isomorphism of fermionic groups
E_{4,0}\cong E_{0,4}
from
2.31
, the
4
-torsion in the
Q_{1}
-local Picard group of
\mathcal{A}(1)

[
342
,
62
]
, and the
4
-periodicity in Smith families over
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and
\mathit{ko}
, studied by
[
185
,
307
,
155
,
326
,
79
,
80
]
and reviewed in
[
80
, Examples 6.14 and 7.8]
. We plan to elaborate on this connection in future work.

On the other hand, our computations make use of technical tools from homotopy theory, many of which are uncommon in the mathematical physics literature. For this reason, we would be interested in learning whether other methods could be used to compute the spiral and F2I maps of spectra that we defined in
3.87
,
3.92
, and
4.51
.

We begin in §
5.1
with some groundwork and utility lemmas for our computations. Then, in §
5.2
, we introduce our key computational examples and use them to prove
5.55
, calculating the interesting part of
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. In §
5.3
we calculate
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
and
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}
. Finally, in §
5.4
, we calculate
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(\mathit{ko})
assuming
\ell,k>0
.

5.1.
Generalities for the computation over
\mathit{ko}

Our computational results are founded on a simple description of
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
that is
(4,1)
-periodic in
(\ell,k)
, again modulo a trivial piece. This is a twisted version of a continuing program
understanding
H^{*}(BV;\mathbb{Z}/2)
,
\mathit{ku}_{*}(BV)
, and
\mathit{ko}_{*}(BV)
for
V
an elementary abelian
2
-group, as described above.

Recall that there is a ring isomorphism

(5.2)

\mathit{ko}_{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}[\eta,a,\beta]/(\eta^{3},2\eta,\eta a,a^{2}-4\beta)\quad\lvert\eta\rvert=1,\lvert a\rvert=4,\lvert\beta\rvert=8.

The class
\beta
is called the (real)
Bott class
, as it implements Bott periodicity in periodic
\mathit{KO}
-theory. For any space or spectrum
X
,
\mathit{KO}_{*}(X)\cong\mathit{ko}_{*}(X)[\beta^{-1}]
, and more generally, for any
\mathit{ko}
-module
M
, there is a natural isomorphism

(5.3)

\pi_{*}(M)[\beta^{-1}]\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{*}(\mathit{KO}\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}M).

The Eilenberg–Mac Lane spectrum
H\mathbb{Z}/2
has a unique
\mathit{ko}
-algebra structure via the
E_{\infty}
-ring map
\mathit{ko}\to H\mathbb{Z}\to H\mathbb{Z}/2
, which is the composition of
0
-truncation and reduction mod
2
(see for example
[
25
, Remark 2.17]
). Whenever we refer to
H\mathbb{Z}/2
as a
\mathit{ko}
-module, we always mean the module structure induced by this algebra structure. Since
\beta
acts trivially on
H\mathbb{Z}/2
for degree reasons,
(H\mathbb{Z}/2)[\beta^{-1}]\simeq 0
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
M
be a bounded below
\mathit{ko}
-module of finite type, so that there is a
\mathit{ko}
-module
splitting

(5.5)

M\simeq\overline{M}\vee\bigvee_{i}\Sigma^{k_{i}}H\mathbb{Z}/2

over some (necessary countable) index
i
, such that
\overline{M}
has no
\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands. This
specifies
\overline{M}
uniquely up to
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence.
We say
M
is
of elementary abelian (EA) type
if

(1)

\beta
acts injectively on
\pi_{*}(\overline{M})
, and

(2)

there is an
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a
\mathit{KO}_{*}
-module isomorphism

(5.6)

\pi_{*}(M)[\beta^{-1}]\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{*+m}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}).

Remark 5.7
.

For convenience, we recall
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
from
[
46
, Theorem 2]
(see also
[
270
, §4]
). Let
\mu_{2^{\infty}}
denote the abelian group of all

n^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity as
n
ranges over all powers of
2
; this group is sometimes denoted

\mathbb{Z}/2^{\infty}
.
Then

(5.8)

\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{m}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})=\begin{cases}0&m=0,4,5,6\bmod 8\\
\mathbb{Z}/2&m=1,2\bmod 8\\
\mu_{2^{\infty}}&m=3,7\bmod 8.\end{cases}

The action of
\eta\colon\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{n}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})\to\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{n+1}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
is injective in degrees
1,2\bmod 8
and vanishes
in all other degrees; in particular, it hits
-1\in\mu_{2^{\infty}}
in
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}_{3}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
. The action
of
a
is uniquely determined by the relation
a^{2}=4\beta
and the requirement that
\beta
acts invertibly.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

Let
M
be a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type. We say a
\mathbb{Z}
-module summand
N\subset\pi_{*}(M)
is

(1)

a
long summand
if the image of
N
under the map
\psi\colon\pi_{*}(M)\to\pi_{*}(M)[\beta^{-1}]
is
nonzero and contained in a
\mu_{2^{\infty}}
summand,

(2)

a
short summand
if the image of
N
under
\psi
is nonzero and contained in a
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand, and

(3)

a
Whitney summand
43
43

43

The name “Whitney summand” is inspired by Conner–Floyd’s use of
Whitney numbers

[
73
, §17]
to refer to integrals of mod
2
cohomology classes of
B\mathrm{O}\times X
as bordism invariants
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{O}}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}/2
, generalizing Stiefel–Whitney numbers that only use the cohomology of
B\mathrm{O}
. Our Whitney summands are detected by Whitney numbers in this sense, hence the name.
if
\psi(N)=0
.

For each
n
,
\pi_{n}(M)
admits a direct-sum decomposition where each summand is long, short, or Whitney.

Remark 5.10

(Ignoring Whitney summands)
.

Above, we foreshadowed that we will give a
mostly
complete description of the interacting Bott spiral map
and the free-to-interacting map. “Mostly” here means that
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type (
5.51
) and we will be ignoring Whitney summands.

Our justification for ignoring these pieces is that for our application, namely studying fermionic phases in the Bott spiral, these pieces are not relevant. For example, these pieces do not contribute to the image of the free-to-interacting map. Recall that fermionic phases
are believed to correspond to invertible field theories on manifolds with twisted spin structures. The
IFTs dual to Whitney summands are trivial in that they do not depend on the twisted spin structure, and therefore
the corresponding phases are fermionic only in a trivial sense. See
[
89
, §3.3]
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

We would be tempted to refer to the summands we ignore as
bosonic
, since they do not depend on the choice of twisted spin structure.
However,
there are also IFTs dual to classes in
non
-Whitney summands that do not depend on a choice of twisted spin structure. These theories are multiples or compactifications
44
44

44

Keeping track of the tangential structure in a general compactified theory can be technical: see Schommer-Pries
[
278
, §9]
for a comprehensive analysis and
[
144
]
for some explicitly worked-out examples from a physics point of view. We only need the compactification of a twisted spin theory on a spin manifold; in this case things are simpler, because the product of a spin manifold and an
(X,V)
-twisted spin manifold has a canonical
(X,V)
-twisted spin structure. Moreover, Yamashita–Yonekura
[
335
, §7.3]
show that for invertible theories, compactification of this sort is a formal consequence of the
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module structure on the spectra classifying twisted spin IFTs. See also Tachikawa–Yamashita
[
306
, §2.2.6]
.

of other IFTs which do depend on a choice of twisted spin structure; hence, if we are working with a direct-sum decomposition or studying the
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}
-module structure on groups of IFTs for the purpose of computations, these IFTs
will be relevant.

An illustrative example occurs in two-dimensional pin
-
bordism. Recall from
2.23
that
the group of deformation classes of
(1+1)
d reflection-positive IFTs of pin
-
manifolds is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/8
.
45
45

45

The group of
(1+1)
d pin
-
IFTs, without requiring reflection positivity data, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{Z}/4
. See
[
265
, (5.1)]
,
[
82
, Examples 4.17 and 4.22]
, and
[
247
, Example 5.38]
, as well as
[
164
, Proposition 5.5]
for a generalization to
(d+1)
d for all odd
d
.
One of the four generators of this group is a theory
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}
whose partition function is the
Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant

[
57
,
195
]

\mathit{ABK}\colon\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mu_{8}
, where
\mu_{8}
denotes the group of
8^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}

[
82
, §5]
.

There are two pin
-
structures on
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, and their Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariants are
e^{\pm i\pi/4}\in\mu_{8}

[
195
, §3]
, so
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}
depends on the choice of pin
-
structure. The same argument applies to any power of
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}
except for
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}^{\otimes 4n}
—and indeed, for any closed pin
-

2
-manifold
\Sigma

[
195
, Lemma 3.6]
,

(5.12)

\mathrm{ABK}(\Sigma)^{4}=(-1)^{\chi(\Sigma)}\in\{\pm 1\}.

So
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}^{\otimes 4}
is “bosonic” in that it does not depend on the pin
-
structure of spacetime!

“Surprisingly bosonic” IFTs such as
\alpha_{\mathit{ABK}}^{\otimes 4}
occur whenever there is a class in the
E_{\infty}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence in filtration
0
(i.e. along the
x
-axis in the standard drawing convention); see
[
117
, §8.4]
or
[
89
, §3.3]
. This often occurs in long summands, as can be seen in the Ext calculations in
5.31
,
5.34
,
5.37
, and
5.40
, drawn in the right-hand sides of
Figures

9
,
10
,
11
, and
12
. For example, four-dimensional pin
+
bordism is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/16

[
136
, §2]
and eight times any generating IFT
\alpha_{\eta}
does not depend on the pin
+
structure. One consequence of this appears in work of Barkeshli–Hsin–Kobayashi
[
41
]
on the higher-group symmetry of the fermionic toric code: where one might expect a
\mathbb{Z}/16
symmetry defined by stacking with
\alpha_{\eta}
before gauging the
\mathbb{Z}/2
symmetry, the lack of dependence of
\alpha_{\eta}^{\otimes 8}
on the pin
+
structure reduces the symmetry group to
\mathbb{Z}/8
. See (
ibid.
, §2.1.1).
∎

In the examples below, we will briefly use the Adams spectral sequence in the form constructed by Baker–Lazarev
[
36
]
; we recommend Beaudry–Campbell
[
49
]
for additional background.

Definition 5.13
.

For a
\mathit{ko}
-module
M
, let

(5.14)

H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\coloneqq\pi_{-*}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(M,H\mathbb{Z}/2).

Let
\mathcal{A}(1)\coloneqq\langle\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\rangle
.

Lemma 5.15

(Baker
[
38
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(H\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathcal{A}(1)
.

By composition of maps,
H_{\mathit{ko}}(H\mathbb{Z}/2)
is an algebra over
\mathbb{Z}/2
; with this structure
5.15
is an algebra isomorphism. For a
\mathit{ko}
-module
M
, composition of maps makes
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
into an
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(H\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathcal{A}(1)
-module.

Lemma 5.16
.

For any spectrum
X
, there is a natural
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module isomorphism
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge X)\cong H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-action on
H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is obtained by restricting the
\mathcal{A}
-action.

Proof.

By the tensor-hom adjunction, there is a natural equivalence of spectra
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge X,H\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{S}}(X,H\mathbb{Z}/2)
; then take
\pi_{-*}
.
∎

Theorem 5.17

(Baker–Lazarev
[
36
]
)
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be
\mathit{ko}
-modules. Then there is a spectral sequence of Adams type, natural in
M
and
N
, with signature

(5.18)

E_{2}^{s,t}=\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M),H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M^{\prime}))\Longrightarrow\pi_{t-s}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(M^{\prime},M)_{2}^{\wedge}.

If
M^{\prime}
is connective and
M
is a CW
\mathit{ko}
-module of finite type, this spectral sequence converges.

We call this the
(Baker–Lazarev) Adams spectral sequence
.

What Baker–Lazarev prove is quite a bit more general, but we only need this special case. The finiteness and CW hypotheses in
5.17
hold in all examples considered in this paper.

Usually one plugs in
M^{\prime}=\mathit{ko}
to
5.17
so that this spectral sequence computes the
2
-completed homotopy groups of
M
: using the natural homotopy equivalence
\mathrm{Map}_{R}(R\wedge X,Y)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{S}}(X,Y)
with
R=\mathit{ko}
,
Y=M
, and
X=\mathbb{S}
,

(5.19)

\pi_{*}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(\mathit{ko},M)_{2}^{\wedge}\cong\pi_{*}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathbb{S},M)_{2}^{\wedge}=\pi_{*}(M)_{2}^{\wedge}.

Remark 5.20
.

The Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence is additive in the sense that a
\mathit{ko}
-module splitting
M\simeq M_{1}\vee M_{2}
induces a direct-sum splitting on
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
and on
\Ext
, and in fact a splitting of the entire Adams spectral sequence into the respective Adams spectral sequences for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
X
be a CW complex with finitely many cells in each dimension Then there is a convergent spectral sequence of Adams type, natural in
X
, with signature

(5.22)

E_{2}^{s,t}=\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2),\mathbb{Z}/2)\Longrightarrow\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge X)_{2}^{\wedge}=\mathit{ko}_{*}(X)_{2}^{\wedge}.

Proof.

Starting from (
5.18
), plug in
M^{\prime}=\mathit{ko}
and
M=\mathit{ko}\wedge X
, then apply
\mathrm{Map}_{R}(R\wedge X,Y)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{S}}(X,Y)
as above. To get the
E_{2}
-page, use that by
5.16
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\cong H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
∎

5.21
is the version one more commonly encounters, often proven via a change-of-rings theorem, such as in
[
49
]
. We will need a few
\mathit{ko}
-modules that do not to our knowledge factor as
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
, so we must use the more general
5.17
.

Throughout this section,
\Ext(N)
means
\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}(N,\mathbb{Z}/2)
if not otherwise specified.

Let
\mathbb{E}\coloneqq\Ext(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. This is the Adams
E_{2}
-page for
\mathit{ko}
, and the
E_{\infty}
-ring structure on
\mathit{ko}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebra structure on
\mathbb{E}
; see
[
49
, §4.2]
for an algebraic description of the product.

Proposition 5.23

(Liulevicius
[
221
, Theorem 3]
)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebras

(5.24)

\mathbb{E}\coloneqq\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[h_{0},h_{1},v,w]/(h_{0}h_{1},h_{1}^{3},vh_{1},h_{0}^{2}w-v^{2})

with
h_{0}\in\Ext^{1,1}
,
h_{1}\in\Ext^{1,2}
,
v\in\Ext^{3,7}
, and
w\in\Ext^{4,12}
.

Thus
\mathbb{E}
acts on the
E_{2}
-page of the Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence of any
\mathit{ko}
-module. This action commutes with all differentials, and is useful for solving extension problems:
h_{0}
lifts to multiplication by
2
,
h_{1}
to
\eta
,
v
to
a
, and
w
to
\beta
.
See
Figure

8
for a picture of
\mathbb{E}
.

Figure 8
.
The
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebra
\mathbb{E}\coloneqq\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{*,*}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2)
described in
5.23
.

The following theorem is surely well-known, but we were unable to find it in the literature, so we have included a proof.

Theorem 5.25

(Margolis’ theorem for
\mathit{ko}
-modules)
.

Let
M
be a bounded below
\mathit{ko}
-module of finite type. Any isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules

(5.26a)

\overline{\varphi}\colon H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\overline{N}\oplus F,

where
F
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module, lifts to a
\mathit{ko}
-module splitting: there is a
\mathit{ko}
-module
N
with
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(N)\cong\overline{N}
and a
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence

(5.26b)

\varphi\colon N\vee\bigvee_{i}\Sigma^{n_{i}}H\mathbb{Z}/2\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}M

such that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}\left(\bigvee\Sigma^{n_{i}}H\mathbb{Z}/2\right)\cong F
and the pullback map of
\varphi
on
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
is equal to
\overline{\varphi}
.

Proof.

Since
M
has finite type, the rank of
F
is countable, so we may induct starting with the lowest-degree summand in
F
, possible because
M
is bounded below. Therefore in the rest of the proof we will assume
F
is rank one:
F\cong\Sigma^{m}\mathcal{A}(1)
.

It suffices to produce
\mathit{ko}
-module maps
f\colon\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2\to M
and
g\colon M\to\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2
with
g\circ f\simeq\mathrm{id}
. To do this, run two instances of the Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence ((
5.17
)): one for
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(M,\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2)
and one for
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2,M)
. Since
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(H\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathcal{A}(1)
, as we noted above, the two
E_{2}
-pages are

(5.27)

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M),\Sigma^{m}\mathcal{A}(1))\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(\Sigma^{m}\mathcal{A}(1),H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)).

Margolis proved that
\mathcal{A}(1)
is both projective and injective as a graded
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
[
236
, Theorem 12.5, Proposition 12.8, Theorem 12.9]
(“graded” is important: see
[
276
]
), so both
E_{2}
-pages in (
5.27
) vanish for
s>0
, and for
s=0
, they coincide with graded Hom. This implies these Adams spectral sequences collapse for degree reasons, and that any
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module homomorphism
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\to\Sigma^{m}\mathcal{A}(1)
(resp.
\Sigma^{m}\mathcal{A}(1)\to H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
) lifts to a
\mathit{ko}
-module morphism
M\to\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2
(resp.
\Sigma^{m}H\mathbb{Z}/2\to M
) and this lift can be chosen to preserve composition up to
\mathit{ko}
-module homotopy equivalence. Thus it suffices to exhibit maps
f
and
g
with
g\circ f\simeq\mathrm{id}
at the level of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules, where they are the maps splitting the rank-one free summand off of
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
.
∎

Definition 5.28
.

A
stable isomorphism
of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules is an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module map
f\colon M\to N
whose kernel and cokernel are free over
\mathcal{A}(1)
.

Lemma 5.29

(
[
236
, Proposition 14.1]
)
.

If
f\colon M\to N
is a stable isomorphism, then there are free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
F
and
F^{\prime}
and an extension of
f
to an isomorphism
\overline{f}\colon M\oplus F\overset{\cong}{\to}N\oplus F^{\prime}
.

5.25
thus implies that a stable isomorphism on
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
may affect Whitney summands, but leaves everything we are actually interested in alone.

Remark 5.30
.

Margolis’ original theorem
[
235
]
is essentially the same result but with
\mathbb{S}
in place of
\mathit{ko}
and
H^{*}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z}/2)
in place of
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
. Using this, one can use a change-of-rings theorem to prove a weaker version of
5.25
: one requires
M\simeq\mathit{ko}\wedge X
, and the splitting is only as spectra, not
\mathit{ko}
-modules. See
[
89
, Theorem 3.22]
.
∎

5.2.
Examples of
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type

In this subsection, we will give several examples of
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type; we will use these examples to first see that
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type, and then in
5.55
compute
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
modulo Whitney summands.

Each of our examples is of the form
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
for some
X
; we will also compute
H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
as a module over the Steenrod subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(1)\coloneqq\langle\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\rangle
, finding four
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
N_{i}
that will play a central role in our computation. They have been studied in detail before by, e.g., Yu
[
342
]
and Bruner
[
62
]
; however, we warn the reader that our modules differ from theirs by suspensions.
This allows us to display the second page of the Adams spectral sequence. In each of these cases the spectral sequence collapses at
E_{2}
without
any extension problems, furnishing a proof that
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
is in fact of EA-type.

Example 5.31
.

It will be little surprise to the reader that
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is of EA-type. For degree reasons we will find
it slightly more convenient to work with
\mathit{ME}_{1,0}=(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
, which is equivalent to

\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
(see, e.g.,
[
204
, Lemma 2.6.5]
).

Let
N_{1}\coloneqq H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We draw this module in
Figure

9
, left.
\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}(N_{1})
was computed by Gitler–Mahowald–Milgram
[
138
, §2]
to be the
\mathbb{E}
-module

(5.32a)

\Ext(N_{1})\cong\mathbb{E}\{\alpha,a_{i}\mid i\geq 1\}/\mathcal{R}_{1}

where
\alpha\in\Ext^{0,0}
and
a_{i}\in\Ext^{0,4i-2}
, and the submodule
\mathcal{R}_{1}
of relations is

(5.32b)

\mathcal{R}_{1}=(h_{0}\alpha,\ v\alpha,\ h_{1}^{2}\alpha-h_{0}^{2}a_{1},\ h_{0}^{4}a_{2},\ h_{1}a_{i},\ va_{i}-h_{0}^{3}a_{i+1},\ wa_{i}-h_{0}^{4}a_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

We draw this in
Figure

9
, right; all
differentials vanish either for degree reasons or because they must commute with
h_{0}
or
h_{1}
, and there are no
extension problems left after taking the
\mathbb{E}
-module structure into account. Therefore as

\mathit{ko}_{*}
-modules,

(5.33a)

\mathit{ko}_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1,0})\cong\mathit{ko}_{*}\{\overline{\alpha},\overline{a}_{i}\mid i\geq 1\}/\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}

where
\lvert\overline{\alpha}\rvert=0
and
\lvert\overline{a}_{i}\rvert=4i-2
, and the submodule
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
of relations is

(5.33b)

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}=(2\overline{\alpha},\ a\overline{\alpha},\ \eta^{2}\overline{\alpha}-4\overline{a}_{1},\ 16\overline{a}_{2},\ \eta\overline{a}_{i},\ a\overline{a}_{i}-8\overline{a}_{i+1},\ \beta\overline{a}_{i}-16\overline{a}_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

These relations imply
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,0}
is
of EA-type, and that
\overline{a}_{i}
generates
a cyclic summand of order
2^{4j+3}
(if
i=2j+1
) or
2^{4j}
(if
i=2j
); these are the long summands.
The classes

\beta^{n}\overline{\alpha}
and
\eta\beta^{n}\overline{\alpha}
,
n\geq 0
, generate short summands. There are no
Whitney summands.

Thus the same conclusions are true for
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
with the degrees shifted upwards by
1
. This recovers preexisting computations of
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
: Mahowald
[
227
, Lemma 7.3]
computes the groups
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
, and Bruner–Mira–Stanley–Snaith
[
59
, §3.1]
give the
\mathit{ko}_{*}
-module structure. ∎

Figure 9
.
Left: the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N_{1}\coloneqq H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. This is also
\widetilde{H}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z}/2)
shifted down in degree by
1
. Right:
\Ext(N_{1})
, the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral
sequence for
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
. All differentials and extension questions are trivial. We discuss
N_{1}
and its Adams spectral sequence in
5.31
.

Example 5.34
.

Let
A_{4}
denote the alternating group on four letters. Then
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[u,v,w]/(u^{3}+v^{2}+w^{2}+vw)

with
\lvert u\rvert=2
and
\lvert v\rvert=\lvert w\rvert=3

[
241
, Theorem 2.6]
. The Steenrod squares of the generators are
\mathrm{Sq}(u)=u+v+w+u^{2}
,

\mathrm{Sq}(v)=v+u^{2}+uw+v^{2}
, and
\mathrm{Sq}(w)=w+u^{2}+uv+v^{2}
.
46
46

46

Mitchell–Priddy do not explicitly give the Steenrod squares, but they can be worked out from their proof. They are also given in
[
89
, Proposition 5.1]
.

One can compute that as an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module,
\widetilde{H}^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a direct sum of countably many
free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules whose lowest-degree elements are in degrees
6
,
8
,
12
,
12
, …, along with exactly
one indecomposable summand
S
; we define
N_{2}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-2}S
.
47
47

47

N_{2}
is called
\Sigma^{-2}P_{2}

in
[
62
, Definition 4.4]
and
R_{5}
in
[
49
,
89
]
.

We draw a picture of
N_{2}
in

Figure

10
, left. Yu
[
342
, Theorem 3.1]
showed that there is an
\mathbb{E}
-module isomorphism

(5.35a)

\Ext(N_{2})\cong\mathbb{E}\{\beta,\beta^{\prime},b_{i}\mid i>0\}/\mathcal{R}_{2},

where
\beta\in\Ext^{0,0}
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\Ext^{3,10}
,
b_{i}\in\Ext^{0,4i-3}
, and the submodule
\mathcal{R}_{2}
of relations is

(5.35b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}=(

\displaystyle h_{0}\beta,\ h_{1}^{2}\beta,\ v\beta,\ w\beta-h_{1}\beta^{\prime},\ h_{0}\beta^{\prime},\ v\beta^{\prime},\ h_{0}b_{1}-h_{1}\beta,

\displaystyle h_{0}^{3}b_{2},\ h_{1}b_{i},\ vb_{i}-h_{0}^{3}b_{i+1},\ wb_{i}-h_{0}^{4}b_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

We draw the Adams chart for
\Ext(N_{2})
in
Figure

10
, right. Because
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is stably isomorphic to
\Sigma^{2}N_{2}
, Margolis’ theorem for
\mathit{ko}
-modules (
5.25
) implies that there is a
\mathit{ko}
-module
M_{2}
with
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M_{2})\cong N_{2}
and that (the
2
-completion of)
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}
is
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalent to a wedge sum of
\Sigma^{2}M_{2}
and a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
. Thus
\Ext(N_{2})
is the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence computing
\pi_{*}(M_{2})_{2}^{\wedge}
, which is a summand of
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*+2}(BA_{4})_{2}^{\wedge}
.

Similarly to what we saw in
5.31
for
N_{1}
, all differentials in this spectral sequence vanish because they are
\mathbb{E}
-linear, and the
\mathbb{E}
-action determines all extensions, so there is a
\mathit{ko}_{*}
-module isomorphism

(5.36a)

\pi_{*}(M_{2})\cong\mathit{ko}_{*}\{\overline{\beta},\overline{\beta}{}^{\prime},\overline{b}_{i}\mid i\geq 1\}/\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{2},

where
\lvert\overline{\beta}\rvert=0
,
\lvert\overline{\beta}^{\prime}\rvert=7
,
\lvert\overline{b}_{i}\rvert=4i-3
, and the submodule
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
of relations is

(5.36b)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{2}=(

\displaystyle 2\overline{\beta},\ \eta^{2}\overline{\beta},\ a\overline{\beta},\ \beta\overline{\beta}-\eta\overline{\beta}{}^{\prime},\ 2\overline{\beta}{}^{\prime},\ a\overline{\beta}{}^{\prime},\ 2\overline{b}_{1}-\eta\overline{\beta},

\displaystyle 8\overline{b}_{2},\ \eta\overline{b}_{i},\ a\overline{b}_{i}-8\overline{b}_{i+1},\ \beta\overline{b}_{i}-16\overline{b}_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

Inverting
\beta
, one sees that
M
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type;
\pi_{*}(M)
has a long summand of order
2^{4j+2}
in degree
8j+1
, a long summand of order
2^{4j+3}
in degree
8j+5
, and short summands in degrees
7,0\bmod 8
. There are no Whitney summands.

As discussed above,
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}\simeq\Sigma^{2}M_{2}\vee F
, where
F
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
, so
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}
is also of EA-type. It has long summands of order
2^{4j+2}
in degree
8j+3
and order
2^{4j+3}
in degree
8j+7
and short summands in degrees
1,2\bmod 8
, except not in degree
1
. In addition, the
H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands contribute Whitney summands to
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(BA_{4})
. See Bruner–Greenlees
[
60
, §7.7.E]
for prior work on
\mathit{ko}_{*}(BA_{4})
. ∎

Figure 10
.
Left: the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N_{2}
, the unique non-free summand of
\Sigma^{-2}\widetilde{H}^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Right:
\Ext(N_{2})
, the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral
sequence for a summand of
\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{-2}BA_{4}
. All differentials and extension questions are trivial. We discuss
N_{2}
and its Adams spectral sequence in
5.34
.

Example 5.37
.

Using the Künneth theorem and the description of
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
from
5.34
, one can show that as an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module,
\widetilde{H}^{*}(BA_{4}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a direct sum of a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module of countably infinite rank and exactly one non-free, indecomposable summand
S^{\prime}
; we let
N_{3}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-3}S^{\prime}
. We draw a picture of
N_{3}
in
Figure

11
, left. Margolis’ theorem for
\mathit{ko}
-modules (
5.25
) thus tells us that there is a connective
\mathit{ko}
-module
M_{3}
with
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M_{3})\cong N_{3}
such that
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
splits as a
\mathit{ko}
-module as a sum of
\Sigma^{3}M_{3}
and a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Yu
[
342
, Theorem 3.1]
exhibits an
\mathbb{E}
-module isomorphism

(5.38a)

\Ext(N_{3})\cong\mathbb{E}\{\gamma,c_{i}\mid i\geq 0\}/\mathcal{R}_{3},

with
\gamma\in\Ext^{2,8}
,
c_{i}\in\Ext^{0,4i}
, and the submodule
\mathcal{R}_{3}
of relations is

(5.38b)

\mathcal{R}_{3}=(h_{0}\gamma,\ h_{1}^{2}\gamma-wc_{0},\ v\gamma,\ h_{0}c_{0},\ h_{0}^{2}c_{1},\ h_{1}c_{i},\ vc_{i}-h_{0}^{3}c_{i+1},\ wc_{i}-h_{0}^{4}c_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

We draw a picture of this
\mathbb{E}
-module in
Figure

11
, right; it is the
E_{2}
-page of the Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence computing
\pi_{*}(M_{3})_{2}^{\wedge}
.

In a pattern that is probably increasingly clear, all differentials vanish because they commute with the
\mathbb{E}
-action, and there are no hidden extensions, so we obtain a
\mathit{ko}_{*}
-module isomorphism

(5.39a)

\pi_{*}(M_{3})\cong\mathit{ko}_{*}\{\overline{\gamma},\overline{c}_{i}\mid i\geq 0\}/\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{3},

with
\lvert\overline{\gamma}\rvert=6
,
\lvert\overline{c}_{i}\rvert=4i
, and the submodule
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{3}
equal to

(5.39b)

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{3}=(2\overline{\gamma},\ \eta^{2}\overline{\gamma}-\beta\overline{c}_{0},\ a\overline{\gamma},\ 2\overline{c}_{0},\ 4\overline{c}_{1},\ \eta\overline{c}_{i},\ a\overline{c}_{i}-8\overline{c}_{i+1},\ \beta\overline{c}_{i}-16\overline{c}_{i+2}\text{ for all }i),

Inverting
\beta
, we see that
M_{3}
is of EA-type; its homotopy groups have no Whitney summands and have short summands in degrees
6,7\bmod 8
. There is a long summand of the form
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4j+1}
in degree
8j
and another of the form
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4j+2}
in degree
8j+4
for all
j\geq 0
. Thus
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
is also of EA-type, and the short and long summands in it homotopy can be worked out similarly to what we did for
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}
in
5.34
; there are also Whitney summands. ∎

.

\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N_{3}
, the unique non-free summand of
\Sigma^{-3}\widetilde{H}^{*}(BA_{4}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Right:
\Ext(N_{3})

\mathit{ko}
-module summand

of

. All differentials and extension questions are trivial. See

for more information on
N_{3}
,

, and this Adams spectral sequence.

Example 5.40
.

Let
N_{0}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-1}H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We draw this module in
Figure

12
, left.
\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}(N_{0})
was computed by Gitler–Mahowald–Milgram
[
138
, §2]
to be the
\mathbb{E}
-module

(5.41a)

\Ext(N_{0})\cong\mathbb{E}\{\delta,d_{i}\mid i\geq 0\}/\mathcal{R}_{4},

where
\delta\in\Ext^{1,2}
, and
d_{i}\in\Ext^{0,4i-1}
. The submodule
\mathcal{R}_{4}
of relations is

(5.41b)

\mathcal{R}_{4}=(h_{0}d_{0},\ h_{0}\delta,\ h_{1}^{2}\delta-vd_{0},\ v\delta,\ h_{1}d_{i},\ vd_{i}-h_{0}^{3}d_{i+1},\ wd_{i}-h_{0}^{4}d_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

We draw this in
Figure

12
, right. All
differentials and
extension problems are trivial, as in previous examples; therefore we learn that as

\mathit{ko}_{*}
-modules,

(5.42a)

\mathit{ko}_{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma})\cong\mathit{ko}_{*}\{\overline{\delta},\overline{d}_{i}\mid i\geq 0\}/\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{4},

where
\lvert\overline{\delta}\rvert=2
and
\lvert\overline{d}_{i}\rvert=4i
. (The shift in degree is because
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Sigma N_{0}
.) The submodule
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{4}
is

(5.42b)

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{4}=(2\overline{d}_{0},\ 2\overline{\delta},\ \eta^{2}\overline{\delta}-a\overline{d}_{0},\ a\overline{\delta},\ \eta\overline{d}_{i},\ a\overline{d}_{i}-8\overline{d}_{i+1},\ \beta\overline{d}_{i}-16\overline{d}_{i+2}\text{ for all }i).

These relations imply
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
is
of EA-type: for
k\geq 0
,
\beta^{k}\overline{\delta}{}^{\prime}
and
\eta\beta^{k}\overline{\delta}{}^{\prime}
generate short summands in degrees
8k+2
, resp.
8k+3
, and
\overline{d}_{i}
generates a long summand isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4+4j}
(
i=2j+1
) or
\mathbb{Z}/2^{1+4j}
(
i=2j
) in degree
4i
. There are no Whitney summands.

This Adams spectral sequence computation is far from new: Giambalvo
[
136
]
and Kirby–Taylor
[
194
]
study the Adams spectral sequence for
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
, and Campbell
[
64
, Example 6.6]
studies it for
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
.

Figure 12
.
Left: the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N_{0}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-1}H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Right:
\Ext(N_{0})
, the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence for
\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
. All differentials and extension questions are trivial. See
5.40
for more information.

∎

Lemma 5.43
.

Let
X
be a space or spectrum of finite type such that as
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules,
H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Sigma^{n}N_{i}\oplus F
where

0\leq i\leq 3
and
F
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module. Then
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
is of EA-type.

Proof.

First assume
F=0
. The arguments we gave in
5.31
,
5.34
,
5.37
, and
5.40
showing those
\mathit{ko}
-modules
are finite type used nothing more about them than the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on their mod
2
cohomology, so the
proofs generalize to
X
. If
F\not\cong 0
,
5.25
implies
\mathit{ko}\wedge X\simeq\overline{M}\vee E
,
where
E
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
and
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\overline{M})\cong\Sigma^{n}N_{i}
for some
i
,
so the lemma follows by reducing to the previous case.
∎

Remark 5.44
.

Not all
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type look like the ones in these examples. Let
C
denote the cofiber of the map
B\mathrm{Pin}_{2}^{-}\hookrightarrow B\mathrm{Spin}_{3}
sending a rank-
2
pin
-
vector bundle
V
to
V\oplus\mathrm{Det}(V)
. Bayen–Bruner
[
46
, Corollary 3]
show that
\mathit{ko}\wedge C
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type, but its cohomology looks nothing like the
N_{i}
, and its Adams spectral sequence has both differentials and hidden extensions (
ibid.
, Theorem 4).
∎

Lemma 5.45

(Yu
[
342
, Lemma 2.5]
)
.

There are stable isomorphisms of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules (see
5.28
)
N_{0}\otimes N_{i}\simeq N_{i}
,
N_{1}\otimes N_{1}\simeq N_{2}
,
N_{1}\otimes N_{2}\simeq N_{3}
, and
N_{1}\otimes N_{3}\simeq\Sigma^{4}N_{0}
.

Thus
N_{i}\otimes N_{j}
is stably equivalent to
N_{i+j\bmod 4}
up to suspensions.

Remark 5.46
.

5.45
establishes a fourfold periodicity in
N_{1}^{\otimes n}
, and therefore a fourfold periodicity in the
\mathit{ko}
-homology and spin bordism of
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\wedge n}
modulo Whitney summands. There is another fourfold periodicity over
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and
\mathit{ko}
, namely that of iterated Smith homomorphisms for the same vector bundle
[
80
, Examples 6.14 and 7.8]
; we saw this periodicity for the vector bundle
\sigma
in
3.25
.
These two periodicities are related: the diagonal map
B\mathbb{Z}/2\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\wedge n}
induces a map of Thom spectra
\Delta\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{(\ell-k)(\sigma-1)}\to\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
. The map
\Delta
intertwines the Smith map on one side with the spiral map
\phi_{\ell,k}
on the other.
∎

Remark 5.47
.

In
Figure

13
, we overlay the Ext charts in
Figures

9
,
10
,
11
, and
12
. This illustrates a phenomenon shown in
[
342
, Theorem 3.1]
: that, up to a shift in topological degree,
\Ext(N_{i})
is the submodule of
\Ext(N_{i-1\bmod 4})
consisting of elements in positive filtration. Of course, this is not a coincidence: in
[
83
]
, two of us joint with Pacheco-Tallaj will produce maps of
\mathit{ko}
-modules realizing these submodule inclusions, whose cofibers are sums of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
; thus they are “generalized Adams covers.” These ideas are due to Bruner
[
61
]
, who did this in the case
i=2
and presumably knew it for all
i
.
∎

.
The result of superimposing the right-hand sides of

,

,

, and

on the same axes, illustrating the close relationship between the Ext charts of the corresponding four
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules. See

.

Example 5.48
.

Let
V
be an elementary abelian
2
-group. For spaces
X
and
Y
, there is a natural splitting

(5.49)

\Sigma^{\infty}(X\times Y)\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}X\vee\Sigma^{\infty}Y\vee\Sigma^{\infty}(X\wedge Y);

applying this iteratively to the
B\mathbb{Z}/2
pieces of
BV
, one deduces a splitting
48
48

48

The analogous splitting with
B\mathbb{Z}/2
replaced with
\mathbb{T}
is the
James splitting
used in the computation of groups of weak topological phases of free fermions. See
[
121
, Theorem 11.8]
and
[
27
, §1.6]
.

(5.50)

\Sigma^{\infty}BV\simeq\bigvee_{k=1}^{n}\bigvee_{i=1}^{\binom{n}{k}}\Sigma^{\infty}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\wedge k}).

We showed
\mathit{ko}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
is of EA-type in
5.31
; then
5.43
and
5.45
allow us to inductively prove that
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\wedge k}
is also of EA-type. Thus we conclude that
\mathit{ko}\wedge BV
splits as a sum of
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type. This is the reason we call these modules
elementary abelian
-type.

Mitchell–Priddy
[
242
, Theorem A]
produce a stable splitting of
\Sigma^{\infty}BV
finer than the one in (
5.50
),
but
the splitting of
\mathit{ko}\wedge BV
induced by their work is coarser than the one obtained by using the methods in this section and tracking the Whitney summands. It would be interesting to compare these two splittings, which would generalize work of Bayen
[
47
, Proposition 3.4.4, §3.6.3]
applicable to the case
n=2
; see also
[
61
, Theorem 4.4]
.
∎

Proposition 5.51
.

Let
i\coloneqq\ell\bmod 4\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
j\coloneqq\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
.
If at least one of
\ell
or
k
is positive,
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type and there is a stable isomorphism

(5.52)

H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}.

Proof.

By
5.43
, the second part of this proposition implies the first part. The Künneth theorem tells us
that since
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
was defined as a smash product of
\ell
copies of
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
and
k
copies of

(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
,
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is isomorphic to a tensor product of
\ell
copies of
N_{1}
and
k

copies of
N_{0}
, so it suffices to identify such a tensor product modulo free summands, which can be done by iteratively applying
5.45
.
Explicitly, we have
\ell=4j+i
for
j\geq 0
and
0\leq i<4
. Then

(5.53)

H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq N_{1}^{\otimes\ell}\otimes(\Sigma N_{0})^{\otimes k}\simeq\Sigma^{k}N_{1}^{\otimes(4j+i)}\otimes N_{0}^{\otimes k}.

Since
N_{1}^{\otimes(4j)}\simeq\Sigma^{4j}N_{0}
and
N_{0}\otimes N_{t}\simeq N_{t}
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}/4
, we obtain

(5.54)

H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\Sigma^{k}(N_{1})^{\otimes(4j)}\otimes N_{i}\otimes N_{0}^{\otimes k}\simeq\Sigma^{k}(\Sigma^{4j}N_{i})\otimes N_{0}^{\otimes k}\simeq\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}.\qed

Recall the function
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
from
0.18
.
5.55
implies that the lowest-degree nonzero element of
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
not contained in a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module summand is in degree
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
. We will see below that the same is true for non-Whitney summands in
\mathit{ko}_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
.

Proposition 5.55
.

Keep the notation from
5.51
, and for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\widetilde{m}\coloneqq\lfloor(n-4j-k)/8\rfloor
. Then for all
n
, there are isomorphisms

\mathit{ko}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})/(\text{\rm Whitney summands})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2,&n\equiv 1+\ell+k-2i\bmod 8,\ n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\\
\mathbb{Z}/2,&n\equiv 2+\ell+k-2i\bmod 8,\ n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\\
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+4-i},&n\equiv 3+\ell+k-2i\bmod 8,\ n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\\
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+5-i},&n\equiv 7+\ell+k-2i\bmod 8,\ n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

The
\mathit{ko}_{*}
-action on these groups is trivial on Whitney summands; the Bott class acts injectively on non-Whitney summands, and the class
\eta\in\mathit{ko}_{1}
acts injectively in all degrees where it is not ruled out by the orders of these groups.

Proof.

By Margolis’ theorem for
\mathit{ko}
-modules (
5.25
), free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module summands in cohomology split off as
H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands of a
\mathit{ko}
-module, which in this case are Whitney summands. Therefore we may ignore those summands and just focus on
N_{0}
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
, or
N_{3}
by
5.51
. Run the Adams spectral sequence for these modules; as worked out in
5.31
,
5.34
,
5.37
, and
5.40
, in all four cases the spectral sequence collapses without extension problems, leading to the
\mathit{ko}
-homology groups in the proposition statement.
∎

5.3.
Computing
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})

To discuss the free-to-interacting map, we need to apply Anderson duality. This modifies the discussion of short,
long, and Whitney summands slightly. Recall the “exponential fiber sequence” from
2.17
(
[
118
, (5.21)]
):

(5.56)

H\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\overset{\delta}{\longrightarrow}\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Lemma 5.57
.

If
M
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type, the map
\delta_{M}\colon I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}M\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}M
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module
equivalence.

Proof.

If we can show
H\mathbb{C}_{*}(M)=\pi_{*}(M)\otimes\mathbb{C}
vanishes, then we can plug that into (
5.56
) to deduce that

\delta_{M}
is an isomorphism on homotopy groups and hence an equivalence. Since
\pi_{*}(M)
is finitely generated in
each degree, it suffices to show that each homotopy group is torsion. The
H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands contribute
\mathbb{Z}/2

summands to homotopy, which are torsion, and the rest of
\pi_{*}(M)
injects into
\pi_{*}(M)[\beta^{-1}]
, which by
definition of EA-type is torsion, so
\pi_{*}(M)
is torsion and we conclude.
∎

The universal property of
I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
, namely that
\pi_{-n}(I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}X)\cong\Hom(\pi_{n}(X),\mathbb{C}^{\times})
,
implies that a direct-sum decomposition of
\pi_{n}(X)
induces a dual direct-sum decomposition of

(I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}X)^{n}
. Thus if
X
is a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type, we also get a dual direct-sum decomposition of

(I_{\mathbb{Z}}X)^{n+1}
by
5.57
. We refer to the duals of long, short, and Whitney summands as long, short, and
Whitney summands respectively, as it will never be ambiguous whether we are working with an EA-type
\mathit{ko}
-module or
its Anderson dual.

Now we can state our first main result, which is the computation of the
\mathit{ko}
-module version of the free-to-interacting map as discussed in
3.99
.

Theorem 5.58
.

On homotopy groups, the free-to-interacting map

\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}\colon\Sigma^{\ell-k-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})

has the following explicit description.

Let
\widetilde{m}\coloneqq\lfloor(d-\ell+i-k+1)/8\rfloor
.

Long summands, part 1:

For
d\equiv\ell+k-2i+2\bmod 8
such that
d\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-2
,
\pi_{-d}(\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k})
is a surjective map

(5.59a)

\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2^{4+4\widetilde{m}-i}

onto the unique long summand in this degree.

Long summands, part 2:

For
d\equiv\ell+k-2i+6\bmod 8
such that
d\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-2
,
\pi_{-d}(\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k})
is a surjective map

(5.59b)

\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2^{5+4\widetilde{m}-i}

onto the unique long summand in this degree.

Short summands:

For
d\equiv\ell+k-2i\bmod 8
or
d\equiv\ell+k-2i+1\bmod 8
such that
d\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)-2
,
\pi_{-d}(\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k})
is
an isomorphism

(5.59c)

\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}/2

onto the unique short summand in this degree.

In all other degrees the domain of the free-to-interacting map is zero; thus the image of the free-to-interacting
map does not intersect the Whitney summands nontrivially.

We now prove
5.58
in a series of lemmas (
5.60
,
5.66
, and
5.68
). Our argument proceeds by induction, first on
k
and then on
\ell
.

Lemma 5.60

(Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, §10]
)
.

5.58
is true for
k=1
and
\ell=0
, and for
k=0
and
\ell=1
.

Proof.

By
3.64
, our twisted ABS maps for the cases
k=1
,
\ell=0
, resp.
k=0
,
\ell=1
coincide with Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABS in their cases
s=-1
, resp.
s=1
; therefore the same is true for the respective free-to-interacting maps. Thus it suffices to observe that the description we gave in our theorem statement coincides with their
calculation in these cases.
∎

Recall from
4.58
the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
: the same as
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
, but with
\mathit{ko}
in place of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
.

Theorem 5.61
.

Modulo Whitney summands, the map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism.

Lemma 5.62
.

The map
\phi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
we defined in
Equation

4.42
induces an injective map on mod
2
cohomology.

Proof.

The map
\phi_{\ell,k}
in
Equation

4.42
is equivalently given by smashing

\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
with a map
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\to\Sigma\mathbb{S}
given by the composition of the zero section map

e_{\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\to\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}
with the crush map
c\colon\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to\Sigma\mathbb{S}
. The pullback
c^{*}\colon H^{*}(\Sigma\mathbb{S};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is
injective, since it is the inclusion of the degree-
1
part. Since we are over a field, it is still injective after tensoring
with
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
. For the second part of
\phi_{\ell,k}
, namely the zero section map, we
make use of the fiber sequence (see, e.g.,
[
80
, (7.1)]
)

(5.63)

\mathbb{S}\longrightarrow(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\xrightarrow{e_{\sigma}}\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}.

Using the induced long exact sequence in mod
2
cohomology
49
49

49

After applying the Thom isomorphism to
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, this long exact sequence can be identified with the usual Gysin sequence for the tautological line bundle over
B\mathbb{Z}/2
. See
[
80
, Remark 5.11]
.

and the fact that
H^{*}(\mathbb{S};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

concentrated in degree
0
, we see that
e_{\sigma}
is injective on mod
2
cohomology. Thus
after tensoring with
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, the map is still injective. ∎

See
Figure

14
for a proof of
5.62
.

Proof of
5.61
.

By the universal property of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
, this is equivalent to
asking that
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands, and this is what we prove.

In
5.51
we showed that
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong F\oplus\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}
, where
j=\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
,
i=\ell\bmod 4
, and
F
is a free summand. Thus the non-free summands in the cohomologies of
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}

and
\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
are graded isomorphic. By
5.62
,
\phi_{\ell,k}^{*}\colon H^{*}(\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is injective. Thus the
\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}
summand in the domain maps isomorphically onto its image. Because
\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}
contains no nonzero free submodule, the only way for this to happen is when the codomain is
\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}
plus a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module is for it to map isomorphically to
a
\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i}
direct summand. Taking Ext groups, this implies that the map of Adams spectral sequences induced by
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}
is an isomorphism except possibly for the pieces coming from the free summands. By Margolis’ theorem, those free summands do not have nontrivial
differentials or extension problems, and so pass to Whitney summands in
\mathit{ko}
-homology. Thus,
modulo Whitney summands,
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.64
.

The diagram

(5.65)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{\ell-k-2}\mathit{KO}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{3}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k+1}(\mathit{ko})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k+1}}

commutes.

Proof.

If one expands out the definitions of
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\phi}(\mathit{ko})
, given in
3.99
and
4.51
, (
5.65
) factors into a smash product of a number of simpler diagrams of
\mathit{ko}
-modules, each of which trivially commutes.
∎

Lemma 5.66
.

5.58
is true for
\ell=1
and
k
arbitrary.

Proof.

Induct on
k
; the case
k=1
is
5.60
. In
5.64
, we proved that the the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
commutes with the free-to-interacting
map. Therefore it suffices to show that
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,{k-1}})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands, which we proved in
5.61
. ∎

Remark 5.67
.

Another way to show that
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands is to identify the fiber
F
of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}
. It is possible to compute that
F
is, as a
\mathit{ko}
-module, equivalent to a sum of shifts
of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
, and so in particular the Bott class acts trivially on
F
. This implies
F\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{KO}\simeq F[\beta^{-1}]
is trivial (see, e.g., (
5.3
)), so after base-changing to
\mathit{KO}
, the Bott spiral map is an isomorphism. Since
base-changing to
\mathit{KO}
is injective on non-Whitney summands of homotopy groups for
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type, including both the domain and the codomain of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\phi_{\ell,k}
, that map is injective on homotopy groups modulo Whitney summands, which is enough to recover the consequences for the free-to-interacting map that prove

5.66
. We will use a similar strategy to prove
5.68
.
∎

Figure 14
.
The effect of the
spiral map
\phi_{\ell,k}
on mod
2
cohomology.
This map factors as the zero section map
e_{\sigma}
composed with the crush map
c
, all smashed with the identity on
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
.
We use
this map on cohomology in the proof of
5.66
.

Lemma 5.68
.

5.58
is true for all values of
k
and
\ell
.

We will prove this with another inductive argument, this time inducting on
\ell
. To do this, we need a map relating
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
. We will first construct and study this map, then return to
5.68
.

Proposition 5.69
.

The Thom spectrum
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
admits the structure of a CW-spectrum with one
k
-cell for each
k\geq 0
, and such that the attaching map of the
2
-cell to the
1
-cell is trivial.

Proof sketch.

This can be directly checked by showing that the attaching map of the
2
-cell to the
1
-cell in
(\mathbb{RP}^{n})^{1-\sigma}
is trivial for all
n\geq 2
. If
\lambda(n)
is the order of
\sigma
in
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{0}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})
, then
(\mathbb{RP}^{n})^{1-\sigma}\simeq(\mathbb{RP}^{n})^{(\lambda(n)-1)(\sigma-1)}
, so one can finish the proof by checking that the attaching map from the
2
-cell to the
1
-cell is trivial in the Thom spectrum of
(\lambda(n)-1)(\sigma-1)\to\mathbb{RP}^{n}
, and Adams
[
7
, Theorem 7.4]
calculates
\lambda(n)
for all
n
.
∎

Corollary 5.70
.

The
k
-cells of the above CW structure on
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
for
k\neq 1
, with the same attaching maps, define a CW spectrum
F
with an “inclusion map”
F\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
whose cofiber is the
1
-cell
\Sigma\mathbb{S}
.
50
50

50

The spectrum
\mathit{TJF}_{\infty}
studied in
[
219
,
45
,
218
,
318
]
appears to be a (nonconnective version of a) height-
2
analog of
F
.

In essence, we want to “forget to include” the
1
-cell of
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
to define
F
. This works if and only if no
2
-cell is nontrivially attached to the
1
-cell, which
5.69
verifies.
The following lemma appears in Bruner
[
61
, §2]
, who writes that it is probably originally due to Davis or Mahowald, and that he learned it from Stolz.

Lemma 5.71
.

With
F
as in
5.70
, the fiber of the map
\varsigma\colon\mathbb{S}\to F
defined by including the
0
-cell is a nontrivial map
\upsilon\colon\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\mathbb{S}
.

We draw this fiber sequence in
Figure

15
.

Definition 5.72
.

Let
\Upsilon_{\infty}
denote the
infinite seagull
, the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module defined to be the submodule of
\Sigma^{-3}\widetilde{H}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z}/2)
of elements in degrees

,

.

\mathcal{A}(1)

.
Though a priori this is only a

-module.

Definition 5.74
.

Recall the map
\lambda_{\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}
from
3.34
(specifically (
3.37
)). Because
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
is
(-2)
-connected,
\lambda_{\sigma}
factors through the
(-2)
-connected cover
\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}
as a map
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}
. Smash
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}
to define a
\mathit{ko}
-module spectrum map
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}
.

Proposition 5.75
.

As
\mathit{ko}
-module maps, the map
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
in
5.74
is equivalent to an odd multiple of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon
after
2
-completing.

Proof.

Using the
isomorphism from
5.31
and the equivalence
\mathrm{Map}_{R}(R\wedge A,B)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{S}}(A,B)
,

(5.76)

\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Map}_{\mathit{ko}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1},\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko})_{2}^{\wedge}\cong\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}{}^{0}(B\mathbb{Z}/2).

Since
\mathit{ko}
is the connective cover of
\mathit{KO}
,
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}{}^{0}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{0}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}

[
19
]
. We will show that both
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon
define odd elements of this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
after
2
-completion. Odd numbers can be inverted in the
2
-adics, so each map is an odd multiple of the other.
Given a map
f\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}
, consider its effect on
\pi_{0}
, where it induces a map
\pi_{0}f\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
. If
f\simeq 2g
, then
\pi_{0}f=2\pi_{0}g
, so it must be the zero map on
\mathbb{Z}/2
. We will show that for
f=\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
and
f=\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon
,
\pi_{0}f\neq 0
, so that both of these maps are odd.

For
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
, precompose with the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map
MT\mathrm{Spin}\to\mathit{ko}
and postcompose with
\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}
, as in
3.40
, to obtain a map
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}
that is precisely Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABS map
[
118
, §10]
(see
3.38
and
3.40
). Freed–Hopkins also show (
ibid.
, Corollary 9.85) that this map is an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
0
,
54
54

54

Freed–Hopkins only report the effect of the Anderson dual of their map, but since the homotopy groups of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
are torsion
[
17
]
, this implies their original map is also an isomorphism in degree
0
, using the universal property of the Anderson dual.
so the middle piece
\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
cannot be the zero map on
\pi_{0}
.

The cofiber of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon
is
\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}F
. Mahowald
[
227
, Lemma 7.2]
shows that,
2
-locally,
55
55

55

This is an equivalence of spectra, but Hill
[
162
, Lemma 3.3]
showed that the two sides are
not
equivalent as
\mathit{ko}
-modules, where we give
H\mathbb{Z}
the
\mathit{ko}
-algebra structure arising from the truncation map
\mathit{ko}\to H\mathbb{Z}
of ring spectra. (This lemma is present in the arXiv version of
[
162
]
but not the published version.)
Instead, we hypothesize that
\mathit{ko}\wedge F
is
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalent to the connective cover of the
L
-theory spectrum of
\mathbb{R}
, which is also known to split as
H\mathbb{Z}\vee\Sigma^{4}H\mathbb{Z}\vee\Sigma^{8}H\mathbb{Z}\vee\dotsb
as spectra but not
\mathit{ko}
-modules. See
[
311
,
159
]
.

(5.77)

\mathit{ko}\wedge F\simeq\bigvee_{n\geq 0}\Sigma^{4n}H\mathbb{Z}.

Plug this into the long exact sequence of homotopy groups induced by the fiber sequence
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\to\mathit{ko}\wedge F
; exactness forces
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon)
to be surjective, hence an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
.
∎

Proposition 5.78
.

Let

(5.79)

\upsilon_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}.

The diagram

(5.80)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{\ell-k-2}\mathit{KO}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{f2i}_{\ell-1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}\upsilon_{\ell,k}}

commutes after
2
-completion up to homotopy and multiplication by an odd integer (which is a unit in the
2
-adic integers).

The diagram (
5.80
) will play a key role in the proof of
5.68
completely analogous to the role of (
5.65
) in the proof of
5.66
.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
5.64
, but replacing “data inducing commutativity” with “data inducing commutativity after
2
-completion, up to homotopy and multiplication by an odd integer.” Also, keep the factors of
\mathit{ko}
instead of getting rid of them. At the end of this simplification process, we have learned that if the diagram

(5.81)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

commutes after
2
-completion, up to homotopy and multiplication by an odd integer, then we have finished the proof. And the former is exactly what we proved in
5.75
.
∎

Lemma 5.82
.

For
\ell,k\geq 1
,
H^{*}(F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module.

We will use a technique called
Margolis homology
to prove
5.82
, so we pause here to review this tool.

Definition 5.83
.

Let
Q_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
and
Q_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}+\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
. Then for
i=0,1
, the Adem relations imply
Q_{i}^{2}=0
, so one may regard any
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N
as a cochain complex with differential
Q_{i}
. The homology of this complex is called the
Margolis homology

H^{*}(M;Q_{i})

[
5
,
236
]
. We say a bounded-below
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
M
is
Q_{i}
-local
if
H^{*}(M;Q_{i})\neq 0
and
H^{*}(M;Q_{1-i})=0
.

We need a few key facts about Margolis homology.

Theorem 5.84

(Wall, Adams–Margolis
[
5
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Let
f\colon M\to N
be a map of bounded-below
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules that is an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology. Then there are free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
and an isomorphism
M\oplus F_{1}\overset{\cong}{\to}N\oplus F_{2}
extending
f
. In particular, if
M
is a bounded-below
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module such that
H^{*}(M;Q_{0})=0
and
H^{*}(M;Q_{1})=0
, then
M
is free.

Adams–Margolis attribute this special case of their more general result to unpublished work of Wall.

Proposition 5.85

(Yu
[
342
, Chapter 2]
)
.

For
i=0,1,2,3
,
N_{i}
is
Q_{1}
-local.

Proposition 5.86

(Künneth formula for Margolis homology
[
236
, Chapter 19, Proposition 17(b)]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules and
i=0,1
. Then there are natural isomorphisms
H^{*}(M;Q_{i})\otimes H^{*}(N;Q_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{*}(M\otimes N;Q_{i})
.

Proof of
5.82
.

By
5.40
and
5.73
,
H^{*}(F\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Sigma\Upsilon_{\infty}\otimes N_{0}
. Since
N_{0}
is bounded below and
Q_{1}
-local (
5.85
) and
\Upsilon_{\infty}
is
Q_{0}
-local
[
62
, Proposition 4.2]
, then by the Künneth formula (
5.86
), the
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology of
\Sigma\Upsilon_{\infty}\otimes N_{0}
both vanish, so
\Sigma\Upsilon_{\infty}\otimes N_{0}
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module by
5.84
.

To finish, use the (usual) Künneth formula on
F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}\simeq(F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k-1})\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
to show that
H^{*}(F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k-1};\mathbb{Z}/2)\otimes(\Sigma\Upsilon_{\infty}\otimes N_{0})
. Since the second term in the tensor product is free, the entire tensor product is a free module.
∎

Proof of
5.68
.

We will show that the map
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\upsilon_{\ell,k}
in (
5.79
) is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands after
2
-completion. With this in hand, the same strategy from the proof of
5.66
, but this time using the commutativity result from
5.78
, implies the conclusion of the lemma, albeit only after
2
-completion and multiplication by an odd integer. Since the homotopy groups of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
are
2
-torsion, this suffices to prove the lemma.

Now back to
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\upsilon_{\ell,k}
. The domain and codomain of this map are the Anderson duals of
\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
, resp.
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
, which have torsion homotopy groups by
5.55
. Therefore by
5.57
it suffices to prove
I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\chi
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands after
2
-completion, and by the universal property of
I_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
, it suffices to prove the same for
\upsilon_{\ell,k}
.

Since the domain and codomain of
\upsilon_{\ell,k}
, namely
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
, resp.
\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
, are
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type, the vertical maps in the diagram

(5.87)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{KO}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon_{\ell,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\upsilon}_{\ell,k}\coloneqq\upsilon_{\ell,k}[\beta^{-1}]}

are injective modulo Whitney summands on homotopy groups, so it suffices to prove the map
\widetilde{\upsilon}_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{KO}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathit{KO}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
, i.e. the map induced from
\upsilon_{\ell,k}
after base changing to
\mathit{KO}
, is an equivalence after
2
-completion. The cofiber of
\widetilde{\upsilon}_{\ell,k}
is
\mathrm{cofib}(\upsilon_{\ell,k})[\beta^{-1}]
; since
\upsilon_{\ell,k}
is obtained by smashing
\Sigma^{-1}\upsilon
with
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}}
,
5.71
implies the cofiber of
\upsilon_{\ell,k}
is
\mathit{ko}\wedge F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
. By
5.82
,
H^{*}(F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module, so Margolis’ theorem for
\mathit{ko}
-modules (
5.25
) implies that after
2
-completion
\mathit{ko}\wedge F\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
—in particular, the Bott class
\beta
acts by
0
on this
\mathit{ko}
-module. Thus,
\mathrm{cofib}(\upsilon_{\ell,k})[\beta^{-1}]\simeq 0
, but this was
\mathrm{cofib}(\widetilde{\upsilon}_{\ell,k})
, so
\widetilde{\upsilon}_{\ell,k}
is an equivalence after
2
-completion, and as noted above this suffices.
∎

5.4.
Computation of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})

We defined the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
in two cases:
\ell>0
and
k>0
(
4.41
) and
\ell\geq 3
and
k=0
(
4.48
).
Throughout this subsection, assume both
\ell
and
k
are positive,
so we are in the first case. We will return to the second case in
Section

6.2
.

Recall that in
4.58
, we defined
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(\mathit{ko})
to be the analog of
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
from
4.51
, but with
\mathit{ko}
in place of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
.
Our goal in this subsection is to prove the following theorem, where we keep the following notation from
5.55
:
i\coloneqq\ell\bmod 4
(thought of as an integer between
0
and
3
, not as an element of
\mathbb{Z}/4
),
j\coloneqq\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
, and
\widetilde{n}\coloneqq n-4j-k
. In addition, let
m\geq 0
, and let
c(m)
be equal to
1
if
m
is odd and
0
if
m
is even.

Theorem 5.88
.

The map
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k}
sends long summands to long summands. Specifically, restricted to the long summand
\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+4}\subset\pi_{4j+k+4m-i+3}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
,
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
surjects onto the long summand
\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+3}\subset\pi_{4j+k+4m-i+2}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k})
.

The orders of these long summands were computed in
5.55
; the key takeaway is that
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(\mathit{ko})
is always surjective on these long summands, with kernel isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Recall that
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
(
4.51
) is the composition of an Euler map
e_{\sigma}
(
Equation

3.20
) and a map induced from the diagonal
\Delta\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
(
4.39
). We will compute the effect of
e_{\sigma}
on the long summand in §
5.4.1
and of
\Delta
on the long summand in §
5.4.2
. We summarize these two calculations in
5.89
and
5.106
, respectively, and these jointly imply
5.88
.

5.4.1.
The Euler map

Let
r_{\ell,k}\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
denote the smash product of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ME}_{\ell.k-1}}
and the map
r\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
taking the cofiber of the inclusion of the basepoint. Then, after applying
\Sigma^{\infty}
(in particular, for homology and cohomology),
r
is projection onto a direct summand, and
\iota\colon B\mathbb{Z}/2\to(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}
is a section.

In this subsubsection, we make the following computation.

Proposition 5.89
.

The composition
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}\coloneqq r_{\ell,k}\circ e_{\sigma}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
sends long summands to long summands; specifically, restricted to the long summand

(5.90a)

\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+4}\subset\pi_{\ell+k+4m-2i+3}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}),

\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}
surjects onto the long summand

(5.90b)

\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+3}\subset\pi_{\ell+k+4m-2i+2}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)).

By
5.55
,
\pi_{4j+k+4m-i+2}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+})
contains two long summands isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+3}
and
\mathbb{Z}/2^{2m+c(m)+i+4}
, coming respectively from
\Sigma B\mathbb{Z}/2
and from the basepoint. The diagonal map, which we study in §
5.4.2
, will destroy the long summand coming from the basepoint, so for the rest of this subsubsection, we will throw out the basepoint, as the long summand we want to surject onto is in the
B\mathbb{Z}/2
summand.

Lemma 5.91
.

The fiber of
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\to\Sigma B\mathbb{Z}/2
is
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. Thus the fiber of
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}\wedge\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}}
is
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
.

Proof.

Compose
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}
with the
homotopy equivalence

e_{\sigma}\colon\Sigma B\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Sigma^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
; additivity of Euler classes implies the composition is
e_{2\sigma}\colon(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\to\Sigma^{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
. Kirby–Taylor
[
194
, Lemma 7]
identify the fiber of
e_{2\sigma}
as
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
; see also
[
80
, Example 7.37]
.
∎

We thus would like to understand
\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
.

Definition 5.92
.

We introduce four more
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules, which we draw in
Figure

16
.

•

F_{0}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{H}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, the unique nonsplit extension of
\mathbb{Z}/2
by
\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2
.

•

F_{3}\coloneqq\Sigma\mathcal{A}(1)/(\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\mathrm{Sq}^{2})
.

•

F_{2}
is the unique nonsplit extension of
J
by
\Sigma J
.

•

F_{1}\coloneqq\Sigma^{5}F_{3}^{\vee}
.

Here
(\text{--})^{\vee}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector space dual; see Baker
[
38
, §3]
for a discussion of the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on the dual of an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module. In practice, if one is familiar with the
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}
diagrams of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules, the dual of a finitely generated
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module is obtained by turning the diagram upside down; the dual of a class in degree
t
lies in degree
-t
.

Figure 16
.
The
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
F_{i}
from
5.92
. Figure adapted from Pearson
[
254
, (8-14)]
.

Definition 5.93
.

Let
R_{2}
denote the kernel of the unique nonzero
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module map
\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{A}(1)\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{Z}/2
, let
\Omega\colon\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{\mathcal{A}(1)}\to\mathit{\mathscr{M}od}_{\mathcal{A}(1)}
be
\Sigma R_{2}\otimes\text{--}
, and let
\Omega^{n}
denote the result of applying
\Omega

n
times.

Lemma 5.94

(Powell
[
260
, §6]
)
.

For
i=0,1,2,3
,
\Omega^{i}F_{0}\simeq\Sigma^{2i}F_{i}
.
