Proposition 5.101
.

Let
i,j
be as in
5.55
and
\mathrm{ldeg}
be as in
0.18
. Let
a(i)=1
if
i=0
or
3
and
a(i)=0
otherwise.
There is an isomorphism

\mathit{ko}_{n}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})/(\text{\rm Whitney summands})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2,&\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 0,1,3,4\bmod 8,\,n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+a(i)\\
\mathbb{Z}/2,&i=2,\ \widetilde{n}\equiv 0\bmod 8,n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)\\
\mathbb{Z}/4,&i\neq 2,\ \widetilde{n}+i\equiv 2\bmod 8,\,n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+a(i)\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

The Bott class acts injectively on non-Whitney summands and trivially on Whitney summands; the class
\eta\in\mathit{ko}_{1}
acts injectively in all degrees on non-Whitney summands where it is not ruled out by the orders of these groups, except when
i=2
and
\widetilde{n}=0
.

Proof.

Use the Adams spectral sequence as usual.

5.97
gives us the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
H^{*}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
modulo free summands, and
5.99
calculates the Ext of this module (again, modulo Ext of free summands). Margolis’ theorem (
5.25
) implies free summands lift to Whitney summands, so we ignore them. The Adams spectral sequence then collapses for degree reasons, implying the
\mathit{ko}
-homology groups in the proposition statement. The actions of
\beta
, resp.
\eta
follow from the actions of
w
, resp.
h_{1}
on the
E_{\infty}
-page.
∎

Proof of
5.89
.

We will consider the long exact sequence in
\mathit{ko}
-homology associated to the fiber sequence
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\to\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\xrightarrow{e_{\sigma}}\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}
constructed in
5.91
. This is a long exact sequence of
\mathit{ko}
-modules, in that it commutes with the action of classes in
\mathit{ko}_{*}
, such as
\eta\in\mathit{ko}_{1}
and the Bott class
\beta\in\mathit{ko}_{8}
. Thus in particular for any
n
we have the following commutative diagram whose rows are exact.

(5.102)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n+7}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n+7}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

There are two cases where this diagram contains long summands:
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 3\bmod 8
and
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 7\bmod 8
. In both cases we will plug in the
\mathit{ko}
-homology groups that we calculated in
5.55
and
5.101
and let exactness finish the proof for us, but the two cases will differ a little. In the rest of the proof,
W
will denote a Whitney summand, which is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector space which
\eta
and
\beta
act trivially on. In particular, we do not assert all
W
s appearing in the same diagram are isomorphic.

First assume
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 3\bmod 8
.
5.55
and
5.101
imply that (
5.102
) takes the following form:

(5.103)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+8+i}\oplus W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+7+i}\oplus W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+4+i}\oplus W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+3+i}\oplus W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}16\,0\\
0\,\,0\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}16\,0\\
0\,\,0\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}1\,0\\
0\,0\end{bmatrix}}

Let
x\in\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,n-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2))
be a generator of the
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+3+i}
summand. We would like to show that
\partial(x)=0
, so that by exactness,
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}
surjects onto the summand generated by
x
; the degrees of the summands in
\mathit{ko}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
imply that the long summand of
\mathit{ko}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
surjects onto the long summand of
\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,n-1}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2))
, which would finish the proof of the proposition in this case.

With respect to the splitting
\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus W
chosen in (
5.103
), let
\partial(x)=(a,b)
. By
5.101
,
\eta
zeroes out the Whitney summand and carries the
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand isomorphically onto the non-Whitney summand of
\mathit{ko}_{n}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})
. Thus
\eta(\partial(x))
is nonzero if and only if
a\neq 0
; since
\eta(\partial(x))=\partial(\eta(x))
and
\eta(x)=0
by
5.55
, we get
a=0
. Thus
\partial(x)
is contained in the Whitney summand, so

(5.104)

\partial(\beta(x))=\beta(\partial(x))=0.

By exactness, there is a
z\in\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
such that
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}(z)=\beta(x)
. Since
\beta(x)
is four times a generator of
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+7+i}
,
2\beta(x)\neq 0
and therefore
2z\neq 0
, so
z
is not contained in a Whitney summand. Moreover,
z=16w
for some
w
: if it were not, then the order-
16
elements of
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}+8+i}
would be in the kernel of
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}
, which is the image of
\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\to\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
, but by
5.101
,
\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})
contains no elements of order
16
. Now, since
z=16w
,
z
is in the image of
\beta\colon\mathit{ko}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to\mathit{ko}_{n+8}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. Let
\widetilde{z}
be a preimage of
z
; then
\widetilde{e}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{z})=x
, so
\partial(x)=0
by exactness and we are done.

The case
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 7\bmod 8
is nearly the same. The orders of the long summands are slightly different in ways that do not affect the proof; the only meaningful difference is that, when
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 7\bmod 8
,
\mathit{ko}_{n}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\cong 0
and
\mathit{ko}_{n-1}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})
vanishes apart from Whitney summands, so the argument with
\eta
is vacuous.
∎

5.4.2.
The diagonal

Our main result in this subsubsection is
5.106
, that
\Delta\colon B\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
(
4.39
) induces an isomorphism on non-Whitney summands after smashing with
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
. Together with
5.89
, this will prove
5.88
, finishing the calculation of the effect of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
on long summands.

Lemma 5.105
.

\mathrm{id}\wedge\Delta\colon\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k+1}
is an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology.

Proof.

The Künneth formula for Margolis homology (
5.86
) immediately reduces us to the case
\ell=k=0
, i.e. showing that
\Delta\colon\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
is an isomorphism on Margolis homology.

Write
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x]
and
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong U\cdot H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong U\cdot\mathbb{Z}/2[a,b]
, where
\lvert x\rvert=\lvert a\rvert=\lvert b\rvert=1
, where
a
, resp.
b
are dual to the left, resp. right, factors in
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
and
U
is the Thom class.
Using our identifications of the mod
2
cohomology of
B\mathbb{Z}/2
and
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(1-\sigma)}
as shifts of the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
N_{0}
and
N_{1}
(
5.31
and
5.40
) together with Yu’s calculation
[
342
, Chapter 2]
of the
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-homology of
N_{0}
and
N_{1}
(see
5.85
), we get

•

\widetilde{H}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;Q_{0})=0
and
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)};Q_{0})=0
.

•

\widetilde{H}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;Q_{1})=\mathbb{Z}/2\cdot x^{2}
and
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)};Q_{1})=\mathbb{Z}/2\cdot Ux
.

Thus, by the Künneth formula for Margolis homology (
5.86
),
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1};Q_{0})=0
and
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1,1};Q_{1})=\mathbb{Z}/2\cdot Uab
. That is, for
i=0,1
, the
Q_{i}
-Margolis homologies of
\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
are abstractly isomorphic, and vacuously
\Delta^{*}
is an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
-homology. For
Q_{1}
-homology, since
\Delta
pulls both
a
and
b
back to
x
,
\Delta^{*}(Uab)=x^{2}
, so
\Delta^{*}
is also an isomorphism on
Q_{1}
-homology.
∎

Corollary 5.106
.

\mathrm{id}\wedge\Delta\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k+1}
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands.

Proof.

5.16
and
5.105
imply that
\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{ko}}\wedge\Delta
induces an isomorphism of both
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-homology on
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
, so by
5.84
, it is an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module isomorphism modulo free summands. By Margolis’ theorem (
5.25
), these free summands lift to Whitney summands, and the non-free summand(s) are isomorphic, inducing an isomorphism in the Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence for the complements of the Whitney summands. Thus we obtain an isomorphism of
2
-completed homotopy groups modulo Whitney summands, but since
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is trivial at odd primes if
(k,\ell)\neq(0,0)
, this suffices.
∎

We discuss a consequence for dpin bordism specifically. Since the worldsheet in Type I superstring theory has a dpin structure
[
182
, §6]
, this may be of independent interest.

Proposition 5.107
.

Let
X
be a space. Then for
k<8
, there is an isomorphism

(5.108)

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{DPin}}(X)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X)\oplus H_{k}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H_{k-2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H_{k-4}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\oplus}H_{k-4}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H_{k-6}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H_{k-6}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2).

Moreover, under the interpretation of dpin structures as spin structures on the orientation double cover
[
182
, §6.1]
, the
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X)
summand is precisely the summand represented by oriented manifolds.

5.107
provides a conceptual explanation for the appearance of some of the generators and bordism invariants for low-degree dpin bordism in
[
182
, Appendices E and F]
.

Proof.

By
1.118
, dpin bordism is exactly the spin bordism of
\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
, which in degrees
7
and below coincides with the
\mathit{ko}
-homology of
\mathit{ME}_{1,1}
. Thus (
5.108
) follows from
5.106
together with the claim that the degrees of the Whitney summands in
\mathit{MT}\mathrm{DPin}
in degrees
7
and below are
0
,
2
,
4
,
4
,
6
, and
6
. This second claim is proven in
[
182
, (F.11)]
(see also (
ibid.
, Figure 6)). The rest of the proof, identifying the unique non-Whitney summand as the oriented dpin manifolds, follows from the description of the map
\mathrm{Spin}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathrm{DPin}
in
[
182
, §6.1]
.
∎

One can extend
5.107
into higher degrees by computing the degrees of more Whitney summands in
\mathit{MT}\mathrm{DPin}
.

6.
Computing over
MT\mathrm{Spin}

In this section, we finish the proofs of our main computational theorems by lifting our results from
\mathit{ko}
to
MT\mathrm{Spin}
. In §
6.1
, we give a complete description of the maps
F2I_{\ell,k}
,
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\phi}
, and
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
in all dimensions, modulo Whitney summands, in
6.15
,
6.16
, and
6.24
.
56
56

56

As in the previous section, we assume
\ell,k>0
for
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
.
This corresponds in physics to starting the Bott spiral in one of the Altland–Zirnbauer classes D, BDI, or DIII, as well as any of the discrete real Altland–Zirnbauer classes.

In §
6.2
, we consider Bott spirals beginning with a continuous Altland–Zirnbauer symmetry type, then applying
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
as above. These “mixed discrete-continuous” symmetry types are described by fermionic tensor products of a continuous Altland–Zirnbauer symmetry group
G_{s}
(see
2.24
) with
E_{\ell,k}
. In
6.49
,
6.50
, and
6.60
, we compute the free-to-interacting maps, the
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
maps, and the
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
maps for these cases, and our results suggest these mixed discrete-continuous spiral maps could model the Bott spiral starting in the Altland–Zirnbauer classes AI, AII, C, CI, and CII. However, it is not possible in these cases to write down a full period of the spiral (see
4.73
), providing evidence that our fully discrete model of the Bott spiral is the correct one. We also use these results to finish the computation of
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,0}^{\psi}
in
6.53
.

Our results rely on
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
, generalizations of the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting
[
18
]
of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
to the twisted spin bordism spectra
\mathit{MT}H(K_{f}(s))
, where
K_{f}(s)
is the continuous fermionic group defined in
2.24
(see
Table

1
),
together with their interactions with Freed–Hopkins’ free-to-interacting maps
[
118
, §9.2.2]
. For
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
(
K_{f}(4)=\mathrm{SU}_{2}
), this is a theorem of Buchanan–McKean
[
63
]
and Mills
[
239
]
; in the remaining four cases, this is new. Two of us, joint with Pacheco-Tallaj, will prove these remaining cases in a separate paper
[
83
]
.

In §
6.3
, we perform the complex analogs of these computations, working first over
\mathit{ku}
, then over
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
. Generally the statements are easier, and the proofs very similar, compared to their real analogs. Our main results are
6.82
,
6.83
, and
6.84
, which compute the free-to-interacting and spiral maps in this setting, modeling the Bott spiral beginning in classes A or AIII. See
Figure

3
for the Bott spiral beginning in class AIII, built by assembling these computations.

Lastly, in §
6.4
, we compute
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
bordism, extending work of Pedrotti
[
255
, Theorem 8.0.8]
in low dimensions. This is the last remaining computation of the twisted spin bordism groups for our discrete Altland–Zirnbauer symmetry groups.

6.1.
Lifting to
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and spiraling from
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}

In this subsection we lift from theorems for
\mathit{ko}
-modules, where the story is simpler, to theorems for
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-modules, which are the actual spectra classifying bordism and invertible field theories of interest. The lift essentially amounts to Anderson–Brown–Peterson’s splitting of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and the description of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation as the projection onto the first summand of this splitting.

Our main theorems in this section are
6.15
,
6.16
, and
6.24
. Essentially they say that the description of the free-to-interacting map is the same as in
5.58
and that
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
behave similarly to their analogues over
\mathit{ko}
.
However, to precisely state these theorems we must first introduce some definitions, so we do that, then state and prove our main theorems.

Our primary tool for lifting
\mathit{ko}
-theoretic statements to
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-level statements is the foundational work of Anderson–Brown–Peterson:

Theorem 6.1

(Anderson–Brown–Peterson
[
18
]
)
.

There are numbers
a_{i},b_{j},c_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and a
2
-local homotopy equivalence

(6.2)

MT\mathrm{Spin}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{a_{i}}\mathit{ko}}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}}\vee\bigvee_{k\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{c_{k}}H\mathbb{Z}/2},

where
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
denotes the
1
-connective cover of
\mathit{ko}
. The degrees of the first few shifts are

(6.3)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}(a_{i})}

\displaystyle=(0,8,16,16,24,24,24,24,\dotsc)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}(b_{j})}

\displaystyle=(8,16,16,24,24,24,24,\dotsc)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}(c_{k})}

\displaystyle=(20,22,24,26,26,\dotsc).

Moreover, the projection
\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}a_{1}}}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\mathit{ko}}
onto the first factor is equivalent to the (connective cover of the) Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map.

For any spectrum
X
, this induces a wedge-sum decomposition of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge X
which we call the
ABP decomposition
:

(6.4)

MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge X\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{a_{i}}\mathit{ko}\wedge X}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge X}\vee\bigvee_{k\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{c_{k}}H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X}.

Remark 6.5
.

One can verify using the defining property of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
that the Anderson dual of a wedge sum is the product of the Anderson duals of the summands. Therefore (
6.4
) induces a direct product decomposition of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge X)
.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
X
be a space or spectrum such that
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge X)
and
\widetilde{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
are both torsion. Then
\pi_{*}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge X)
is also torsion.

Proof.

Using the splitting (
6.4
), all we need to verify is that
\pi_{*}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge X})
and
\pi_{*}({\color[rgb]{0,1,1}H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X})
are torsion. The latter is isomorphic to the mod
2
homology of
X
, which is by definition torsion, so we focus on the former.

Taking the Postnikov cover induces a fiber exact sequence
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge X\to\mathit{ko}\wedge X\to\tau_{\leq 1}\mathit{ko}\wedge X
, which induces a long exact sequence on homotopy groups. By assumption,
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge X)
is torsion, so if we also know that
\pi_{*}(\tau_{\leq 1}\mathit{ko}\wedge X)
is torsion, we would be done. Since
\tau_{\leq 1}\mathit{ko}
has only two nonzero homotopy groups,
\pi_{0}\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\pi_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence can be used to show that, since
\widetilde{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is torsion, so is
\pi_{*}(\tau_{\leq 1}\mathit{ko}\wedge X)
.
∎

This in particular applies to
X=\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
—
5.55
shows its
\mathit{ko}
-homology is torsion, and for its integral homology, use the Thom isomorphism to identify
H_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z})
with certain twisted reduced homology groups of
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\ell+k}
, which have been computed by Lastovecki
[
210
]
.

The definition of a Whitney summand (i.e. the pieces we will ignore) is a little delicate in this setting, ultimately because
6.1
is not an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence. We would like to define Whitney summands by comparing with
\mathit{ko}
-theory, but to do that we would need a map of ring spectra
\mathit{ko}\to MT\mathrm{Spin}
, which Stolz
[
299
, §7]
showed does not exist. Fortunately, Stolz (
ibid.
, §1) introduced a weaker criterion that is good enough for us.

Recall that the isomorphism
\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{O}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}/2
counting the number of points mod
2
lifts to an
E_{\infty}
-ring map
p\colon M\mathrm{O}\to H\mathbb{Z}/2
. Mahowald
[
226
, 2.6]
constructed a section
s_{\mathrm{O}}\colon H\mathbb{Z}/2\to M\mathrm{O}
of
p
as homotopy-commutative ring spectra, which Hopkins observed refines to an
E_{2}
-ring map (see
[
151
, §1]
).
57
57

57

See
[
261
,
71
,
225
,
54
,
237
,
50
,
26
,
200
,
151
,
214
,
234
]
for additional proofs and generalizations of Mahowald’s result.

Definition 6.7
.

A section
s\colon\mathit{ko}\to MT\mathrm{Spin}
of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map (as a map of spectra) is a
weak algebra section
if

(1)

the induced map
s_{*}\colon H_{*}(\mathit{ko};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{*}(MT\mathrm{Spin};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is an algebra homomorphism; and

(2)

the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

(6.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{ko}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MT\mathrm{Spin}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H\mathbb{Z}/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\mathrm{O}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathrm{O}}}

Here
\phi
is induced by the forgetful map
B\mathrm{Spin}\to B\mathrm{O}
and
\tau_{0}
is the Postnikov
0
-truncation map followed by mod
2
reduction.

Proposition 6.9

(Stolz
[
299
, §6]
)
.

Weak algebra sections of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation exist, and all of them induce equal maps on mod
2
homology.

Without loss of generality, choose such a map
s
.

Definition 6.10

(Stolz
[
299
, §1]
)
.

A
homology
\mathit{ko}
-module
is a spectrum
Y
together with a map
\mu_{Y}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge Y\to Y
of spectra such that the induced map on homology

(6.11)

(\mu_{Y})_{*}\colon H_{*}(\mathit{ko};\mathbb{Z}/2)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)\longrightarrow H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)

defines the structure of an
H_{*}(\mathit{ko};\mathbb{Z}/2)
-module on
H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Remark 6.12
.

(1)

Any
\mathit{ko}
-module, including
\mathit{ko}
,
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
, and
H\mathbb{Z}/2
, is a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module in a canonical way.

(2)

Any
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module
Y
with action map
\mu^{s}_{Y}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge Y\to Y
has a canonical homology
\mathit{ko}
-module structure with
\mu_{Y}\coloneqq\mu_{Y}^{s}\circ(s\wedge\mathrm{id}_{Y})\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge Y\to Y
(
ibid.
, (1.2)). Moreover, if the
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module structure arises from a
\mathit{ko}
-module structure through the ABS map, this homology
\mathit{ko}
-module structure agrees with the one from (
1
).

(3)

The Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting in
6.1
is a splitting of homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules, as is implicit in (
ibid.
, §6).
∎

Definition 6.13
.

Let
M
be an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module. A
Whitney summand
of
M
is an
H\mathbb{Z}/2
-summand of
M
as homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules.

If
M
acquired its
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module structure from a
\mathit{ko}
-module structure of EA type via the ABS map,
5.9
and
6.13
coincide.

Remark 6.14
.

In (
6.4
), each
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X}
piece splits as a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
indexed by a basis of
H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Each of these
H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands is by definition a Whitney summand.
∎

Whitney summands of
M
are dual to Whitney summands of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}M
, as one can verify using the compatibility of Anderson duality with wedge sums.

Theorem 6.15
.

(1)

Let
(\mathrm{Dir}_{\ell,k})_{*}
be the group of IFTs whose partition functions vanish on all spin-
(\ell,k)
manifolds whose images under the projection
\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}a_{1}}}\colon\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\mathit{ko}_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})}
vanish.
The image of the free-to-interacting map
\Sigma^{\ell-k-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
on homotopy groups is a subgroup of
(\mathrm{Dir}_{\ell,k})_{*}
.

(2)

(\mathrm{Dir}_{\ell,k})_{*}\cong\pi_{-*}(\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}))
, and the image of the free-to-interacting map inside these groups is exactly as given in
5.58
.

This is a corollary of
3.99
, where we saw that
F2I_{\ell,k}
factors as the composition of the map
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}\colon\Sigma^{\ell-k-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
followed by the dual ABS map
\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to\Sigma^{2}(I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. By
6.1
and
6.5
, this is the inclusion of a direct summand on
\pi_{n}
for any
n
.

Theorem 6.16
.

The spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands.

To prove this, we need a little more information on
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
.

Lemma 6.17
.

(1)

There is a finite, connective spectrum
\mathbf{J}
and a
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\simeq\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{2}\mathbf{J}
.

(2)

Let
J\coloneqq\mathcal{A}/(\mathrm{Sq}^{3})
. There is an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module equivalence
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko})\cong\Sigma^{2}J
.

Part (
1
) was apparently a folklore theorem; one reference is Baker
[
37
, Corollary 4.2]
.
58
58

58

The spectrum
\mathbf{J}
was constructed by Hopkins
[
166
]
; what is folklore is the connection to
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
. In fact Hopkins shows there are exactly two such homotopy classes of spectra, distinguished by whether
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
acts nontrivially on their mod
2
cohomology, and they are Spanier–Whitehead dual to each other.
Part (
2
) then follows by combining
[
37
, Corollary 4.2]
and
[
38
, Example 5.4]
.

Proof of
6.16
.

In
4.58
, we defined the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
by defining a map
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
, smashing with
MT\mathrm{Spin}
, and applying Anderson duality. Therefore, as we discussed above, we can compute the effect of the Bott spiral map by checking what it does on each of the three kinds of summands in the ABP decomposition of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\text{--})
.

(1)

For the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{a_{i}}\mathit{ko}}
summands, we get the map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
, which we proved is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands in
5.61
.

(2)

Next the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}}
summands. By
6.17
,
\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathbf{J}\simeq\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
as
\mathit{ko}
-modules. Therefore on these summands the interacting Bott spiral map has the form

(6.18)

\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\phi}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}})\colon\mathrm{Map}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}},I_{\mathbb{Z}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1}))\to\mathrm{Map}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}},I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})).

That is, we can think of it as a natural transformation of cohomology theories, evaluated on the spectrum
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}}
. We can compute the effect of this map by considering the induced map from the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence computing the
I_{\mathbb{Z}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1})
-cohomology of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}}
to the
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
-cohomology of the same spectrum. On
E_{2}^{\bullet,q}
, this map is exactly the map induced by applying
\pi_{-q}
to
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{ko})
, then applying
H^{*}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}};\text{--})
. By
5.61
, the map of
E_{2}
-pages is an isomorphism modulo those
\mathbb{Z}/2
summands coming from Whitney summands. Since those Whitney summands are Eilenberg–Mac Lane summands of the spectra we applied the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence to, they split off of the spectral sequence. Specifically, they (1) do not have nontrivial differentials or extensions to or from other summands and (2) they converge to Whitney summands of
\mathrm{Map}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}},I_{\mathbb{Z}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1}))
(resp.
\mathrm{Map}({\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\mathbf{J}},I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}))
). Therefore we can focus on the unique, non-Whitney summand of both; by the above discussion, the map of Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequences on this summand is an isomorphism on
E_{2}
, hence is an isomorphism on homotopy groups.

(3)

The
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{c_{k}}H\mathbb{Z}/2}
summands are Whitney summands (see
6.14
), so we ignore them. ∎

To discuss the behavior of
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
, we need to understand the structure of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
a little better.

Definition 6.19

(
[
6
,
342
]
)
.

A bounded-below, finite-type
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
M
is
stably invertible
if there is some other
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N
such that
M\otimes N
is stably isomorphic (see
5.28
) to
\mathbb{Z}/2
.
M
is
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible
if
M
is
Q_{1}
-local (
5.83
) and there is another
Q_{1}
-local
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
N
such that
M\otimes N\simeq N_{0}
.

Thus if
M
and
N
are stably invertible, resp.
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible,
M\otimes N
is stably invertible, resp.
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible.

Lemma 6.20

(
[
62
, Theorem 5.2]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules, and assume that
M
is stably invertible and
N
is
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible. Then
M\otimes N
is
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible, and is stably isomorphic to
(M\otimes N_{0})\otimes N
.

Proposition 6.21

(Yu
[
342
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

N_{i}
is
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}/4
. Conversely, let
M
be a
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module. Then there are
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}/4
such that
M\simeq\Sigma^{\alpha}N_{i^{\prime}}
.

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
M
be a finite type
\mathit{ko}
-module such that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
is stably invertible or
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible and
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
. Then
M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type.

Proof.

By
5.51
and
6.21
,
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module; since
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
is either stably invertible or
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible. By
6.21
,

H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\cong\Sigma^{\alpha}N_{i^{\prime}}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}/4
. The result then follows from
5.43
.
∎

Lemma 6.23

(Adams–Priddy
[
6
, §3]
)
.

The
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
J\cong\Sigma^{-2}H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko})
defined in
6.17
is stably invertible.

Thus every non-Whitney summand in the ABP decomposition (
6.4
) of
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type, whence we may refer to long, short, and Whitney summands of each, and likewise for the Anderson dual.

Theorem 6.24
.

For
k,\ell>0
, the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k})\to I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
sends long summands to long summands; on each long summand it is an inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/2^{N}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2^{2N}
with cokernel
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Hence we can think of this map as “multiplication by
2
” on long summands.

Proof.

Just like for the proof of
6.16
, because
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
was defined in
4.41
by applying
I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Spin}
to a map
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell+1,k}
, we may smash that map with each non-Whitney summand in the ABP decomposition to give the proof. For the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{a_{i}}\mathit{ko}}
summands, we are done by
5.88
. For the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{b_{j}}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}}
summands, we need to check that
5.89
and
5.106
both still hold after smashing with
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
. For
5.106
, this is completely straightforward: the proof applies
mutatis mutandis
. For
5.89
, the proof can be adapted in essentially the same way, but one needs to know that the fiber of

(6.25)

\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\longrightarrow\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}\wedge\Sigma B\mathbb{Z}/2

has
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
stably equivalent to
\Sigma^{\alpha}F_{i^{\prime}}
for certain
\alpha
and
i^{\prime}
. This follows from
5.95
and
6.21
, and the fact that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko})
is stably invertible, as we observed after the proof of
6.22
. The remaining details are the same as for
\mathit{ko}
.
∎

Remark 6.26

(Put a pin in it)
.

This completes the story for class D; in this remark we explain how these theorems also apply to Bott spirals starting in classes BDI or DIII. In these three cases, the discrete and continuous fermionic groups coincide (see
2.24
and
1
), so we may continue with the discrete groups, corresponding to the tangential structures pin
-
and pin
+
, respectively. Moreover, Freed–Hopkins’ free-to-interacting maps
[
118
, §9.2.2]
for classes BDI and DIII coincide with our
(\ell,k)=(1,0)
resp.
(0,1)
free-to-interacting maps by construction (see
3.64
), so there is no difference between the free-to-interacting map,
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
, or
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
for
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
,
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}
, and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k-1}
.
∎

6.2.
Spiraling from
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
,
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h\pm}
, and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}\pm}

The ideas we used in §
6.1
to calculate the free-to-interacting and Bott spiral maps when the starting symmetry type is one of spin, pin
+
, or pin
-
apply with only slight modifications to spin
h
, pin
\tilde{c}\pm
, and pin
h\pm
, which correspond to the
continuous
Altland–Zirnbauer classes C, AI, AII, CI, and CII, respectively, from
2.24
and
1
.
59
59

59

In the
discrete
case, except for class C, these groups are
E_{\ell,k}
groups, and the free-to-interacting maps match, so we may proceed like in
6.26
. For discrete class C, corresponding to
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
-structure,
2.29
implies that if
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
,
\mathit{MT}(Spin\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell+3,k-1}
(see
4.45
), taking care of the computation of the bordism groups and the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}
and
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
. There is then the question of the free-to-interacting map, which we solve in
6.106
.
Thus, in this section, we are considering Altland–Zirnbauer types which are “mixed discrete-continuous:” we start with a continuous fermionic group, and then in the process of running the Bott spiral take the fermionic tensor product with the discrete symmetry groups
E_{\ell,k}
.

We study these mixed discrete-continuous spiral maps in order to better understand how to mathematically model the Bott spiral. We were surprised to discover that, even though the behavior of the free-to-interacting map and both spiral maps is qualitatively very similar to the purely discrete maps we studied in the previous subsection, assembling them into a mixed continuous-discrete Bott spiral does not work very well. We discuss this further in
4.73
.

In all cases, the key ingredient in our Bott spiral calculations is an Anderson–Brown–Peterson-style splitting generalizing
6.1
:
6.27
for
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
,
6.31
for
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}\pm}
, and
6.34
for
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h\pm}
. The spin
h
splitting is a recent result of Buchanan–McKean
[
63
]
and Mills
[
239
]
; the others are new to our knowledge. All of these splittings are strictly finer than what one obtains by combining (
6.4
), Freed–Hopkins’ shearing equivalences
[
118
, (10.20)]
, and the stable splittings of
(B\mathrm{O}_{2})^{\pm(V-2)}
and
(B\mathrm{O}_{3})^{\pm(V-3)}
constructed by Mitchell–Priddy
[
242
,
241
]
and Kashiwabara–Zare
[
191
, Theorem 1]
.

Once we have these ABP-style splittings, the main theorems in this section are
6.49
on the image of the F2I map,
6.50
computing the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{MTH})
modulo Whitney summands (where
H
is one of
\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
,
\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}\pm}
, or
\mathrm{Pin}^{h\pm}
), and
6.60
computing the spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{MTH})
on long summands. We also finish the computation of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}
, for
\ell\geq 3
, in
6.53
.
The proofs of these theorems are similar to those of
6.15
and
6.16
. Together with the work of the previous sections, these theorems account for all eight of the continuous fermionic groups over the real numbers.

Theorem 6.27

(Buchanan–McKean
[
63
, Theorem 7.2]
, Mills
[
239
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
denote the fiber of the algebra map
\mathit{ko}\to H\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\mathit{ksp}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-4}(\tau_{\geq 4}\mathit{ko})
, so that
\mathit{ksp}
is the connective cover of the quaternionic
K
-theory spectrum
\mathit{KSp}
. Then there are numbers
d_{i},e_{j},f_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and a
2
-local equivalence of homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules

(6.28)

MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{d_{i}}\mathit{ksp}}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{e_{j}}\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}}\vee\bigvee_{k\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{f_{k}}H\mathbb{Z}/2},

where
d_{1}=1
.
Moreover, the composition

(6.29)

MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}d_{1}}}}\mathit{ksp}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\geq 0}}\Sigma^{4}\mathit{KO}

is equivalent as
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module maps to Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABS map
[
118
, §9.2.2]
.

The homology
\mathit{ko}
-module structures on the spectra appearing in
6.27
are as defined in
6.12
. The fact that (
6.28
) is a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module splitting is not discussed in
loc. cit.
, but follows from Mills’ proof
[
239
, §3]
, which uses a theorem of Stolz
[
299
]
which produces splittings of homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules. The comparison between
\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}d_{1}}}
and the twisted Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map is implicit in
[
63
, §5.5]
via the definition of their
\mathit{KSp}
-Pontrjagin classes.

Lemma 6.30
.

(1)

There is a finite, connective spectrum
Y_{1}
and a
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence
\mathit{ksp}\simeq\mathit{ko}\wedge Y_{1}
.

(2)

Let
\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(1)/(\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{3})
. Then

Q

is stably invertible and there is an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module isomorphism
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})\cong\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}
.

(3)

There is a finite, connective spectrum
Y_{2}
and an equivalence of
\mathit{ko}
-modules
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}\simeq\mathit{ko}\wedge Y_{2}
.

(4)

Let
R_{2}
be the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module defined in
5.93
. Then
R_{2}
is stably invertible and there is a
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module isomorphism
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathit{ko}})\simeq R_{2}
.

Factoring a
\mathit{ko}
-module
M
as
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
for a spectrum
X
is far from automatic: for example, it is impossible for
M=H\mathbb{Z}

[
38
, §5]
.

Proof.

Item (
1
) is proven in
[
224
, Theorem 1.5]
, where it is attributed to Anderson
[
21
]
, and Stolz
[
299
, §8]
proves (
3
). Except for stable invertibility, which was proven for these
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules by Adams–Priddy
[
6
, §3]
, items (
2
) and (
4
) are implicit in
[
38
, §5]
.
∎

The remaining four ABP-style splittings we need in this subsection have not been previously shown to our knowledge.

Theorem 6.31

(
[
83
]
)
.

There are numbers
g_{i}^{\pm},h_{j}^{\pm},m_{k}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{N}
and
2
-local equivalences of homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules

(6.32)

MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}\pm}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{g_{i}^{\pm}}\mathit{ko}}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\Sigma^{h_{j}^{\pm}}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}}\vee\bigvee_{k\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{m_{k}^{\pm}}H\mathbb{Z}/2},

in which
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}g_{1}^{+}}=2
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}h_{1}^{-}}=-2
. The splittings (
6.32
) may be chosen so that the compositions

\displaystyle MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}g_{1}^{+}}}}\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\geq 0}}\Sigma^{2}\mathit{KO}

.

These splittings are finer than the ones induced by smashing

with the splittings of

[

and Kashiwabara–Zare

.

We will also identify how Freed–Hopkins’ Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro maps

Theorem 6.37

(

.

(1)

.

Remark 6.39

(No obvious shortcut in classes CI and CII)
.

By
[
84
, (3.11)]
,
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{0,1}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1,0}
. This suggests that we could understand the Bott spirals starting with pin
h\pm
in terms of the spiral starting with spin
h
, parallel to
6.26
, but it is not obvious that the free-to-interacting maps match (see
[
118
, Figure 5, cases
s=\pm 3
and
s=4
]
), and indeed we had to give a more complicated description in
6.37
.
∎

Definition 6.40
.

For
s=\pm 2
,
\pm 3
, and
4
, let
K_{f}(s)
be the continuous fermionic group that we associated to the parameter
s
in
Table

1
and
\mathit{ABS}_{s}\colon\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\to\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}
be Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABS map
[
118
, (9.43)]
. Let
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}
be the twisted ABS map defined in
3.62
.
Then define
\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k,s}
to be the composition

(6.41)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k,s}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))}\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\Sigma^{-s}\mathit{KO}}\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{k-\ell}\mathit{KO}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}k-\ell}-{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}s}}{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\mathit{KO}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathit{ABS}_{s}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathit{ABS}_{\ell,k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\mu}}

where
\mu
denotes multiplication. Taking the Anderson dual of (
6.41
), we obtain a free-to-interacting map

(6.42)

\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,s}\colon\Sigma^{\ell-k+s-2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}).

Recall the modules
N_{i}
,
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, from §
5.2
. For
i\geq 4
, define
N_{i}\coloneqq\Sigma^{4}N_{i-4}
.

Lemma 6.43
.

(1)

Let
\ell,k\geq 0
such that
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
.
Let
i\coloneqq\ell\bmod 4
, thought of in the set
\{0,1,2,3\}
and
j\coloneqq\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
. Then there are stable equivalences of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules

(6.44a)

\displaystyle H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})

\displaystyle\cong\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\otimes H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\simeq\Sigma^{4j+k-1}N_{i+3}

(6.44b)

\displaystyle H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})

\displaystyle\cong J\otimes H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\simeq\Sigma^{4j+k}N_{i+2}.

(2)

If
M
is one of
\mathit{ko}
,
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
,
\mathit{ksp}
,
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
,
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(1-\sigma)}
,
M_{2}
(see
5.34
), or
M_{3}
(see
5.37
), or a suspension of one of these, then
M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA type.

Proof.

The first isomorphisms in (
6.44
) are from the Künneth formula; for the stable equivalences, since

Q

and
J
are stably invertible (see
6.30
and
6.23
),
5.45
immediately reduces these equivalences to the assertions

(6.45)

\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\otimes N_{0}\cong\Sigma^{-1}N_{3}\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad J\otimes N_{0}\cong N_{2}.

Since

Q

and
J
are stably invertible, then by
6.20
,
\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\otimes N_{0}
and
J\otimes N_{0}
are
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible. By
6.21
, this implies these tensor products are stably equivalent to a shift of some
N_{i^{\prime}}
, so we can prove (
6.45
) by just calculating these tensor products in low degrees until the shift and
i^{\prime}
are uniquely determined.

On to part (
2
). Since
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko})\cong\Sigma^{2}J
and
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})\cong\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}
, part (
1
) implies part (
2
) for these two choices of
M
. We already showed the case of
\mathit{ko}
just before
6.24
. By
6.30
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathit{ko}})
is stably invertible, so we can use
6.22
. Finally, as we saw in
5.31
,
5.34
,
5.37
, and
5.40
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
of
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)}
,
M_{2}
, and
M_{3}
are isomorphic to the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
N_{i}
for different
i
; by
6.21
, these
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules are
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible, so
6.22
implies that, as we wanted, smashing
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)}
,
M_{2}
, and
M_{3}
with
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
yields a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type.
∎

As a consequence of
6.43
and our generalized ABP splittings in
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
, we have long, short, and Whitney summands in the homotopy groups of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. This will be helpful in our descriptions of
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,s}
and
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
in
6.49
and
6.60
respectively.

Definition 6.46
.

Given
\ell,k,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
j\coloneqq\lfloor\ell/4\rfloor
and
\widetilde{n}\coloneqq n-4j-k
. For
s\in\{-3,-2,0,2,3\}
, define
\widetilde{m}(s)\coloneqq\lfloor(\widetilde{n}+s)/8\rfloor
. Also, let
\widetilde{m}(4)\coloneqq\widetilde{m}(0)
.

The implicit dependence of
\widetilde{m}(s)
on
\widetilde{n}
is an abuse of notation, but in the places
\widetilde{m}(s)
will appear,
\widetilde{n}
will be clear from context. Recall the notation
\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
from
0.18
.

Proposition 6.47

(Locations of long summands)
.

Let
\ell
,
k
,
i
,
j
,
n
, and
\widetilde{n}
be as in
5.55
, and
\widetilde{m}(s)
be as in
6.46
.

(2)

\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
have the following long summands.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(2)+2-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 1\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+2,k)
.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(2)+3-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 5\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+2,k)
.

(-2)

\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
have the following long summands.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(-2)+4-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 5\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+2
.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(-2)+5-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 1\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+2
.

(3)

M_{3}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h+}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
have the following long summands.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(3)+1-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 0\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+3,k)
.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(3)+2-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 4\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+3,k)
.

(-3)

\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{h-}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
have the following long summands.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(-3)+4-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 6\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+3
.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(-3)+5-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 2\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)+3
.

(4)

\mathit{ksp}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
and
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
have the following long summands.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(4)+5-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 7\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+3,k)-1
.

•

A
\mathbb{Z}/2^{4\widetilde{m}(4)+2-i}
in degree
n
if
\widetilde{n}+i\equiv 3\bmod 8
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell+3,k)-1
.

Proof.

By
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
, in each of these five cases, the first spectrum
\mathcal{M}_{1}
that appears is a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module summand of the second (e.g.
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
is a summand of
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{\tilde{c}-}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathit{ksp}
is a summand of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
, etc.), so it suffices to prove the proposition for the first spectrum in each of the five cases. By
6.43
, part (
2
), in each of these cases,
\mathcal{M}_{1}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type, so we can prove the proposition in the same manner as
5.55
as soon as we know
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{1}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. For
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
and
\mathit{ksp}
, we did this in
6.43
, part (
1
); for
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}
, use that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\Sigma^{t}\mathit{ko})\cong\Sigma^{t}\mathbb{Z}/2
by definition; and for
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
and
M_{3}
, use
5.37
,
5.40
, and
5.45
.
∎

As a corollary, we get another characterization of the long summands that we are interested in.

Proposition 6.48
.

In
\pi_{n}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
, hence also in
\mho^{n}_{\ell,k}
, exactly one of the following is true.

(1)

n\equiv\ell+k-2i-1\bmod 4
and
n\geq\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
. In this case,
\pi_{n}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is the direct sum of the long summand
L
from the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{0}\mathit{ko}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
piece of the ABP decomposition (
6.4
) and cyclic groups of orders at most that of
L
, and strictly smaller order than
L
if
\lvert L\rvert>2
.

(2)

n\not\equiv\ell+k-2i-1\bmod 4
or
n<\mathrm{ldeg}(\ell,k)
, and
\pi_{n}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
is simple
2
-torsion.

Proof.

6.1
gives a direct-sum decomposition of the spectrum
MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
, and
5.55
and
6.47
compute the homotopy groups of the summands modulo simple
2
-torsion, implying the result.
∎

Using the twisted ABP splittings in
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
, the reader can provide analogs of
6.48
in which
MT\mathrm{Spin}
is replaced with
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
for the other continuous Altland–Zirnbauer groups
K_{f}(s)
.

Theorem 6.49
.

Let
s=\pm 2
,
\pm 3
, or
4
, and
\ell,k\geq 0
but not both equal to
0
. The image of the free-to-interacting map
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,s}
of
6.40
consists of the short and long summands of the summand of
\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
dual to the unique lowest-degree summands in
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
, i.e.
\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
,
\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}
,
M_{3}
,
\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{1-\sigma}
, and
\mathit{ksp}

for
s=2
,
-2
,
3
,
-3
, and
4
respectively. Specifically, in spatial dimension
d\equiv k-\ell-s+2\bmod 4
,
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,s}
maps
\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathit{KO}^{d+\ell-k+s-2}
surjectively onto this long summand in
I_{\mathbb{Z}}^{d+2}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})
. The isomorphism type of this summand is the same as the corresponding long summand in
6.47
, where
n=d+1
.

The lower bounds on
\widetilde{n}
in
6.47
appear implicitly in
6.49
as follows: if
d
,
\ell
, and
k
are such that
\widetilde{n}
does not reach this lower bound, the image of the free-to-interacting map in that degree is
0
.

Proof.

For
s=2
,
-2
, and
4
,
6.27
and
6.31
identify Freed–Hopkins’ twisted ABP maps with projection onto the summand
\Sigma^{2}\mathit{ko}
,
\Sigma^{-2}\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
, resp.
\mathit{ksp}
, followed by a connective cover map to the requisite shift of
\mathit{KO}
, and so an analogous dual statement is true for these free-to-interacting maps. Then we smash with the free-to-interacting map for
\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
. Therefore in the degrees that these free-to-interacting maps
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,s}
are nontrivial, they are equivalent to shifts of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
of the connective cover
\mathit{ko}\to\mathit{KO}
(the Anderson dual of the connective cover is an isomorphism in nonnegative cohomological degrees) followed by shifts of the map
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}
from
3.99
, and we computed the latter in
5.58
.

The case
s=-3
is scarcely more difficult: by
6.34
and
6.37
, we can similarly identify
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k}
as
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\to\mathit{KO})
followed by the smash product of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{abs}_{-1}
and
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k}
. Ultimately by
3.64
, this smash product is equivalent to
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k+1}
, so we have once again reduced to
5.58
.

Finally
s=3
. By
6.37
, this free-to-interacting map factors as
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{abs}_{1}
followed by the Anderson duals of
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
, then the inclusion of the direct summand claimed in the current theorem statement. If not for
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
, the proof would be identical to that of
s=-3
, except that we would get
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell+1,k}
instead of
\mathit{f2i}_{\ell,k+1}
. But by
6.37
, since the maps
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
are injective on long summands, their Anderson duals are surjective on long summands, so their presence in the decomposition (
6.38a
) of
\mathit{F2I}_{\ell,k,3}
does not change the proof or the final outcome.
∎

Theorem 6.50
.

With
s
and
K_{f}(s)
as in
6.40
, the
spiral map

(6.51)

\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s)))\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1})

is an isomorphism on homotopy, modulo Whitney summands.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of
6.16
: consider the map of Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequences induced by
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
on each summand of the Anderson–Brown–Peterson decomposition of
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
proven in
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
(for
s=4
,
\pm 2
,
\pm 3
, respectively), observe that the
E_{2}
-page is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands, and therefore the same is true for the
E_{\infty}
-page.

To make this argument, though, we need to show that each summand appearing in these ABP decompositions factors as
\mathit{ko}\wedge X
, so that we can use the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence.

(1)

For
\mathit{ko}
this is tautological.
H\mathbb{Z}/2
is always a Whitney summand, so we ignore it.

(2)

For
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
, we did this in
6.17
.

(3)

\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)}
is already in this form.

(4)

We factored
\mathit{ksp}
and
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
in this way in
6.30
.

(5)

We do not realize
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
in this way, but
5.34
proves
\mathit{ko}\wedge(BA_{4})\simeq\Sigma^{2}M_{2}\vee B
, where
B
consists of Whitney summands. Thus we apply the first paragraph to show that
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}
applied to
\mathit{ko}\wedge BA_{4}
is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands, so the same is true for
M_{2}
.
5.37
shows the same for
M_{3}
, with
BA_{4}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2
in place of
BA_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 6.52
.

(1)

Let
M
be a
\mathit{ko}
-module and
\ell,k
be such that
M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type. Then
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k+1})
is an isomorphism on homotopy, modulo Whitney summands.

(2)

The above holds when
M\in\{\mathit{ko},\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko},\mathit{ksp},\widetilde{\mathit{ko}},\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)},M_{2},M_{3}\}
and
\ell,k>0
.

Proof.

The proof of part (
1
) is completely analogous to that of
6.24
. Thus part (
2
) follows as soon as we know that, for these
M
,
M\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type, which we proved in
6.43
.
∎

Theorem 6.53
.

For all
\ell\geq 3
,
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,0}^{\psi}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,0})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,1})
is an isomorphism on homotopy, modulo Whitney summands.

Proof.

By
6.24
, the corollary is true if
k>0
, so for the rest of the proof we assume
k=0
, meaning
\ell\geq 3
and we are in the situation of
4.48
. Looking at the definition of
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,0}
there, as the identity map on
\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})
smashed with
\psi_{\ell-3,1}
, up to
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence, we see that it suffices to show that
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell-3,1}^{\psi}(\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8}))
(
4.58
) is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands. This uses the more generic definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}
(
4.41
).

In
6.102
, we will show that, as a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module,
\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})
splits as a sum of shifts of
\mathit{ksp}
,
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
, and
H\mathbb{Z}/2
. (That proof will not use any facts about the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,k}
.) The
H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands are Whitney summands, so we ignore them. Thus to prove the corollary, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell-3,1}^{\psi}(M)
is a
\pi_{*}
-isomorphism modulo Whitney summands for
M\in\{\mathit{ksp},\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}\}
, which we did in
6.52
, part (
2
).
∎

Definition 6.54
.

For conciseness, let

(6.55)

\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,k}\coloneqq\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}.

We give
\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,k}
the homology
\mathit{ko}
-module structure induced from its
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module structure (see
6.12
).

Lemma 6.56
.

For
s\in\{\pm 2,\pm 3,4\}
and
(\ell,k)\neq(0,0)
,
\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,k}
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type.

This proof uses a few facts about
\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})
that we prove later, in
Section

6.4
. Those facts do not depend on anything we do in this or the next subsection.

Proof.

In
6.91
, we construct a real vector bundle
V\to B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2)
and show there is an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence
\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}
. Therefore there are
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalences

(6.57)

\displaystyle\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge_{MT\mathrm{Spin}}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}.

Let
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\{\mathit{ko},\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko},\mathit{ksp},\widetilde{\mathit{ko}},\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1}),N_{2},N_{3}\}
.
By
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
,
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
is, as a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module, equivalent to a sum of shifts of
\mathit{ko}
-modules equivalent to some
M\in\mathcal{S}
or to
H\mathbb{Z}/2
. For any spectrum
X
,
H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge X
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
. Thus for any
\Sigma^{t}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summand of
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
, the summands coming from
\Sigma^{t}H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}
in (
6.57
)
are equivalent to shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
as promised in the lemma statement. Therefore it suffices to consider summands that are shifts of the
\mathit{ko}
-modules in
\mathcal{S}
—and since the lemma statement is insensitive to such shifts, we may assume the summands are in
\mathcal{S}
without a shift.

For any
M\in\mathcal{S}
, the homology
\mathit{ko}
-module structure on
M
is induced from an actual
\mathit{ko}
-module structure (see
6.12
). Thus there are equivalences of homology
\mathit{ko}
-modules

(6.58)

\displaystyle M\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}

\displaystyle\simeq M\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}

\displaystyle\simeq M\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\Bigg(\mathit{ksp}\vee\bigvee_{i\geq 0}\Sigma^{n_{\ell}}H\mathbb{Z}/2\Bigg)

for some integers
n_{\ell}\geq 0
, with the latter equivalence by
6.95
. As in the previous paragraph, we may without loss of generality lose the
\Sigma^{n_{\ell}}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands. Therefore it suffices to show that
M\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is a sum of shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
and a
\mathit{ko}
-module of EA-type.

By the Künneth theorem for
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
, there is an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module isomorphism

(6.59)

H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp})\cong H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\otimes H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp}).

We claim that for all
M\in\mathcal{S}
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
is either stably invertible or
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible, and likewise that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})
is stably invertible. This amounts to checking, case by case, that we have already seen this: for
\mathit{ko}
, stable invertibility of
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ko})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
is true by definition. For
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}
, see
6.23
. For
\mathit{ksp}
and
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
, see
6.30
. For
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\pm(\sigma-1)}
,
N_{2}
, and
N_{3}
, see
6.21
.
Thus by
6.19
and
6.20
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)\otimes H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})
is also either stably invertible or
Q_{1}
-locally stably invertible, so by
6.22
,
M\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}
is of EA-type, finishing the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.60
.

With
s
and
K_{f}(s)
as in
6.40
and
\ell\geq 1
,
the spiral map

(6.61)

\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s)))\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\mathit{ME}_{\ell,k}\to I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))\wedge\Sigma\mathit{ME}_{\ell-1,k}

sends long summands to long summands; on each long summand it is an inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/2^{N}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2^{N+1}
with cokernel
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Proof.

Thanks to the generalized ABP splittings in
6.27
,
6.31
, and
6.34
,
\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))
is homology
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalent to a sum of Whitney summands and shifts of
\mathit{ko}
-modules
M
, where
M
is as in
6.52
. Therefore if
k>0
,
6.52
, part (
2
) finishes the proof.

This leaves the case
k=0
,
\ell\geq 3
. In this case we defined
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}
using
\overline{\psi}_{\ell,k}
in
4.48
, and the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}(\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s)))
in
4.58
is the Anderson dual of the map

(6.62)

\mathrm{id}_{\mathit{MTH}(K_{f}(s))}\wedge\overline{\psi}_{\ell,0}\colon\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,0}\longrightarrow\Sigma\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell+1,1}

(where
\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,k}
is as defined in
6.54
).
By
6.56
,
\mathit{MQ}_{s,\ell,0}
is a sum of
\mathit{ko}
-modules of EA-type and shifts of
H\mathbb{Z}/2
, so we can invoke part (
1
) of
6.52
and finish.
∎

6.3.
The complex cases: spiraling from

MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
and
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{c}

The story over
\mathbb{C}
, i.e. using
\mathit{KU}
and
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
, is similar but easier. In this section we compute the F2I and interacting Bott spiral maps in the complex case, again modulo Whitney summands. Our main results are
6.82
,
6.83
, and
6.84
, in which we compute the free-to-interacting map,
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\phi}
, and
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
respectively in Altland–Zirnbauer class A. Analogously to
6.26
, the identification of
MT\mathrm{Pin}^{c}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1}
in a manner compatible with the free-to-interacting map allows us to extend these results to class AIII.

The most striking change in our computation is that our two indices
\ell
and
k
behave identically, as discussed in
1.121
.
Recall the definition of
\mathit{ME}_{m}
from
1.122
.

6.3.1.
Working over
\mathit{ku}

We begin with
\mathit{ku}
-module computations.

There is an isomorphism

(6.63)

\mathit{ku}_{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{\mathbb{C}}],\qquad\qquad\lvert\beta_{\mathbb{C}}\rvert=2,

where
\beta_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex Bott class. As for
\beta
in
\mathit{KO}
-theory,
\beta_{\mathbb{C}}
implements Bott periodicity: there is a natural isomorphism
\mathit{KU}_{*}(X)\cong\mathit{ku}_{*}(X)[\beta_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}]
, and for any
\mathit{ku}
-module
M
,
\mathit{KU}\wedge_{\mathit{ku}}M\simeq M[\beta_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}]
.

There is a
\mathit{ku}
-module structure on
H\mathbb{Z}/2
defined analogously to the
\mathit{ko}
-module structure.

Lemma 6.64

(Wilson
[
330
, Proposition 1.2]
)
.

The reduced
\mathit{KU}
-cohomology of
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is

(6.65)

\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{i}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})=\begin{cases}~~0,&i\text{ even}\\
~~\mu_{2^{\infty}},&i\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

Definition 6.66
.

We define
\mathit{ku}
-modules of EA-type exactly as in
5.4
, but with
\mathit{ku}
and
\mathit{KU}
in place of
\mathit{ko}
, resp.
\mathit{KO}
.
We define Whitney summands and long summands in the same way as
5.9
, mutatis mutandis.

Because
\widetilde{\mathit{KU}}{}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
is simpler than
\widetilde{\mathit{KO}}{}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
, the analog of
5.9
is simpler. There is no analog of short summands in the complex setting.

Define
H_{\mathit{ku}}^{*}
analogously to
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
from
5.13
: for a
\mathit{ku}
-module
M
,
H_{\mathit{ku}}^{*}(M)\coloneqq\pi_{-*}(\mathrm{Map}(M,H\mathbb{Z}/2))
. When
M=H\mathbb{Z}/2
, this is a graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebra with respect to composition.

Recall the stable cohomology operations
Q_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
and
Q_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}+\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
from
5.83
, and let
\mathcal{E}(1)\coloneqq\langle Q_{0},Q_{1}\rangle\subset\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 6.67

(Baker
[
38
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebras
H_{\mathit{ku}}^{*}(H\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathcal{E}(1)
.

Thus for any
\mathit{ku}
-module
M
,
H_{\mathit{ku}}^{*}(M)
is naturally an
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module. The analog of
5.16
holds for
\mathit{ku}
-modules of the form
\mathit{ku}\wedge X
.

We also have the analogue of
5.17
for
\mathit{ku}
: a Baker–Lazarev Adams spectral sequence for a
\mathit{ku}
-module
M
with analogous convergence hypotheses:

(6.68a)

E_{2}^{s,t}=\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,t}(H_{\mathit{ku}}^{*}(M),\mathbb{Z}/2)\Longrightarrow\pi_{*}(M)_{2}^{\wedge}.

And, just as in (
5.22
), if
M=\mathit{ku}\wedge X
, this spectral sequence simplifies to one more commonly constructed using the change-of-rings theorem:

(6.68b)

E_{2}^{s,t}=\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,t}(H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2),\mathbb{Z}/2)\Longrightarrow\mathit{ku}_{*}(X)_{2}^{\wedge}.

The algebra
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the Adams
E_{2}
-page for
\mathit{ku}
, and it acts on the Adams
E_{2}
-page for any
\mathit{ku}
-module. Differentials commute with this action.

Proposition 6.69

(Liulevicius
[
221
, §2]
)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebras

(6.70)

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(\mathbb{Z}/2)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}/2[h_{0},v_{1}],

with
h_{0}\in\Ext^{1,1}
and
v_{1}\in\Ext^{1,3}
.

See also
[
49
, Example 4.5.6]
.

The class
h_{0}
in (
6.70
) lifts to multiplication by
2
, and
v_{1}
lifts to multiplication by
\beta_{\mathbb{C}}
. The analog of Margolis’ theorem (
5.25
) in this setting is true; see Bruner–Greenlees
[
58
, §2.1]
.

Example 6.71
.

The prototypical
\mathit{ku}
-module of EA-type is
\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
. Just like in
5.31
, we will work with
\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1}\simeq\Sigma^{-1}(\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
. To run the Adams spectral sequence, we need
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})
, which was computed by
[
89
, Proposition 4.48]
60
60

60

This source computes
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(\Sigma N_{0})
, but
\Sigma N_{0}\cong N_{1}
as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules, which is yet another avatar of the fact that
\mathrm{Pin}^{+}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{U}_{1}
define the same symmetry type:
\mathrm{Pin}^{c}
.
,
61
61

61

See also Dhankhar–Field–Nigam–Quigley–Yang
[
91
, Figure 2]
for an independent calculation of the
\mathbb{Z}/2[h_{0}]
-module structure on
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})
.
to be

(6.72)

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[h_{0},v_{1}]\{e_{0},e_{1},\dotsc\}/(h_{0}e_{0},\ v_{1}e_{i}-h_{0}e_{i+1})

with
e_{i}\in\Ext^{0,2i}
. We draw this in
Figure

18
, right. All differentials vanish for degree reasons, and all extension questions are solved by the
h_{0}
- and
v_{1}
-actions, so we learn that

(6.73)

\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}\{\overline{e}_{i}\colon i\geq 0\}/(2\overline{e}_{0},\ \beta_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{e}_{i}-2\overline{e}_{i+1})

with
\lvert\overline{e}_{i}\rvert=2i
; see
Figure

18
. Therefore
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{2j+1}(\mathit{ME}_{1})=0
and
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{jk}(\mathit{ME}_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{j+1}
generated by
\overline{e}_{j}
; the latter is a long summand. Inverting
\beta_{\mathbb{C}}
, one concludes
\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{1}
is EA-type.

Just as for
\mathit{ko}
, there are no Whitney summands, and we can obtain the same results for
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
by shifting degrees by
1
. The groups
\mathit{ku}_{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{\infty})
were first computed by Hashimoto
[
154
, Theorem 3.1]
.
∎

.
Left: the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure on
N_{1}
; straight lines are

, the

. Gray diagonal lines denote

. This figure adapted from
