, Figure 6, right]
.

Proposition 6.74
.

For
m\geq 1
,
\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m}
is of EA-type, and there is a stable isomorphism of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{m};\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\Sigma^{m-1}N_{1}
.

Proof sketch.

The proof is identical to that of
5.51
, except using the stable equivalence
N_{1}\otimes N_{1}\simeq\Sigma N_{1}
of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules due to Ossa
[
253
, §3]
. We also need the
\mathit{ku}
-module analog of
5.43
, that if
H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\Sigma^{n}N_{1}
as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules then
\mathit{ku}\wedge X
is of EA-type, but this can be proven in a similar way using the analysis of
6.71
, which used nothing about
\mathit{ME}_{1}
save for the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure on its cohomology.
∎

Remark 6.75
.

Comparing
N_{1}^{\otimes n}
as an
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module versus as an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module, one discovers that the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
N_{0}
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
, and
N_{3}
are all stably equivalent as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules (in the sense of
5.28
) up to a shift.
This can be verified directly (c.f.
[
342
, Chapter 2]
or
[
89
, §4.4.4, §5.2.4]
).
∎

Proposition 6.76
.

There is an isomorphism

(6.77)

\mathit{ku}_{n}(\mathit{ME}_{m})/(\text{\rm Whitney summands})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2^{j+1},&n=m+2j-1,\ j\geq 0\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Just as in the proof of
5.55
, use Margolis’ theorem to reduce to studying the Ext of the unique non-free
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module summand of
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{m};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By
6.74
, this amounts to just studying
N_{1}
up to a shift, which we did in
6.71
.
∎

6.3.2.
Lifting to
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}

Before we describe the image of the free-to-interacting map, we recall Anderson–Brown–Peterson’s splitting of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
. It is a little simpler than the
MT\mathrm{Spin}
version from
6.1
.

Theorem 6.78

(Anderson–Brown–Peterson
[
18
]
, Stong
[
302
, Chapter XI]
)
.

There are numbers
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
and a
2
-local homotopy equivalence

(6.79)

MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\Sigma^{s_{i}}\mathit{ku}}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\Sigma^{t_{j}}H\mathbb{Z}/2}.

The degrees of the first few shifts are

(6.80)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}(s_{i})}

\displaystyle=(0,4,8,8,12,12,12,16,16,16,16,16,\dotsc)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}(t_{j})}

\displaystyle=(10,14,18,18,18,20,22,22,22,22,22,\dotsc)

The projection
\mathrm{proj}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}s_{1}}}\colon MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\mathit{ku}}
onto the first summand is equivalent to the connective cover of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map.

Anderson–Brown–Peterson write that their proof of the splitting of
MT\mathrm{Spin}
(
6.1
) can be generalized to the complex case; Stong is the first to write it down. See Abdallah–Salch
[
2
]
for discussion of the degrees
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}(t_{j})}
in
6.78
.

Remark 6.81
.

The real Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map is
7
-connected, which means that in physics applications to IFTs of (spatial) dimension
6
and below, one can essentially ignore the difference between
MT\mathrm{Spin}
and
\mathit{ko}
. However, because
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}s_{2}}=4
in the complex ABP splitting, the complex Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map is only
3
-connected, and therefore using
\mathit{ku}
instead of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
misses IFTs beginning in spatial dimension
2
. This includes, for example, the example studied
by Wang–Senthil in
[
328
, §III.E]
.
∎

We define a Whitney summand of an
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
-module to be a Whitney summand of the underlying
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module.
62
62

62

One could try to generalize Stolz’ analysis in
[
299
]
to maps
\mathit{ku}\to MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
splitting the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map, and therefore obtain an principle narrower definition of a Whitney summand and therefore a sharper result on the Bott spiral. We felt this would digress too far from the present discussion.

Theorem 6.82
.

On homotopy groups, the image of the free-to-interacting map
\Sigma^{m-2}\mathit{KU}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m})
is contained in the subgroup
(\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}({\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}\mathit{ku}}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m}))^{*}\subset(\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m}))^{*}
corresponding to the first factor in the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro decomposition. Restricted to that subgroup of the codomain, the free-to-interacting map is
a surjection
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/2^{j+1}

onto the unique long summand in degree
m+2j-4
for each
j\geq 0
,
and the zero map in degrees
n\equiv m+1\bmod 2
;
the Whitney summands are not in the image.

Proof sketch.

The proof is quite similar to the proofs of
5.58
and
6.15
. The only significant difference is a simplification: when studying the image of the F2I map
\Sigma^{m-2}\mathit{KU}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ku}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m})
, instead of a two-step induction on
k
, then
\ell
, we only need to induct on
m
. Therefore this proof includes analogs of
5.60
and
5.66
, but we do not need to adapt
5.68
or the arguments supporting it.
∎

Recall that
m=\ell+k
.

Theorem 6.83
.

The
spiral map
\mathrm{sp}^{\phi}_{\ell,k}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m+1})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m})
is an isomorphism modulo Whitney summands.

The argument proceeds completely analogously to those for
5.61
and
6.16
.

Theorem 6.84
.

The spiral map
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m+1})\to\Sigma I_{\mathbb{Z}}(MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m})
sends long summands to long summands; on each long summand it is an inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/2^{N}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2^{N+1}
, with cokernel
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Since we can treat the indices
\ell
and
k
identically when working over
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
, the extra complication in the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}
in the real case (see
4.48
) does not appear here.

Proof.

As usual, first work over
\mathit{ku}
, then use
6.78
to lift to
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
. Since all non-Whitney summands in the ABP decomposition of
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
are shifts of
\mathit{ku}
, the second step is immediate, and we focus on the first step.

The calculation over
\mathit{ku}
is also analogous to the proof of
5.88
. The part with the diagonal is exactly the same; the part with
e_{\sigma}
is similar, except that we want to work over
\mathcal{E}(1)
, rather than
\mathcal{A}(1)
. For
i=0,1,2,3
, there is a stable isomorphism of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules
F_{i}\simeq\Sigma^{i}F_{0}
, so we focus on
F_{0}
.
[
89
, (4.49)]
shows
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(F_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[v_{1}]
with
v_{1}\in\Ext^{1,3}
as a module over
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
63
63

63

As just a bigraded abelian group, this Ext computation was done earlier by Davis–Mahowald
[
78
, §2]
.
Thus, analogously to
5.101
,

(6.85)

\mathit{ku}_{n}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\wedge\mathit{ME}_{m})/(\text{Whitney summands})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2,&n=2j+m,\ j\geq 0\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and the Bott class acts injectively on the non-Whitney summands. Thus one can imitate the rest of the proof of
5.89
and finish.
∎

We will occasionally need to know the groups
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{2})
—corresponding to class
\mathrm{A}^{\prime}
in the language of §
4.1
—in low degrees, including Whitney summands. This is the spin
c
analog of the dpin computations in
[
182
, Appendices E and F]
.

Proposition 6.86
.

There are isomorphisms

(6.87)

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{0}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{4}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4}

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{1}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{5}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{2}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{6}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 6}.

\displaystyle\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}^{3}(\mathit{ME}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4

In the groups given above, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand is a Whitney summand if and only if it has even degree.

Proof.

As usual, the universal property (
2.18
) of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}
reduces the question to the computation of
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(\mathit{ME}_{2})
in degrees
6
and below, together with the assertion that
\Omega_{7}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(\mathit{ME}_{2})
is torsion. The proof of the latter fact is closely analogous to the proof over
MT\mathrm{Spin}
, discussed in and after
6.6
. For the bordism groups, use the spin
c
ABP decomposition (
6.78
) to identify
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(\mathit{ME}_{2})\cong\mathit{ku}_{k}(\mathit{ME}_{2})\oplus\mathit{ku}_{k-4}(\mathit{ME}_{2})
for
k\leq 7
.

Since
\mathit{ME}_{2}
is trivial at odd primes, it suffices to work
2
-locally, so we can use the Adams spectral sequence over
\mathcal{E}(1)
, given in (
6.68b
). The first step is to compute
H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
as an
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module in low degrees, this time up to isomorphism, not stable equivalence. From
[
182
, (F.11)]
we learn that as
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules,

(6.88a)

H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong F\oplus\Sigma N_{1}\oplus P,

where
P
is concentrated in degrees
7
and above, so we may ignore it, and
F
is a free
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module with a basis of homogeneous elements in degrees
0
,
2
,
4
,
4
,
6
, and
6
. Since
\mathcal{E}(1)
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}(1)
, this determines the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure: because there is an
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module isomorphism
\mathcal{A}(1)\cong\mathcal{E}(1)\oplus\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{E}(1)

[
47
, §4.7.2]
, we learn that as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules,

(6.88b)

H^{*}(\mathit{ME}_{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong F^{c}\oplus\Sigma N_{1}\oplus P.

Here
P
is as in (
6.88a
) and
F^{c}
is a free
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module with a basis of homogeneous elements in degrees
0
,
2
,
2
,
4
,
4
,
4
,
6
,
6
,
6
,
6
,
8
, and
8
. By Margolis’ theorem, the summands in
F^{c}
lift to Whitney summands in
\mathit{ku}_{*}(\mathit{ME}_{2})
, giving the Whitney summands in the proposition statement. The rest of the proof is identical to the Adams computation in
6.71
, but with the degrees shifted up by one.
∎

6.4.
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
bordism

In this subsection, we compute
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
bordism (contingent as usual on the specific degrees of the suspensions in the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting from
6.1
) and the corresponding free-to-interacting map. This is the exceptional case from
2.27
: the fermionic group
Q_{8}
is not isomorphic to
E_{\ell,k}
for any
(\ell,k)
, so the bordism groups we need are not computed in
5.55
. Fortunately, the computation is not any more difficult.

Lemma 6.89

(Mitchell–Priddy
[
241
, §2]
)
.

The central extension
1\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to Q_{8}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to 1
is classified by
x^{2}+xy+y^{2}\in H^{2}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where
x
, resp.
y
are dual to the first, resp. second
\mathbb{Z}/2
summands.

Since the grading
\theta\colon Q_{8}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
is zero,
H(Q_{8})\cong\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}

[
293
, Example 9]
and
6.89
gives us the following result.

Corollary 6.90
.

A
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
structure is equivalent to a
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2,0,x^{2}+xy+y^{2})
-twisted spin structure.

Lemma 6.91
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\sigma_{i}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
be the line bundle that is nontrivial on the
i^{\mathrm{th}}

\mathbb{Z}/2
summand and trivial on the other summand. Let
V\coloneqq\sigma_{1}\oplus\sigma_{2}\oplus(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})
;
then there is an
MT\mathrm{Spin}
-module equivalence

(6.92)

\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}.

Proof.

By the Whitney sum formula,
(w_{1}(V),w_{2}(V))=(0,x^{2}+xy+y^{2})
, matching the twist in
6.90
. Thus
1.104
finishes the proof.
∎

Moreover, interpreting the representation
V
as a homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SO}_{3}
, we see that upon taking spin covers,
\rho
is the map
Q_{8}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SU}_{2}
from the discrete to the continuous class C fermionic group that we discussed in
2.24
.

As usual, we start with the corresponding twisted
\mathit{ko}
-homology.

Lemma 6.93
.

There is a
\mathit{ko}
-module splitting

(6.94)

\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}\simeq\mathit{ksp}\vee\dotsb.

such that the projection
p_{0}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}\to\mathit{ksp}
is
3
-connected.

Proof.

Buchanan–McKean
[
63
]
and Mills
[
239
]
(
6.27
) split
MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\simeq MT\mathrm{Spin}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}
; using the original Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting (
6.1
)
MT\mathrm{Spin}\simeq\mathit{ko}\vee\dotsb
and smashing with
(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}
, Buchanan–McKean–Mills’ splitting induces a splitting of the form (
6.94
) by taking the unique summands of each splitting which are not
1
-connected. Inspecting the remaining summands, the projection onto the
\mathit{ksp}
factor is a
3
-connected map.
∎

Proposition 6.95
.

(1)

There is a
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence

(6.96)

\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}\simeq\mathit{ksp}\vee\bigvee_{i\geq 0}\Sigma^{n_{\ell}}H\mathbb{Z}/2.

If
n=4k+m
,
0\leq m<4
, the number of
\Sigma^{n}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands in (
6.96
) is
0
if
m
is even,
k+2
if
m=1
, and
k+1
if
m=3
.

(2)

With respect to the decomposition (
6.94
), the map

(6.97)

\rho\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}\to\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}

induced by the fermionic group homomorphism
Q_{8}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SU}_{2}
is the identity on the
\mathit{ksp}
summands.

Before we prove this proposition, we make a few Margolis homology calculations that we will need.

Lemma 6.98
.

Let
V^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma_{1}\oplus\sigma_{2}\oplus 3(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})
and
U\in H^{0}((B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5};\mathbb{Z}/2)
denote the mod
2
Thom class. Then
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5};Q_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, generated by
U
, and
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5};Q_{1})\cong\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
, generated by
U(a^{2}+ab+b^{2})
.

Proof.

By the Whitney sum formula,
w_{1}(V^{\prime})=0
and
w_{2}(V^{\prime})=ab
. Thus
Q_{0}(U)=\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(U)=0
, so the Thom isomorphism
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5};\mathbb{Z}/2)
commutes with the
Q_{0}
-action. Thus
H^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5};Q_{0})\cong H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;Q_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
concentrated in degree
0
by the Künneth formula (
5.86
). Giambalvo
[
137
, Proof
of Lemma 3.4]
calculates the
Q_{1}
-homology (in his notation, this is
H(\Phi(Z_{2}[\alpha,\beta]),Q_{1})
), showing that it is one-dimensional and
U(a^{2}+ab+b^{2})
generates. ∎

Corollary 6.99
.

6.98
is true mutatis mutandis with
V-3
in place of
V^{\prime}-5
.

Proof.

If
U
, resp.
U^{\prime}
are the Thom classes for
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}
, resp.
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5}
, respectively, then by the Wu formula, the Thom isomorphisms to the cohomology of
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
identify
Q_{i}(U)
and
Q_{i}(U^{\prime})
for
i=0,1
. Therefore these isomorphisms induce an isomorphism of the
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homologies of
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}
and
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V^{\prime}-5}
.
64
64

64

This strategy generalizes to any pair of twists of
\mathit{ko}
-homology over a space
X
which induce equivalent twists of
\mathit{ku}
, because the
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-homology only depend on the underlying
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure of an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module.

∎

Proof of
6.95
.

Localized away from
2
, this is trivial (as
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq\mathrm{pt}
at odd primes), so in the rest of the proof, we localize at
2
. We will show that the map
\rho
in (
6.97
) induces an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology, once we restrict to the
\mathit{ksp}
summand of
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}
; since
\mathit{ksp}
has no free summands, this and the stable Whitehead theorem imply that
\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3}
is
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalent to a sum of
\mathit{ksp}
and some
\Sigma^{k}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands. The specific degrees of the
\Sigma^{k}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands can be computed using a Poincaré polynomial computation similar to those in
[
118
, §§D.3–D.6]
.

After applying
5.16
and the Thom isomorphism, the effect of
\rho
on
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
is
the same as
(B\rho^{*})\colon H^{*}(B\mathrm{SO}_{3};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(BV;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Recall from
6.93
that there is a
3
-connected map
p_{0}\colon\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}\to\mathit{ksp}
which is the projection onto a direct
\mathit{ko}
-module summand. Let
\widetilde{\rho}\coloneqq p_{0}\circ\rho
; we will show

\widetilde{\rho}
is an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology.

Baker
[
38
, §5]
shows
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})\cong\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}
, where
\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(1)/(\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{3})
. Adams–Priddy
[
6
, §3]
showed that
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp};Q_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp};Q_{1})\cong\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
. Since

p_{0}
is
3
-connected, the generators pull back to the unique nonzero classes of
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3})\cong H^{*}((B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3};\mathbb{Z}/2)
(
5.16
) in
degrees
0
and
2
, which are
U
and
Uw_{2}
, respectively. Then
\rho
pulls these back to
U
, resp.

(6.100)

U(w_{2}(\sigma_{1}\oplus\sigma_{2}\oplus(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})))=U(a^{2}+ab+b^{2}),

so by
6.99
,
\widetilde{\rho}
induces an isomorphism on
Q_{0}
- and
Q_{1}
-Margolis homology as promised, and the proof is
complete.
∎

Lemma 6.101
.

There is a
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp}\simeq\Sigma^{2}H\mathbb{Z}/2\vee\Sigma^{4}\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
.

Proof.

Baker
[
38
, §5]
shows
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko})\simeq\Sigma^{2}J
,
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\mathit{ksp})\cong\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}
, and
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathit{ko}})\cong R_{2}
, where
J
,

Q

, and
R_{2}
are the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules we defined in
5.93
,
6.17
, and
6.30
. Mills
[
239
, Lemma 3.4(2)]
proves
J\otimes\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\cong\mathcal{A}(1)\oplus\Sigma^{2}R_{2}
; then the
2
-localization of the lemma statement follows from Stolz’ splitting theorem
[
299
, Theorem 4.1]
.

At odd primes, the lemma is less exciting: there are
\mathit{ko}[1/2]
-module equivalences
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}[1/2]\simeq\Sigma^{4}\mathit{ko}[1/2]
,
\mathit{ksp}[1/2]\simeq\mathit{ko}[1/2]
, and
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}[1/2]\simeq\mathit{ko}[1/2]
, which can be proven from the definitions of these
\mathit{ko}
-modules and the fact that at localized at an odd prime
p
, the generator of
\pi_{4}\mathit{ko}
is a unit in the ring
\pi_{*}\mathit{ko}_{(p)}
. After these identifications the result is straightforward.
∎

We use the following theorem, which splits the spectrum
\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})
and computes its homotopy groups, in the proof of
6.53
, calculating the maps
\mathrm{sp}^{\psi}_{\ell,0}
on long summands. This is not circular: our proof of
6.102
does not use the maps
\mathrm{sp}_{\ell,k}^{\psi}
at all, nor anything that depends on them.

Theorem 6.102
.

There are numbers
u_{i}
,
v_{j}
, and
w_{k}
and a homology
\mathit{ko}
-module equivalence

(6.103)

\mathit{MTH}(Q_{8})\simeq\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\bigvee_{i\geq 1}\Sigma^{u_{i}}\mathit{ksp}\vee\bigvee_{j\geq 1}\Sigma^{v_{j}}\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}\vee\bigvee_{k\geq 1}\Sigma^{w_{k}}H\mathbb{Z}/2.

Explicitly, on homotopy groups, recall the numbers
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}a_{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}b_{j}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}c_{k}}
from
6.1
, and the numbers
n_{\ell}
from
6.95
.

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}
is the direct sum of the following abelian groups.

(1)

For each
i\geq 1
:

(a)

a copy of
\pi_{*+{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}a_{i}}}(\mathit{ksp})
, and

(b)

for each
\ell\geq 1
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{1,0.18,1}a_{i}}+n_{\ell}
.

(2)

For each
j\geq 1
:

(a)

a copy of
\pi_{*+{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}b_{j}}+4}(\widetilde{\mathit{ko}})
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}b_{j}}+2
, and

(b)

for each
\ell\geq 1
and
m\in\{2,\dotsc,6\}
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}b_{j}}+n_{\ell}+m
.

(3)

For each
k\geq 1
:

(a)

for each
m\in\{0,2,3\}
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}c_{k}}+m
, and

(b)

for each
\ell\geq 1
and
m\in\{0,1,2,4,5,6\}
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}c_{k}}+n_{\ell}+m
, and a
\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
in degree
{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}c_{k}}+n_{\ell}+3
.

In degrees
4
and below, these bordism groups were computed by Pedrotti
[
255
, Theorem 8.0.8]
; the remaining computations are new. From the definitions of
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}
and
\mathit{ksp}
in
6.27
, we see that
\pi_{*}(\widetilde{\mathit{ko}})\cong\pi_{*}(\mathit{ko})
(though there is no map of
\mathit{ko}
-modules implementing this isomorphism), and
\pi_{n}(\mathit{ksp})\cong\pi_{n+4}(\mathit{ko})
as long as
n\geq 0
. In negative degrees
\pi_{*}(\mathit{ksp})
vanishes.

Proof.

Plug (
6.96
) into the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting (
6.4
), then compute
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp}
,
\tau_{\geq 2}\mathit{ko}\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}H\mathbb{Z}/2
,
H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}\mathit{ksp}
, and
H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}H\mathbb{Z}/2
. The first of these is
6.101
; for the rest, use the identification
\pi_{*}(H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge_{\mathit{ko}}M)\cong H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}(M)
for any
\mathit{ko}
-module
M
, which can be proven using the Künneth formula for
H_{\mathit{ko}}^{*}
and Margolis’ theorem (
5.25
).
∎

Explicitly, we get the following bordism groups in low degrees.

(6.104)

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{7}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{8}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong 0\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{9}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 8}

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{10}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{11}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 8}

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{12}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

Remark 6.105
.

Teichner
[
312
, §1]
showed that the signature
\sigma\colon\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}}\to\mathbb{Z}
has image
4\mathbb{Z}
; Pedrotti
[
255
, Theorem 8.0.8]
showed
\tfrac{1}{4}\sigma
is an isomorphism onto
\mathbb{Z}
. One can choose the generator to be the quotient
H
of a smooth Enriques surface by a free antiholomorphic involution, as Teichner
[
312
, §1]
showed this is a closed
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}

4
-manifold with signature
4
. Hitchin
[
163
, §4]
showed that Enriques surfaces with these involutions exist, and gave an example.

By
6.95
, part (
2
),
H
also generates a
\mathbb{Z}
summand of
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{h}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, though Hu
[
168
, §A]
has already shown this summand is generated by
\mathbb{HP}^{1}
.
6.95
, together with Buchanan–McKean’s work
[
63
]
on spin
h
bordism, can also be used to show that
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}
bordism is detected by Buchanan–McKean’s
\mathit{KSp}
-characteristic classes
[
63
, §5.5]
, Anderson–Brown–Peterson’s
\mathit{KO}
-Pontrjagin classes
[
16
, §4]
, and characteristic classes in mod
2
cohomology (“Whitney classes” in the language of Conner–Floyd
[
73
, §17]
; see
5.9
).
∎

Recall from
3.97
that we defined a free-to-interacting map
F2I_{s=4}^{\delta}\colon\Sigma^{2}\mathit{KO}\to\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}))
, essentially by composing the usual spin
h
free-to-interacting map with
\rho\colon Q_{8}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SU}_{2}
.

Proposition 6.106
.

I_{\mathbb{Z}}\rho\colon I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}\to I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8})
restricts to an isomorphism from the image of the continuous class C free-to-interacting map to the image of the discrete class C free-to-interacting map.

Of course, the cokernels are quite different. For example, the above proposition implies the entirety of
\mho_{\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}Q_{8}}}^{1}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
is in the cokernel of the discrete F2I map, whereas by
[
118
, Corollary 9.99]
, the cokernel of the continuous F2I map vanishes in degrees
2
and below.

Proof.

By
3.99
, we may replace
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{MT}(\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}Q_{8}))
with
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V-3})
and
I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
with
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3})
.
After this, the proposition follows from a combination of the fact that the continuous free-to-interacting map has image contained in the
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ksp}
summand of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}MT\mathrm{Spin}^{h}
, which follows from
6.27
, and the fact that
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\rho
preserves this
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ksp}
summand and kills all other summands of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{ko}\wedge(B\mathrm{SO}_{3})^{V-3}
, which is
6.95
, part (
2
).
∎

Freed–Hopkins
[
118
, Corollary 9.99]
calculate the image of the continuous free-to-interacting map:
\mathbb{Z}
in degrees
d\equiv 3\bmod 4
,
d\geq-1
, and
\mathbb{Z}/2
in degrees
d\equiv 5,6\bmod 8
,
d\geq 0
.
